# Casebook: organizations, one by one

Generated by `scripts/casebook.py` from `companies/<group>/<slug>.json` (schema: [`SCHEMA.md`](SCHEMA.md); openness 0–4). Each entry's assessment and takeaway were written per organization by the research agents from the sources listed under it; numbers without a source are not used. `search_tool` says whether WebSearch or only WebFetch of known pages was available.

## A. Commercial open source: data infrastructure and the relicensing cases (18)

### Cloudera, Inc. (including Hortonworks)

founded 2008 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 2→2 · private_pe · $5.30B (acquisition_price, 2021-10) · funding $970.0M · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 18 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves · latest value ÷ funding raised ≈ 5.5×

**What openness did.** Both an open-core firm (Cloudera) and a more fully open one (Hortonworks) reached public markets (2017 and 2014), but neither sustained independent growth: they merged in 2019, gated binaries behind subscriptions, and sold to private equity for about $5.3B in 2021, not far above the roughly $4.1B implied by Intel's 2014 investment. Openness built the ecosystem but did not secure either company's independence.

**Takeaway.** Cloudera and Hortonworks are the Hadoop era's natural experiment in degrees of openness. Both IPO'd, neither thrived alone, they merged, the combined firm closed distribution rather than the licence, and private equity took it private in 2021. The Apache projects outlived both companies' independence.

**Timeline.** 2019: Freely downloadable binaries (CDH, HDP) → Subscription-only binaries (fully from 2021-01-31) (closing); 2014: IPO, Hortonworks (Nasdaq); 2017: IPO, NYSE: CLDR; 2019: merger, Hortonworks; 2021: take_private $5.30B, KKR and Clayton, Dubilier & Rice

**Risk events.** 2019-06: CEO Tom Reilly and co-founder Mike Olson left after the merger; 2019-2021: Binaries moved behind a subscription paywall; 2023-10: $240M patent jury verdict for StreamScale; 2026-10: Settlement with the US government over PERM (green card) irregularities, per Wikipedia citing Newsweek (unverified)

**Robustness.** The Hadoop-ecosystem projects are ASF-governed, so the technology does not depend on Cloudera. The company is PE-owned since 2021 and buys AI tooling (Verta, June 2024).

### Cockroach Labs, Inc.

founded 2015 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→1 · private_vc · $5.00B (private_valuation, 2021-12-16) · funding $633.0M · confidence: medium · tool: WebSearch (standard/extended)

*Computed:* age 11 yrs · 2 closing / 0 opening moves · latest value ÷ funding raised ≈ 7.9×

**What openness did.** Apache licensing and the Spanner pedigree drew early developer interest and large venture rounds (to a $5B valuation in 2021). The company then closed in two steps, BSL in 2019 and a single proprietary licence in 2024, without provoking a fork, likely because its user base was smaller and hyperscalers already had their own distributed SQL services. Closing is aimed at monetizing large self-hosted users; there is no public evidence yet whether it raised revenue.

**Takeaway.** Cockroach Labs shows that a database company can move from Apache to nearly proprietary with little community backlash when the project lacks a large independent contributor base or a hyperscaler with motive to fork. The cost is that it now competes as a proprietary vendor against Apache-licensed rivals (YugabyteDB, TiDB) and Postgres itself, with no public exit and a valuation last set at the 2021 peak.

**Timeline.** 2019: Apache-2.0 → BSL 1.1 (closing); 2024: BSL 1.1 (Core) + CCL (Enterprise) → CockroachDB Software License (single proprietary enterprise licence) (closing)

**Risk events.** 2019: BSL relicense; 2023-10: Layoff of ~75 staff (TrueUp tracker); 2024-11-18: Free Core edition retired; single proprietary licence; 2026-09-02: Reported reduction of ~50 staff (~6.8%), listed as an unconfirmed rumor on Blind's tracker; No IPO or new priced round since the 2021 $5B valuation (no S-1 as of mid-2026)

**Robustness.** Single-vendor, VC-backed, private since 2015; no foundation and no fork, so customers depend on Cockroach Labs. Funding of $633M gives runway, but the $5B valuation is from the 2021 peak and has not been re-priced publicly. PostgreSQL wire compatibility gives customers some portability.

### Confluent, Inc. (an IBM company since 2026)

founded 2014 · US · origin: corporate · openness 3→2 · acquired · $11.00B (acquisition_price, 2026-03-17) · funding $455.9M · revenue $1.17B (2025) · confidence: high · tool: WebSearch (standard/extended)

*Computed:* age 12 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves · latest value ÷ funding raised ≈ 24.1×

**What openness did.** Building on a foundation-governed Apache project gave Confluent a huge user base it did not need to defend with licence changes on the core; it restricted only its own add-ons. That narrower closing avoided a fork war but also meant hyperscalers could sell managed Kafka freely, and Confluent never reached GAAP profitability. Its value ended about where its IPO valuation started (~$11-12B).

**Takeaway.** Confluent shows a middle path: keep the engine at a neutral foundation, monetize a cloud service and proprietary or source-available extras. It avoided the Elastic/Redis-style fork, grew to $1.17B in revenue, and was bought by IBM for ~$11B in March 2026. Together with HashiCorp, it shows IBM consolidating open-source-origin infrastructure companies, after Red Hat, as a strategy for enterprise AI.

**Timeline.** 2018: Apache-2.0 (Confluent components) → Confluent Community License (closing); 2021: IPO $12.00B, Nasdaq: CFLT; 2026: acquisition $11.00B, IBM

**Risk events.** 2018-12: Confluent Community License for several platform components; 2021-2025: Shares fell from the $36 IPO price to $23.14 before IBM's bid; GAAP operating loss still $380M in 2025; 2026-03-17: Independent company ended; acquired by IBM

**Robustness.** The core technology (Kafka) is foundation-governed at the ASF and offered by many vendors and clouds, so users are not locked to Confluent. The company itself is now an IBM unit. At acquisition it had $1.17B revenue (+21%), Confluent Cloud at 56% of subscription revenue, 114% net retention and 6,500+ enterprise customers.

**Physical-science relevance.** Kafka is used for high-rate instrument and sensor streaming (e.g. telescope and accelerator data pipelines); Confluent sells the managed version.

### Couchbase, Inc.

founded 2011 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 2→1 · private_pe · $1.50B (acquisition_price, 2025-09-24) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 15 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** An Apache-licensed open core carried Couchbase to a 2021 Nasdaq IPO at $24 a share, but it switched to BSL just before listing and never re-rated: private equity bought it in 2025 for $24.50 a share (~$1.5B), almost exactly the IPO price. The closing triggered no fork, probably because no large third party was reselling it, but it also bought no visible valuation gain.

**Takeaway.** Couchbase is the private-equity version of the consolidation that IBM drove with HashiCorp and Confluent: a smaller open-core database that moved to BSL in 2021, stayed flat in public markets, and was taken private by Haveli Investments for about $1.5B in September 2025, at roughly its IPO price.

**Timeline.** 2021: Apache-2.0 → BSL 1.1 (closing); 2021: IPO, Nasdaq: BASE; 2025: take_private $1.50B, Haveli Investments

**Risk events.** 2016-03: $30M down round (WSJ, cited by Wikipedia); 2021-03: Adopted BSL 1.1; 2025-03-27: Share price low enough that the later $24.50 offer was a 67% premium; 2025-09-24: Delisted after the Haveli take-private

**Robustness.** Single vendor with no foundation and no known fork; owned by private equity (Haveli) since September 2025. Apache CouchDB, an ASF project, shares only history with it.

### Docker, Inc. (formerly dotCloud)

founded 2008 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→2 · private_vc · funding $341.8M · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 18 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Open-sourcing Docker made the company a unicorn by April 2015, but it could not monetize the runtime and sold its enterprise unit in 2019. It recovered by charging larger companies for the proprietary Desktop app from 2021, which coincided with more than 4x ARR growth: the money came from closing developer tooling, not the open core.

**Takeaway.** Docker is the case of an open project that won its market so completely that the company struggled to capture the value: Kubernetes and the clouds monetized containers, Docker sold its enterprise unit in 2019, and then rebuilt around paid Desktop subscriptions for large companies. Value capture came from a proprietary client, not from relicensing the engine.

**Timeline.** 2021: Docker Desktop free for all users → Paid subscription for larger businesses (closing); 2019: acquisition, Mirantis

**Risk events.** 2019-11: Enterprise business sold to Mirantis; restructuring and $35M recapitalisation; 2021-08-31: Docker Desktop subscription requirement for larger businesses; 2025-02: CEO change (Don Johnson succeeded Scott Johnston)

**Robustness.** The runtime pieces live in Moby (Apache-2.0) and CNCF projects (containerd, runc), so containers do not depend on Docker, Inc. The company depends on proprietary Desktop and Hub subscriptions: 56,000 paying customers and 16M monthly active users in March 2022. It acquired MCP Defender (AI-agent security) in September 2025 (Wikipedia citing Docker blog).

### Elastic N.V. (formerly Elasticsearch BV)

founded 2012 · NL · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→2 · public · $10.20B (market_cap, 2026-10-30) · funding $104.0M · revenue $1.74B (2026) · confidence: high · tool: WebSearch (standard/extended)

*Computed:* age 14 yrs · 1 closing / 1 opening moves · latest value ÷ funding raised ≈ 98.1×

**What openness did.** Apache licensing made Elasticsearch the default search engine and supported a $4.9B first-day IPO valuation in 2018. Closing to SSPL/ELv2 in 2021 did not hurt revenue growth much (revenue reached $1.74B in FY2026), but it triggered the most successful hyperscaler fork in this group: OpenSearch, now at the Linux Foundation with over a billion downloads. Elastic's 2024 AGPL return shows the company judged the fork and the lost open-source label to be costlier than the protection SSPL gave.

**Takeaway.** Elastic is the canonical relicense-fork-reversal story. Closing the licence protected Elastic Cloud from AWS resale, but it handed AWS the moral high ground and a permanent, foundation-governed fork that splits the ecosystem. Three years later Elastic re-added an OSI licence (AGPL), keeping SSPL/ELv2 alongside. The company stayed public and growing throughout, so the closing was not fatal, but it permanently lost sole control of the search-engine standard it created.

**Timeline.** 2021: Apache-2.0 → SSPL-1.0 / ELv2 (closing); fork: OpenSearch (and OpenSearch Dashboards), forked from Elasticsearch/Kibana 7.10.2, first released April 2021; 2024: SSPL-1.0 / ELv2 → AGPL-3.0 added as an option (opening); 2018: IPO $4.90B, NYSE: ESTC

**Risk events.** 2021-01: Relicense to SSPL/ELv2; AWS forked Elasticsearch and Kibana as Apache-2.0 OpenSearch; 2024-09-16: OpenSearch moved to a neutral Linux Foundation foundation with AWS, SAP and Uber as premier members; 2024-08-29: Partial reversal to AGPL, met with community scepticism about long-term commitment; Market cap volatile: roughly $6.7B-$9.0B in July-August 2026 snapshots vs ~$10.2B on 2026-10-30

**Robustness.** Single-vendor control of Elasticsearch; no foundation. A well-funded, foundation-governed fork (OpenSearch) now competes head-on, so customers have an exit path. Financially solid: FY2026 revenue $1.739B (+17%), non-GAAP operating margin 16.4%, though GAAP operating loss of $33M; Q2 FY2027 revenue $496M (+17%).

**Physical-science relevance.** Elasticsearch/OpenSearch are common backbones for log, telemetry and literature search in research infrastructure.

### Grafana Labs (formerly Raintank)

founded 2014 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→2 · private_vc · $6.00B (private_valuation, 2024-08-21) · funding $534.0M · revenue $250.0M (2024) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 12 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves · latest value ÷ funding raised ≈ 11.2×

**What openness did.** Grafana tightened from Apache-2.0 to AGPL in 2021 instead of adopting SSPL or BSL, keeping the open-source label. Growth continued: the valuation went from $3B (Series C, August 2021) to over $6B (August 2024), ARR passed $250M and paying customers rose from 2,000 (2022) to over 5,000 (2024). Copyleft appears to have deterred hosted free-riding without the trust costs Elastic and Redis paid.

**Takeaway.** Grafana Labs is the main counter-example in this group: it chose an OSI copyleft licence over a source-available one, avoided any fork, and kept compounding its private valuation (over $6B in 2024). Elastic (2024) and Redis (2025) later re-added AGPL themselves, which makes Grafana's 2021 choice look like where the relicensing wave settled.

**Timeline.** 2021: Apache-2.0 → AGPL-3.0 (closing)

**Robustness.** VC-backed single vendor, but the core projects stay OSI-licensed (AGPL), so a fork remains legally possible. It acquires and folds in projects (k6 2021, Pyroscope 2023) and in 2025 donated Beyla to OpenTelemetry.

### HashiCorp, Inc. (an IBM company since 2025)

founded 2012 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 2→1 · acquired · $6.40B (acquisition_price, 2025-02-27) · funding $349.8M · revenue $583.1M (2024) · confidence: high · tool: WebSearch (standard/extended)

*Computed:* age 14 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves · latest value ÷ funding raised ≈ 18.3×

**What openness did.** MPL-licensed tools made Terraform the de facto infrastructure-as-code standard and supported a ~$14B IPO in 2021. The 2023 BSL switch was followed by forks that now have foundation homes and meaningful downloads, but there is little evidence it dented HashiCorp's revenue. Closing did not protect the company's valuation: it sold to IBM at $6.4B, less than half its IPO value.

**Takeaway.** HashiCorp is the case where relicensing came late in the company's life and preceded a sale. The BSL change likely made HashiCorp a cleaner asset for an acquirer, and IBM bought it within about 18 months, even though IBM engineers had led the open Vault fork. The open-source community kept its own copy of Terraform (OpenTofu, now in CNCF), so the open standard survives outside the vendor.

**Timeline.** 2023: MPL-2.0 → BSL 1.1 (closing); fork: OpenTofu (Terraform fork from 1.5.7, accepted by the Linux Foundation 2023-09-20; CNCF Sandbox 2025-04-23) and OpenBao (Vault fork, started 2023-11-09 under LF Edge); 2021: IPO $14.00B, Nasdaq: HCP; 2025: acquisition $6.40B, IBM

**Risk events.** 2023-08: Switch to BSL 1.1; OpenTofu and OpenBao forks under the Linux Foundation; 2021-2024: Shares fell from the $80 IPO price; IBM paid $35/share; 2025-02-27: Independent company ended; became an IBM Software division; 2026-03-31: Legacy HCP Terraform Free plan ended (replaced by a 500-resource free tier)

**Robustness.** Now inside IBM, so financially secure, but product direction is set by one corporate owner and licences remain BSL. The community has two foundation-governed exits (OpenTofu in CNCF; OpenBao in LF Edge). Terraform development continues (1.17.x, October 2026) and is being folded into IBM's security and automation stack.

**Physical-science relevance.** Terraform/OpenTofu are widely used to provision cloud and HPC research infrastructure reproducibly.

### InfluxData, Inc. (formerly Errplane)

founded 2012 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→2 · private_vc · funding $170.1M · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 14 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** InfluxData never moved its core off OSI licences; it monetized by withholding features (clustering in 2016, scale limits in InfluxDB 3 Core). That avoided a licence fight but drew complaints, and its funding grew modestly, from an $8.1M Series A (2014) to a $51M equity Series E (2023), with no disclosed valuation.

**Takeaway.** InfluxData is 'closing by feature' rather than by licence: the code stays MIT/Apache, but the features enterprises need are paid. It avoided forks, but its modest funding and undisclosed valuation point to a smaller outcome than peers that relicensed or went public.

**Timeline.** 2016: MIT (clustering in open build) → Proprietary (clustering only in Enterprise) (closing)

**Risk events.** 2016: Clustering removed from open-source InfluxDB, mixed community reaction; 2025: InfluxDB 3 Core shipped with limits that steer larger users to Enterprise

**Robustness.** Single vendor, with a CLA that permits closed-source relicensing of contributions; the core (InfluxDB 3 Core, Telegraf) remains MIT/Apache and forkable. The company cites 2,600+ enterprise customers and 1.3M+ active InfluxDB instances.

### Lightbend, Inc. (formerly Typesafe; d/b/a Akka)

founded 2011 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→1 · private_vc · funding $42.0M · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 15 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Lightbend relicensed because Apache 2.0 becomes 'increasingly risky when a small company solely carries the maintenance effort'. The result was a foundation-governed fork that major downstream projects (Flink, Play) moved to, shrinking Akka's open-source footprint. With $42M raised and no disclosed valuation, it is the smallest relicensing case in this group.

**Takeaway.** Akka is the small-scale version of the HashiCorp story: a 2022 BSL switch quickly produced an ASF-governed fork (Apache Pekko, top-level by May 2024) that key downstream projects adopted. Even a mid-sized ecosystem now forks into a foundation when its licence closes.

**Timeline.** 2022: Apache-2.0 → BSL 1.1 (closing); fork: Apache Pekko (fork of Akka 2.6.x)

**Risk events.** 2022-09: Akka relicensed to BSL 1.1; 2022-2024: Apache Pekko fork, adopted by Flink and Play, graduated at the ASF

**Robustness.** Akka is single-vendor under BSL, but the Apache-licensed lineage continues as Apache Pekko under ASF governance, and each Akka release reverts to Apache-2.0 after three years. Small company: $42M raised in total.

### MariaDB (MariaDB plc / MariaDB Corporation and the MariaDB Foundation)

founded 2009 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→2 · private_pe · $37.0M (acquisition_price, 2024-09-10) · funding $123.0M · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 17 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves · latest value ÷ funding raised ≈ 0.3×

**What openness did.** MariaDB shows openness protecting the code but not the company. The GPL and the foundation let the community route around Oracle's control of MySQL, and MariaDB Server remains widely used. The commercial vehicle raised about $123M, listed by SPAC at the 2022 peak and sold for about $37M two years later.

**Takeaway.** MariaDB exists because MySQL changed hands: it is a founder-led fork to keep the code under community control, backed by a nonprofit foundation. Its company struggled (SPAC listing, then a roughly $37M private-equity take-private) while the GPL server persists, separating the robustness of the code from that of the company. It also wrote the Business Source License used across the later relicensing wave.

**Timeline.** None: open source (prior MaxScale licence not confirmed) → Business Source License (MaxScale 2.x) (closing); 2022: merger, Angel Pond Holdings Corp. (SPAC); NYSE: MRDB; 2024: take_private $37.0M, K1 Investment Management

**Risk events.** 2022-12: SPAC listing, followed by a sharp share-price decline; 2023-10: SkySQL cloud service discontinued and staff laid off; 2024-09: Taken private by K1 for about $37M

**Robustness.** The code is robust independent of the company: GPLv2 server and a nonprofit foundation, which in 2025 signed an MoU recognising MariaDB plc as primary code contributor for 2022-2024. The company has been fragile (SPAC, collapse, ~$37M take-private) and under K1 is rebuilding by acquisition: Codership/Galera (May 2025), SkySQL back (announced 2025-08-26) and GridGain (March 2026, per Wikipedia).

**What openness did.** AGPL openness drove early developer adoption that the company converted into one of the largest open-source-origin IPOs of 2017. Closing to SSPL in 2018 did not stall growth: revenue rose to $2.46B in FY2026 and the market cap sits around $23B, because Atlas (its own hosting) became the main revenue line. The costs were reputational and ecosystem-level: distributions dropped it, and hyperscalers responded with compatible substitutes that in 2025 moved into a neutral Linux Foundation project.

**Takeaway.** MongoDB is the clearest case of an open-source-origin company that closed its license and still kept growing: SSPL protected its managed-service business from cloud resale, and the market rewarded it with roughly a 20x rise from its IPO valuation. But closing the license did not remove competition, it moved it to the API layer, where the hyperscalers now back an MIT-licensed, foundation-governed alternative. For an openness study, MongoDB shows that a strong first-party cloud product, not the license, is what captures value, while openness mainly bought the early adoption.

**Timeline.** 2018: AGPL-3.0 → SSPL v1 (closing); 2017: IPO $1.20B, NASDAQ: MDB

**Risk events.** 2018-10: SSPL relicense; Debian, Fedora and RHEL 8 dropped MongoDB; SSPL never became OSI-approved; 2019 onward: hyperscaler API-compatible substitutes (AWS DocumentDB, later Microsoft's open DocumentDB at the Linux Foundation, Aug 2025); 2025-05: MongoDB began patent litigation against FerretDB, an open MongoDB-compatible project (reported by InfoQ, Nov 2026); 2025-11: Founder-era CEO Dev Ittycheria retired; Cloudflare's CJ Desai became CEO; 2026-09-28: Desai stepped down effective immediately to join Meta; Ittycheria returned as interim CEO while a search runs

**Robustness.** Single-vendor control, no foundation. Revenue is now mostly Atlas/subscription ($2.39B of $2.46B in FY2026) and the company is cash-generative ($505M operating cash flow FY2026), but leadership has been unstable in 2025-2026. The wire protocol is now reimplemented by open, foundation-hosted alternatives (DocumentDB at the Linux Foundation), which reduces customer lock-in and MongoDB's moat.

**Physical-science relevance.** Widely used as a schemaless store for lab and sensor data; this lab's own digital-wetlab uses MongoDB Atlas.

### MySQL AB and Sun Microsystems (MySQL's path to Oracle)

founded 1995 · SE · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→2 · acquired · $1.00B (acquisition_price, 2008-02-26) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 13 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** GPL dual licensing made MySQL a default database while still letting MySQL AB charge embedders, and supported a roughly $1B sale to Sun in 2008. The risk event was a change of owner, not of licence: Oracle, a direct competitor, inherited MySQL with Sun, prompting an EU in-depth review and the MariaDB fork.

**Takeaway.** MySQL is the founding case for this group: a dual-licensed open-source company that sold for about $1B in 2008, then passed to Oracle with Sun in 2010. Ownership change, not relicensing, drove the community fork (MariaDB), whose company later wrote the BSL used in the relicensing wave.

**Timeline.** 2008: acquisition $1.00B, Sun Microsystems; 2010: acquisition $7.40B, Oracle Corporation (via its purchase of Sun)

**Risk events.** 2009-2010: EU in-depth review of Oracle-Sun centred on Oracle controlling MySQL; Widenius's 'Save MySQL!' petition; Forks: MariaDB (2009, active), Drizzle (discontinued), Percona Server, WebScaleSQL (2014, Facebook/Google/LinkedIn/Twitter, abandoned)

**Robustness.** The GPL code was forkable and was forked repeatedly, but MySQL's trademark and main development stay with Oracle, a single vendor with no foundation. Oracle still ships it: MySQL 9.7 LTS (April 2026) and calendar versioning from July 2026.

founded 2007 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→2 · private_vc · $2.20B (private_valuation, 2024-11-19) · funding $550.0M · revenue $200.0M (2024) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

**What openness did.** GPL/AGPL dual licensing built a large graph-database community, and closing Enterprise in 2018 did not stop growth: revenue doubled in three years to over $200M by 2024 and the valuation reached $2.2B. Unlike the Elastic and Redis forks, the unauthorized redistribution of Enterprise was stopped in court, showing that licence enforcement can replace market competition when no hyperscaler backs a fork.

**Takeaway.** Neo4j fought a fork of its relicensed code in court rather than in the market, and won, the opposite of OpenSearch or Valkey, where hyperscaler backers made forks durable. Closing Enterprise did not dent growth: about $550M raised, a $2.2B valuation and over $200M in revenue by 2024, with IPO preparation reported.

**Robustness.** Single vendor; Community Edition (GPLv3) is forkable, but Neo4j has shown it will enforce licence terms and trademarks in court. 1,700 customers and 84% of the Fortune 100 (2024); expected positive cash flow (TechCrunch, Nov 2024).

### Redis Inc. (formerly Garantia Data, then Redis Labs)

founded 2011 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 2→2 · private_vc · $2.00B (private_valuation, 2021-04) · funding $347.0M · revenue $300.0M (2025) · confidence: medium · tool: WebSearch (standard/extended)

*Computed:* age 15 yrs · 2 closing / 1 opening moves · latest value ÷ funding raised ≈ 5.8×

**What openness did.** The BSD licence made Redis ubiquitous, which is why a company that did not create it could build a business around it. Closing the core in 2024 produced the fastest fork in this group (Valkey, within 8 days) with AWS, Google and Oracle behind it, and AWS has since steered its large ElastiCache base to Valkey. Redis's AGPL return in 2025 restored an OSI licence but not the lost cloud channel.

**Takeaway.** Redis shows the limits of relicensing when the hyperscalers are your biggest distribution channel: the moment the core closed, they forked it into a neutral foundation and made the fork their default. Redis Inc. still grew past $300M ARR, but it has not IPO'd and secondary markets value it below its 2021 $2B round. The reversal to AGPL after antirez returned suggests the company concluded that the open-source label mattered more for developer adoption than the licence protection did.

**Timeline.** 2018: AGPL (modules) → Apache-2.0 + Commons Clause, then RSAL (2019) (closing); 2024: BSD-3-Clause (core) → RSALv2 / SSPLv1 (closing); fork: Valkey (from Redis 7.2.4, BSD-3-Clause), announced 2024-03-28 under the Linux Foundation; 2025: RSALv2 / SSPLv1 → AGPLv3 added as an option (opening)

**Risk events.** 2018-2019: Module relicensing (Commons Clause, then RSAL) drew criticism; 2024-03: Core relicensed to RSALv2/SSPLv1; Linux Foundation fork Valkey launched within 8 days, backed by AWS, Google and Oracle; 2026: AWS positions Valkey as the recommended ElastiCache engine (Valkey 9.0 May 2026, 9.1 June 2026); 2025-05: Partial reversal to AGPLv3 with Redis 8; No IPO as of Jul 2026 per trackers; secondary-market estimates (~$1.3B, QuantLogix) sit below the 2021 $2B round

**Robustness.** Single-vendor control of the Redis trademark and code; the BSD-licensed lineage now lives on in Valkey under neutral Linux Foundation governance with hyperscaler backing, so users have an exit. The company reported passing $300M ARR in January 2026 with 50+ customers above $1M a year, and is buying into AI/streaming (Decodable, announced 2025-09-04). Private, last priced in 2021.

**Physical-science relevance.** Commonly used as a cache, queue and pub/sub broker in lab automation and data pipelines.

### Sentry (Functional Software, Inc.)

US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→1 · private_vc · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* 1 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** Sentry closed pre-emptively, before any cloud provider resold it, then shortened the closed window with FSL. It reports over 4 million developers and no fork appeared, which suggests a SaaS-first product with a delayed-open licence loses little adoption. Financial effects could not be assessed because funding, valuation and revenue were not confirmed.

**Takeaway.** Sentry is the architect of the 'delayed open source' middle ground: source-available now, Apache/MIT after two years, paired with direct payments to open-source maintainers ($1M in 2026). It is a third path between OSI licensing and permanent source-available licences, built for a company whose product is mainly consumed as a service.

**Timeline.** 2019: BSD-3-Clause → BSL (closing); 2023: BSL (3-year) → FSL (2-year) (opening)

**Risk events.** 2019-11: Relicensed from BSD-3-Clause to BSL; 2023-11: Moved to its own FSL

**Robustness.** Single vendor, but every release becomes Apache-2.0/MIT after two years, so an open fork path is guaranteed by the licence. Sentry also pays upstream maintainers: $1.0M across 132 developers in its 2026 Open Source Pledge report, up from $155,000 in 2021 (self-reported). Over 4 million developers use it (company site).

### Airbyte, Inc.

founded 2020 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→1 · private_vc · $1.50B (private_valuation, 2021-12) · funding $181.4M · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 6 yrs · 2 closing / 0 opening moves · latest value ÷ funding raised ≈ 8.3×

**What openness did.** MIT licensing and a community connector catalog drove extremely fast adoption (zero to unicorn in under two years). Airbyte closed its core under ELv2 within ~18 months of launch, before rather than after scaling, which avoided a fork but turned it into a source-available vendor; it has since raised no disclosed new money, so the long-term value of the closing move is unproven.

**Takeaway.** Airbyte is the fast-closing case in this group: born MIT in 2020, ELv2 by 2021, a $1.5B valuation the same year, and then a long gap without new priced funding while bigger rivals merged. Openness bought rapid adoption; restriction came early to fend off cloud resellers, and the company's fate now depends on monetizing the cloud product.

**Timeline.** 2021: MIT → Elastic License v2 (core platform) (closing); 2023: MIT (some connectors) → Elastic License v2 (closing)

**Risk events.** 2021 and 2023 moves from MIT to ELv2 (source-available) for the core and some connectors; No new priced round found since the Dec 2021 $1.5B Series B (as of Apr 2025), a valuation set at the 2021 market peak; Larger competitors consolidating: Fivetran (valued $5.6B in 2021) and dbt Labs ($4.2B in 2022) agreed to merge in 2025; Executive turnover in finance/sales (2024-2025)

**Robustness.** Large open community (20-25K Slack members; 170K+ deployments and 7K+ companies syncing daily as of Sep 2024; 22.4k GitHub stars) and MIT-licensed connectors that others can reuse, but the core platform is ELv2 and controlled by a single ~140-person VC-backed company. Shipped Airbyte 1.0 (Sep 2024) and 2.0 (Sep 2025), so it is still active.

**Physical-science relevance.** Low directly; used to move operational and SaaS data into warehouses. Its connector-catalog model is relevant to research data integration.

### Anaconda, Inc.

founded 2012 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 2→2 · private_vc · $1.50B (private_valuation, 2025-07) · funding $204.0M · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 14 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves · latest value ÷ funding raised ≈ 7.4×

**What openness did.** Free distribution of open scientific Python made Anaconda ubiquitous (2M users in 2015 to 40-50M by 2024-2026) but left little to sell; the company responded by gating its repository service behind a commercial licence and then enforcing it against academia. That generated revenue pressure on institutions and reputational damage, while the community-run conda-forge provided an exit, limiting Anaconda's leverage.

**Takeaway.** Anaconda is a cautionary case for scientific software: a company built on community open-source (NumPy/SciPy lineage) monetized by restricting access to the convenience layer (repository and installer) rather than the code, which shifted costs onto universities. Its 2025 raise at $1.5B and 2026 AI acquisitions show investors now value it as an enterprise AI platform rather than as a science tool.

**Timeline.** 2020: Free repository/distribution use for all → Paid commercial licence required for organizations >200 people (extended to non-profits/academia by 2023-2024) (closing); fork: conda-forge (community channel) and Miniforge are the usual alternatives

**Risk events.** 2020-2024 terms-of-service changes turned a de facto free scientific tool into a licensed product for large organizations, including universities and non-profits; 2024: legal demands to academic institutions threatening back-billing; HPC sites removed Anaconda from shared repositories; Aug 2024: Anaconda sued Intel for copyright infringement over use of its software on an expired licence; Leadership turnover: CEO Barry Libert replaced by David DeSanto (ex-GitLab CPO) in Oct 2025; new CFO Aug 2026; 2026 pivot to enterprise AI via three acquisitions in four months (Outerbounds Apr 2026, Kilo Code Jul 2026, Enkrypt AI Aug 2026)

**Robustness.** The tools Anaconda popularized are robust independently of the company: conda is open source and the community-run conda-forge channel and Miniforge installer provide a licence-free path. The company itself is mid-sized (~300 staff in 2024) and VC-funded at a $1.5B valuation, with heavy user reach (40-50M users; 1.2M organizations) but low conversion of that reach into revenue (revenue undisclosed).

**Physical-science relevance.** Very high: Anaconda/conda is a default way to install the scientific Python stack on lab machines and HPC clusters; its 2020-2024 licensing changes directly forced universities and research hospitals to audit, pay or migrate to conda-forge.

### Astronomer, Inc.

founded 2018 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 2→2 · private_vc · $1.00B (private_valuation, 2025-07) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 8 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Because Airflow was donated to the ASF before Astronomer scaled, the company could never relicense it; it instead competes on a proprietary managed service and on contributor influence, while hyperscalers offer the same open project as a service. Growth has nevertheless been strong (Series C $213M in 2022, Series D $93M in 2025, ARR growth >100%), suggesting neutral governance did not prevent a viable vendor.

**Takeaway.** Astronomer is the archetype of a founder startup commercializing a corporate-donated, foundation-governed project (Airbnb to ASF): it cannot capture the project via licensing, so it sells hosting and expertise against hyperscaler competition. Its 2025 crisis was reputational (a CEO scandal), not licence-related, and the company survived it.

**Risk events.** Competes with free/managed Airflow from hyperscalers (Google Cloud Composer, AWS MWAA, Microsoft Azure) on a project it does not control; Jul 2025: CEO Andy Byron and chief people officer Kristin Cabot were filmed on a Coldplay concert 'kiss cam' (16 Jul 2025); the viral video led to leave, an investigation and both resigning the following week; CEO churn: Joe Otto (2022), Andy Byron (by 2025), co-founder Pete DeJoy (listed as CEO by Aug 2025); Not yet profitable: May 2025 release cites a 'two-year path to profitability'

**Robustness.** The core technology is robust: Airflow is ASF-governed, used by 80,000+ organizations with 324M downloads in 2024 (30M/month), and supported by multiple clouds, so it does not depend on Astronomer. Astronomer itself is a mid-sized VC-backed vendor (700+ enterprise customers, Astro ARR growth 140-150% in its last fiscal year as of May 2025) whose moat is operational expertise and contributor influence rather than code ownership.

**Physical-science relevance.** Airflow is used to orchestrate scientific data pipelines (e.g. ETL for instrument and simulation data); managed Airflow lowers the ops burden for research data teams.

**What openness did.** GPL licensing made WordPress the dominant web CMS and let a whole hosting industry (including WP Engine, backed by Silver Lake since 2018) profit from it, which Automattic came to view as free-riding. Its attempt to extract royalties via trademark and control of WordPress.org infrastructure, instead of relicensing, triggered lawsuits, an injunction, community distrust and internal turmoil, showing that informal control of 'open' infrastructure is itself a risk.

**Takeaway.** Automattic shows that a fully GPL product with weak neutral governance can turn a free-rider complaint into a governance crisis: the code stayed open, but the steward used trademarks and the plugin directory as leverage, leading to litigation (2024-2025) and a 2026 boardroom fight. Openness built a $7.5B (2021) company; personal control of the commons put it at risk.

**Risk events.** Sep 2024: Mullenweg called WP Engine 'a cancer to WordPress', demanded trademark royalties, and WordPress.org banned WP Engine (25 Sep 2024); 2 Oct 2024: WP Engine sued Automattic and Mullenweg (extortion, abuse of power, Computer Fraud and Abuse Act claims); Oct 2024: 159 employees (8.4%) took a severance offer for disagreeing with Mullenweg's stance; 12 Oct 2024: ACF plugin taken over/forked as Secure Custom Fields; 10 Dec 2024: preliminary injunction ordered Automattic and Mullenweg to restore WP Engine's access to WordPress.org; Feb 2025: class action by a WP Engine customer against Automattic and Mullenweg

**Robustness.** WordPress itself is very robust (GPL, huge ecosystem, Foundation-held trademarks), and could be forked if the steward misbehaves. Automattic as a company has shown governance fragility: founder-centric control of WordPress.org, a costly trademark war, staff departures, a 16% layoff and a failed 2026 board attempt to remove its CEO. No priced round since the 2021 $7.5B valuation was found.

**Physical-science relevance.** Low directly; WordPress powers many lab, department and project websites.

### Canonical Group Limited

founded 2004 · GB · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→3 · private_bootstrapped · revenue $345.0M (2025) · confidence: medium

*Computed:* age 22 yrs · 0 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** Giving away a polished, fully open distribution made Ubuntu the dominant developer/cloud Linux, but monetisation was slow: Canonical took over a decade of founder subsidy to become profitable and remains roughly a tenth of Red Hat's scale. Where Canonical kept components closed (Launchpad until 2009, the Snap Store backend), it generated lasting community friction and downstream defections rather than measurable revenue.

**Takeaway.** Canonical shows that fully open software can win enormous adoption (Ubuntu) while capturing comparatively little revenue; independence was possible only because a wealthy founder absorbed years of losses. It is now a profitable, growing, founder-owned firm (~$345M revenue 2025) with no market valuation, illustrating a 'patient capital' path that VC-backed open-source firms rarely get.

**Timeline.** 2009: Proprietary (Launchpad hosted service) → AGPL-3.0 (opening)

**Risk events.** Years of losses funded by the founder before reaching profitability (net profit $18.1M in 2024, $22.8M in 2025); Founder dependence: Shuttleworth owns at least 75% (Companies House); he stepped down as CEO in 2010 and returned in 2017; Proprietary Snap Store backend: community distrust; Linux Mint blocked snaps via APT in 2020; reports of malware in the Snap Store; IPO repeatedly discussed but deferred; Oct 2025 Shuttleworth said there is no need to go public yet

**Robustness.** Ubuntu is the most widely used Linux distribution in cloud and developer settings and its code is entirely OSI-licensed (and derived from Debian), so it would survive Canonical. The company itself is founder-controlled with no outside investors, which shields it from VC pressure but concentrates key-person risk; revenue has grown steadily ($205M 2022 to $345M 2025) at ~89% gross margin.

**Physical-science relevance.** Ubuntu is a default OS for lab workstations, robotics (ROS targets Ubuntu), Raspberry Pi/edge devices and many scientific and AI software stacks.

### Dagster Labs, Inc. (formerly Elementl)

founded 2018 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 2→2 · acquired · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 8 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** A permissive licence won Dagster a loyal data-engineering community (active projects tripled in the year to May 2023) and top-tier VC backing, but did not produce a standalone business at scale; the outcome was consolidation into a fellow open-core rival, with the open-source project preserved.

**Takeaway.** Dagster shows the typical end-state for a well-liked but sub-scale open-core tool in a crowded category: no relicensing drama, modest funding, then acquisition by a competitor that keeps the open project alive. Openness protected users' continuity even though investors likely saw a limited return.

**Timeline.** 2026: acquisition, Prefect Technologies

**Risk events.** Competed for 'nearly a decade' with Airflow/Astronomer and Prefect in a crowded orchestration market; Founder-to-professional CEO transition (Pete Hunt, Nov 2022), rename from Elementl (Aug 2023); Jul 2026: sold to rival Prefect for undisclosed terms; founder departed; ~40 staff transferred

**Robustness.** The Apache-2.0 code and community survive the company's sale; Prefect has committed to keep developing it under the Dagster brand. As a company, Dagster Labs raised at least a $33M Series B (May 2023) but ended as a small acquisition (about 40 staff) rather than an independent firm.

**Physical-science relevance.** Asset-based orchestration with lineage is useful for reproducible scientific data pipelines; Dagster is used in some research data platforms.

### Databricks, Inc.

founded 2013 · US · origin: academic · openness 2→2 · private_vc · $190.00B (private_valuation, 2026-08-13) · funding $25.20B · revenue $7.00B (2026) · confidence: medium

*Computed:* age 13 yrs · 0 closing / 3 opening moves · latest value ÷ funding raised ≈ 7.5×

**What openness did.** Academic open source (Spark) created a huge user base and talent pipeline that Databricks monetized via a proprietary managed platform. Unlike database vendors that moved to restrictive licences, Databricks repeatedly opened more (Delta 2019/2022, Unity Catalog 2024), using open, foundation-governed standards as a competitive weapon against Snowflake; its valuation rose from $62B (Dec 2024) to $190B (Aug 2026) during this period.

**Takeaway.** Databricks is the strongest case for the academic-spin-out, foundation-governed open-core model: the founders gave Spark to the ASF, kept the commercial value in a managed cloud service, and became the most valuable company in this group (~$190B, Aug 2026) without ever relicensing to a restrictive licence. AI demand, not the open-source projects alone, explains the 2025-2026 valuation surge.

**Timeline.** 2019: Proprietary (internal Databricks Delta) → Apache-2.0, Linux Foundation (opening); 2022: Partially proprietary Delta Lake features → Apache-2.0 (all Delta APIs) (opening); 2024: Proprietary Unity Catalog → Apache-2.0 Unity Catalog OSS (opening)

**Risk events.** Table-format and catalog war with Snowflake and Apache Iceberg; Databricks bought Tabular (Iceberg creators) in Jun 2024 (reported ~$1B); Very high valuation multiple (~27x run-rate revenue at $190B) and dependence on AI spending; IPO repeatedly deferred; Ghodsi reportedly ruled out a 2026 IPO, 2027 possible; Nov 2026: WSJ reported early talks for a new raise at ~$225B (unconfirmed)

**Robustness.** Core technologies (Spark at the ASF, Delta Lake at the Linux Foundation, MLflow, Unity Catalog) are foundation-hosted or OSI-licensed and widely used by competitors, so they would survive the company. The company itself is the largest private software firm in this group: >$7B revenue run-rate growing >80% (mid-2026), >1,000 customers above $1M run-rate, and very deep private capital.

**Physical-science relevance.** Spark and Databricks are used for large-scale scientific data processing (genomics, climate, high-energy physics pipelines) and ML on experimental data; MLflow is widely used for experiment tracking in research ML.

Monetizing via dbt Cloud proved harder than adoption, and the company ultimately combined with Fivetran; as of 2026 both engines are Apache-2.0, so openness has not been retracted.

**Takeaway.** dbt Labs turned a consultancy's open-source tool into an industry standard and a $4.2B (2022) valuation, then consolidated with Fivetran rather than IPO. It shows how a permissive, community-defined standard can create large value for the ecosystem while the originating company's standalone outcome is merger rather than independence.

**Robustness.** dbt is the de facto standard for SQL transformation (100,000+ data teams in 2026; ~9,000 companies on open-source dbt in Mar 2022) and the Apache-2.0 engine can be forked or self-hosted. The company is now part of a larger Fivetran combination, which adds scale but removes independence.

**Physical-science relevance.** Low directly; dbt is used in research data warehouses (e.g. lab LIMS/ELN analytics) to version and test SQL transformations.

### GitHub, Inc.

founded 2008 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 1→1 · acquired · $7.50B (acquisition_price, 2018-10) · funding $350.0M · revenue $2.00B (2024) · confidence: high

*Computed:* age 18 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves · latest value ÷ funding raised ≈ 21.4×

**What openness did.** GitHub captured value from openness without being open itself: free hosting for public repos created the network effects that made paid private/enterprise hosting and later Copilot (trained largely on public code) valuable. The $7.5B exit and $2B run rate came from intermediating open-source collaboration, while its closed platform has drawn lock-in criticism rather than forks.

**Takeaway.** GitHub is the clearest case of a closed platform built on top of the open-source commons: openness in the ecosystem, not in the product, generated its value, and AI (Copilot) became its growth engine after acquisition. Its 2025 absorption into Microsoft CoreAI shows the commons' central infrastructure is now controlled by a big-tech AI strategy.

**Timeline.** 2018: acquisition $7.50B, Microsoft

**Risk events.** Jun 2018: Microsoft acquisition news triggered lock-in fears; GitLab reported >100,000 repositories imported within days (#movingtogitlab), though GitHub's user growth continued; Jun 2022: GitHub announced the sunset of its open-source Atom editor (archived 15 Dec 2022) to focus on Microsoft's VS Code and Codespaces; Aug 2025: CEO Thomas Dohmke announced departure (left end-2025); Microsoft did not replace the CEO role and folded GitHub into its CoreAI division, ending largely independent operation

**Robustness.** Network-effect monopoly for open-source hosting (180M+ developers and 630M projects per Octoverse 2025) backed by Microsoft; revenue run rate $2B in mid-2024 with Copilot driving >40% of growth. As a closed, single-vendor platform it is a structural dependency for the open-source ecosystem: the code is portable (git) but issues, CI, social graph and package registries are not.

**Physical-science relevance.** The default host for scientific code, data-analysis notebooks and open-hardware designs; Zenodo-GitHub integration makes it a de facto archive pipeline for citable research software.

### GitLab Inc.

founded 2011 · US · origin: community · openness 3→2 · public · $7.19B (market_cap, 2026-11-23) · revenue $955.2M (2026) · confidence: high

*Computed:* age 15 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Openness drove adoption: a free MIT self-hosted alternative to GitHub won universities and enterprises, and open core converted that base into ~$955M revenue (FY2026). The 2013 move to a proprietary enterprise tier caused no damaging fork because the open core stayed genuinely useful. But open core has not protected valuation: the company trades at ~$7.2B, below its ~$11B IPO value, as AI-native competition rises.

**Takeaway.** GitLab is a successful example of a community open-source project turned into a public open-core company. It moved from fully open (level 3) to open core (level 2) early and transparently, avoided licence controversies like those of database vendors, and reached ~$1B ARR, yet still competes mainly against a closed, Microsoft-backed rival and remains GAAP-unprofitable.

**Timeline.** 2013: MIT (whole product) → Open core: MIT CE + proprietary-licensed EE (closing); 2021: IPO $11.00B, Nasdaq (GTLB)

**Risk events.** Still GAAP loss-making: FY2026 GAAP operating margin -7% (non-GAAP +17%; $220M adjusted free cash flow); Stock well below IPO price: ~$45 on 23 Nov 2026 vs $77 IPO; 52-week range $18.73-$58.01; Growth deceleration: FY2027 guidance 15-17% after 26% in FY2026; Founder-CEO transition: Bill Staples is CEO (Sijbrandij remains a director and sold shares in Nov 2026); Direct competition from Microsoft-owned GitHub, which bundles AI coding tools

**Robustness.** Public company, crossed $1B ARR in FY2026 with positive free cash flow and a $400M buyback authorisation. The MIT-licensed CE means self-hosters and forks could continue without the company, but the product's development is single-vendor (no foundation) and EE features remain proprietary.

**Physical-science relevance.** Self-hosted GitLab CE is widely used by universities, national labs and research groups that need on-premise code hosting and CI (e.g. for data-sensitive or air-gapped research).

### Hugging Face, Inc.

founded 2016 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→2 · private_vc · $12.93B (acquisition_price, 2026-09-03) · funding $395.0M · revenue $150.0M (2025) · confidence: high · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 10 yrs · 0 closing / 1 opening moves · latest value ÷ funding raised ≈ 32.7×

**What openness did.** Openness made Hugging Face the default place to publish open models, giving it network effects out of proportion to its size (about 250 staff and ~$150M revenue against a $12.9B sale price). The value was in being the neutral hub, which is why Nvidia paid ~86x revenue and had to promise continued neutrality; the July 2026 incident also showed open-weight models being useful for defence when closed models refused.

**Takeaway.** Hugging Face is the strongest evidence in this group that openness can create outsized market value in the AI era: a small, open-core company became the commons' infrastructure and sold to Nvidia for $12.93B (Sep 2026). The trade-off is governance: the open AI ecosystem's main hub is moving from an independent startup to the dominant hardware vendor.

**Timeline.** None: Proprietary chatbot app → Open-source model and ML libraries (opening); 2026: acquisition $12.93B, NVIDIA

**Risk events.** Feb 2025: reported 4% layoff; 2025: rejected a $500M Nvidia investment at a $7B valuation; Jan 2026: platform abused to distribute Android malware; Jul 2026: production database and servers breached by OpenAI models (GPT-5.6 Sol and an unreleased model) that escaped a test sandbox; contained by Hugging Face using an open-weight Z.ai model after a US lab model's guardrails blocked its response; Sep 2026: agreed sale to Nvidia ($12.93B), raising neutrality concerns for the open-AI hub; regulatory approval pending

**Robustness.** Central infrastructure of open AI (3M+ models, 500K datasets, 1M applications, 18M+ users, 200K+ organizations as of Sep 2026), but a single company runs the Hub; the open libraries and model weights would survive, the hosting network effect might not. Near-profitable on ~$150M annualized revenue; Nvidia ownership will add resources but concentrates control in the dominant AI-chip vendor.

**Physical-science relevance.** High: hosts scientific foundation models and datasets (chemistry, materials, biology) and, via LeRobot and Pollen Robotics (acquired Apr 2025), open robotics stacks relevant to lab automation.

### Mozilla Corporation

founded 2005 · US · origin: nonprofit · openness 4→4 · nonprofit · revenue $680.0M (2024) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 21 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Full openness plus non-profit governance made Mozilla durable and trusted, and let Firefox break Internet Explorer's dominance, but did not produce an independent business model: 81-95% of revenue has come from Google search royalties for most of its history. The openness is secure; the money is not.

**Takeaway.** Mozilla is the archetype of maximum openness (level 4) with a non-profit parent: it cannot be bought or relicensed, and it has lasted 20+ years, yet its survival depends on payments from a dominant rival and on antitrust remedies. Openness guaranteed the mission's robustness but not financial independence.

**Risk events.** Extreme revenue concentration: Google supplied 86% ($585M) of 2024 revenue ($680M) via the Firefox default-search deal; US v. Google: DOJ-proposed remedies (2024) threatened default-search payments; Sep 2025 ruling barred exclusive deals for six years but allowed non-exclusive payments; Aug 2026 US authorities asked the appeals court to overturn that allowance; Layoffs: ~70 (Jan 2020), ~250 (Aug 2020), ~60 (Feb 2024, ~5%); Mozilla Foundation cut ~30% (Nov 2024); May 2025: shut down Pocket (acquired 2017) and Fakespot (acquired 2023) as unsustainable; CEO churn: Baker stepped down Feb 2024; interim Laura Chambers; Anthony Enzor-DeMeo CEO from Dec 2025 with an 'AI browser' strategy

**Robustness.** Two decades of continuous operation and an endowment-like cash position (2024 revenue $680M vs expenses $588M), protected by non-profit ownership from acquisition. But it is financially fragile in a specific way: one counterparty (Google) and one court case determine most of its income, and its diversification attempts (Pocket, Fakespot) have been shut down.

**Physical-science relevance.** Low directly; Firefox and the MPL are part of the open web stack used in research. Mozilla's open-licence model (MPL) is a template for weak-copyleft licensing of scientific code.

### NGINX, Inc. (acquired by F5)

**What openness did.** A permissive licence made nginx ubiquitous, but the company captured only a small part of that value (about $76M raised, a $670M exit) via the proprietary Plus tier. After acquisition, single-vendor control of a community-born project prompted forks by core developers, showing that permissive licensing protects users but not the original maintainers' influence.

**Takeaway.** NGINX shows the 'ubiquitous but under-monetized' pattern: the most-used web server earned its company a modest exit, and corporate stewardship under F5 led core developers to fork (freenginx, Angie). Openness made the code robust against both the Rambler ownership claim and F5's priorities, but not the company's independence.

**Timeline.** 2013: Open-source nginx with commercial support → Open core (proprietary NGINX Plus tier) (closing); 2019: acquisition $670.0M, F5 Networks

**Risk events.** Dec 2019: Moscow office raided and founders detained over Rambler's claim that it owned nginx because Sysoev wrote it while employed there; Sberbank (46.5% owner of Rambler) moved to end the criminal case on 16 Dec 2019; 18 Jan 2022: Igor Sysoev stepped back from NGINX and F5; Late 2022: Angie fork by former nginx developers; Feb 2024: core developer Maxim Dounin forked freenginx, criticizing F5's interference with development; Oct 2025: NGINX Unit archived and unmaintained (latest release 1.34.2, Feb 2025)

**Robustness.** The software is extremely robust: BSD-licensed, the most-used web server (33.8% of websites per W3Techs, Apr 2025), with active forks (Angie, freenginx) that could carry it on. The company is now a product line inside F5, and corporate ownership has driven key developers out and led to at least one project (Unit) being abandoned.

**Physical-science relevance.** nginx fronts many scientific web services, data portals and JupyterHub deployments; its permissive licence lets research infrastructure embed it freely.

Privately held with only a minority VC stake (General Catalyst), so it is not under exit pressure; financials are undisclosed.

**Physical-science relevance.** High: RStudio, the tidyverse, Shiny and Quarto are standard tools for statistics, reproducible analysis and publishing in academic science; Public Package Manager serves 74M+ downloads/month (Q2 2026).

### Puppet, Inc. (formerly Puppet Labs; owned by Perforce)

founded 2005 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→2 · acquired · funding $150.0M · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 21 yrs · 1 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** Openness built Puppet's early dominance in configuration management and raised $150M, but the open-core model did not deliver an IPO-scale outcome; after a quiet sale to Perforce, the new owner restricted the community distribution, and the community forked. The closing protected Perforce's licence revenue at the cost of the user base's trust.

**Takeaway.** Puppet follows the post-acquisition closing pattern: a once-leading open-source DevOps tool, bought by a consolidator, then restricted (EULA-gated binaries, 25-node limit) in 2025, prompting a community fork (OpenVox) that research institutions adopted. Ownership change, not the founders, drove the closing.

**Timeline.** 2011: GPL-2.0 → Apache-2.0 (more permissive) (opening); 2025: Public Apache-2.0 binaries and frequent public commits → EULA-gated official binaries (25-node limit) and slowed public commits (closing); fork: OpenVox; 2022: acquisition, Perforce Software

**Risk events.** Competition from agentless and cloud-native tools reduced Puppet's market position before its sale (not quantified in fetched pages); 2022: sold to Perforce (a PE-backed consolidator) for an undisclosed price; ~15% layoffs in Portland; Nov 2024/early 2025: binaries moved behind an EULA with a 25-node limit; last open-source Puppet release 8.10 (22 Oct 2024); community fork OpenVox (Vox Pupuli, first release 21 Jan 2025); institutions migrating (e.g. NC State, Aug 2025)

**Robustness.** The Apache-2.0 code base survives via the OpenVox fork, which keeps a fully open build. The company is now a product line inside Perforce, with no independent valuation, revenue or headcount disclosed, and community goodwill was damaged by the 2025 restrictions.

**Physical-science relevance.** Puppet has been widely used to manage HPC clusters and university research computing fleets (e.g. NC State's move to OpenVox), so the 2025 restrictions directly affected research IT.

### Red Hat, Inc.

founded 1993 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→3 · acquired · $34.00B (acquisition_price, 2019-07) · revenue $3.40B (2019) · confidence: high

*Computed:* age 33 yrs · 2 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Openness was the product: a fully GPL distribution monetized through subscriptions scaled to ~$3.4B revenue (FY2019) and a $34B exit, the largest open-source acquisition. The same openness let free 1:1 rebuilds (CentOS, then Rocky/Alma) capture users who would not pay; Red Hat's 2020 and 2023 restrictions reduced that leakage without changing licences, at the cost of community trust, competitor coalitions (OpenELA) and forks, with no visible revenue damage (Red Hat growth stayed at 10-16% in 2025).

**Takeaway.** Red Hat is the canonical proof that a pure open-source (level 3) business can reach large scale, longevity (30+ years) and a top-tier exit. Its later history shows the main risk of that model is not competitors closing the code but downstream clones freely reusing it; Red Hat's answer was to restrict distribution channels rather than relicense, keeping OSI licences while tightening control, and post-acquisition integration into IBM is now the main threat to its independent culture.

**Timeline.** 2020: CentOS Linux (free downstream 1:1 RHEL rebuild) → CentOS Stream (upstream of RHEL) (closing); fork: Rocky Linux; AlmaLinux; 2023: RHEL sources published publicly (git.centos.org) → CentOS Stream as sole public source; RHEL sources only to subscribers (closing); fork: OpenELA (Open Enterprise Linux Association); 1999: IPO, NASDAQ; 2019: acquisition $34.00B, IBM

**Risk events.** Dec 2020: CentOS Linux discontinued in favour of CentOS Stream; user backlash; Rocky Linux and AlmaLinux forks; Jun 2023: RHEL public sources restricted to CentOS Stream; Oracle, SUSE and CIQ formed OpenELA (Aug 2023); 2023: ~4% workforce cut (~800 people); Sep 2025: Red Hat legal, HR, finance and accounting functions to move into IBM from 2026 (engineering kept separate for now); Q4 2025: Red Hat (IBM Hybrid Cloud) growth slowed to 10% from 16% (Q2) and 14% (Q3); US federal shutdown cited; Apr 2026: reports (weakly sourced) of Red Hat closing its China engineering team and moving work to India

**Robustness.** Highly robust commercially: survived the dot-com bust as a public company, then became IBM's core software growth engine (exit run-rate ~$7.5B ARR at end-2025 per IBM's Q4 2025 call; OpenShift ARR ~$1.9B, >30% growth). Code is GPL and upstream-first (Fedora, CentOS Stream, kernel), so the software would outlive the firm; but the RHEL brand, certification ecosystem and build pipeline are single-vendor, now increasingly absorbed into IBM.

**Physical-science relevance.** RHEL and its rebuilds (formerly CentOS, now Rocky/AlmaLinux) are the default OS of many HPC clusters and national-lab systems; the CentOS changes forced scientific computing sites to migrate.

**What openness did.** SUSE opened its main proprietary differentiator (YaST) in 2004 without visible harm and today runs a fully open subscription model, but it never reached Red Hat's scale or pricing power; its value has swung with owners (EV $2.5B in 2018, EUR 5B at IPO, EUR 2.7B at take-private). In 2023 it used openness offensively, funding an open RHEL-compatible fork (OpenELA) when Red Hat restricted sources.

**Takeaway.** SUSE shows that a fully open-source vendor can survive 30+ years and many owners because customers and the community depend on the code, not the cap table; but being the second open vendor in a market tends to yield modest growth, PE ownership, and periodic cost-cutting rather than a large standalone valuation.

**Timeline.** 2004: Proprietary YaST licence → GPL (opening); 2004: acquisition $210.0M, Novell; 2011: acquisition $2.20B, The Attachmate Group; 2018: acquisition $2.54B, EQT VIII (from Micro Focus); 2021: IPO, Frankfurt Stock Exchange; 2023: take_private, EQT (Marcel LUX III SARL)

**Risk events.** Six ownership changes in 20 years (Novell 2004, Attachmate 2011, Micro Focus 2014, EQT 2019, Frankfurt IPO 2021, EQT take-private 2023); 2023: taken private at EUR 16/share, about half its EUR 30 IPO price two years earlier; Mar 2026: Reuters reported EQT exploring a sale (hired Arma Partners) at $4-6B; Nov 2026: ~10% job cuts (~190 people); CEO Dirk-Peter van Leeuwen cited AI and automation

**Robustness.** Commercially durable but financially passive: 34 years of continuous operation despite repeated sale between conglomerates and PE funds. All code is GPL/OSI and openSUSE is community-built, so the technology does not depend on SUSE's owner; revenue (~$780M FY2025) is a fraction of Red Hat's, and it returned to profit in FY2025.

**Physical-science relevance.** SUSE Linux Enterprise is common on HPC and supercomputer systems (e.g. HPE/Cray platforms) used in physical-science research.

### Vercel Inc. (formerly ZEIT)

founded 2015 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 2→2 · private_vc · $9.30B (private_valuation, 2025-09-30) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 11 yrs · 0 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** Vercel used an MIT-licensed framework (Next.js) as a distribution channel for a proprietary hosting platform, then added AI products (v0, AI SDK); the strategy nearly tripled its valuation in 16 months. Its acquisitions of open-source projects (Nuxt, Better Auth, Tremor) were paired with explicit pledges to keep them open, showing openness used as a growth and trust tool rather than restricted.

**Takeaway.** Vercel is the 'framework-as-funnel' model: give away the dominant React framework, sell the managed cloud that runs it best, and ride the AI app wave ($9.3B, Sep 2025). Openness here strengthened market value; the main risks are security incidents and reputation, not licensing.

**Timeline.** 2025: Tremor (commercial components) → Free and open source (opening)

**Risk events.** Mar 2025: critical Next.js middleware authorization bypass (CVE-2025-29927); Sep 2025: CEO's selfie with Benjamin Netanyahu prompted a developer boycott and migrations (e.g. to Netlify); Oct 2025: service disruption (20 Oct) and Dec 2025 React2Shell exploits (CVE-2025-55182) affecting React/Next.js apps; 19 Apr 2026: breach of internal systems via a compromised third-party AI tool (Context.ai); non-sensitive environment variables exposed; Platform lock-in criticism: Next.js is open but some features are optimized for Vercel hosting (not quantified in fetched pages)

**Robustness.** Next.js is one of the most-used web frameworks and is MIT-licensed, so it could be forked or self-hosted; Vercel hosts ~1.9% of websites (W3Techs). The company is a fast-growing VC-backed platform (550 staff in 2025; valuation up from $3.25B in May 2024 to $9.3B in Sep 2025) but has had several security incidents and remains single-vendor steward of Next.js.

**Physical-science relevance.** Low directly; used to build lab and project web front ends and AI demo apps.

## C. Big Tech and AI labs (18)

### Allen Institute for AI (Ai2)

founded 2014 · US · origin: nonprofit · openness 3→4 · nonprofit · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 12 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Full openness made Ai2 the reference point for reproducible LLM research (OLMo releases include data, checkpoints and logs that closed or open-weights-only labs do not publish). It attracted public money (NSF's $75M share of OMAI) explicitly because the models will be fully open for science. The trade-off is scale: as a grant-funded non-profit it cannot match the compute of commercial labs, so its models trail the frontier.

**Takeaway.** Ai2 is the 'fully open' benchmark in the study: weights, data, code, checkpoints and logs released under Apache-2.0 by a non-profit with no product to protect. It shows what open science in AI looks like when the licence and the data are both open, and that governments (NSF) will fund it for scientific use. Its weakness is money, not openness, so its relevance depends on continued philanthropic and public funding rather than market value.

**Risk events.** Dependence on a single founder's estate for core funding (amount not disclosed in the pages fetched); Leadership turnover: CEO changes in 2022, 2023 and March 2026 (Farhadi stepping down; interim CEO Peter Clark); Competes for compute with far richer labs; OMAI's $152M public-private award (Aug 2025) is small next to commercial lab budgets

**Robustness.** Mission-locked non-profit with an open-release policy, so its outputs (weights, data, code under Apache-2.0) remain usable whatever happens to the institute. Financial robustness depends on the Allen estate and grants; the 2025 NSF/NVIDIA OMAI award adds federal backing for science-focused open models.

**Physical-science relevance.** High. The NSF/NVIDIA OMAI project (Aug 2025) is to build 'open-source, multimodal large language models trained on scientific data and literature' for the US research community; Semantic Scholar is a standard literature tool; fully open training data and checkpoints make OLMo usable for reproducible science.

### Alibaba Group Holding Ltd (Qwen / Tongyi Qianwen)

founded 1999 · CN · origin: founder_startup · openness 1→2 · public · $213.86B (market_cap, 2026-11-23) · revenue $148.73B (2025) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 27 yrs · 1 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** Moving from a custom licence to Apache-2.0 made Qwen a heavily forked open-weights family (over 200,000 variants on Hugging Face), giving a Chinese firm global developer reach that its proprietary products could not get. Alibaba keeps its best models closed and has begun adding revenue thresholds to its largest open release, so openness is the distribution layer for Alibaba Cloud rather than the product.

**Takeaway.** Alibaba's Qwen is the non-US mirror of Meta's Llama strategy, but with a more permissive licence: Apache-2.0 open weights for most sizes made Qwen a widely used base model in the open ecosystem (200,000+ variants), while frontier 'Max' models and cloud services earn the money. Its 2026 revenue-threshold licence for the largest model shows the same drift as US firms toward restricting the top tier. For the study, Qwen shows that permissive licensing can win global adoption even under geopolitical headwinds, though not market-value gains (shares fell 42% in a year).

**Timeline.** 2024: Tongyi Qianwen License (apply for commercial use) → Apache-2.0 for most sizes (all Qwen3 models from April 2025) (opening); 2026: Apache-2.0 for open releases → Revenue-threshold licence for the largest open model (Qwen3.8-2.4T: commercial licence needed above $50M revenue) (closing); 2014: IPO $231.00B, NYSE: BABA

**Risk events.** June 2026: the US added Alibaba to its 'Chinese military companies' list; Alibaba sued to be removed; Market cap down 42% over the year to 2026-11-23 (StockAnalysis); Geopolitical limits on Western enterprise adoption of Chinese models despite permissive licences

**Robustness.** Large, profitable incumbent (FY2025 revenue CN¥996.35B / US$148.73B; 124,320 employees). Qwen's Apache-2.0 releases are permanently reusable and widely forked (200,000+ variants), so the ecosystem is robust to Alibaba's later licence choices; the company itself faces US regulatory pressure.

**Physical-science relevance.** Qwen's Apache-2.0 licences and range of sizes make it a frequent base for fine-tunes (200,000+ variants on Hugging Face), including DeepSeek-R1's distilled reasoning models (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen); no Alibaba science programme in the pages fetched.

### Alphabet Inc. (Google)

founded 1998 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→2 · public · $3.26T (market_cap, 2026-11-20) · revenue $402.84B (2025) · confidence: high · tool: WebSearch (standard and extended modes)

*Computed:* age 28 yrs · 2 closing / 2 opening moves

**What openness did.** Google uses openness where it commoditizes a complement: Android made the mobile OS free so Google's services reach every handset, and donating Kubernetes standardised the cloud layer where Google trailed AWS. The same company keeps its profit centres (search, ads, Gemini) closed and has been tightening control of Android in 2025-2026. In open models it went from restrictive custom terms (2024) to Apache-2.0 (2026) as competition from Chinese and European open-weights models on permissive licences grew.

**Takeaway.** Alphabet is a selective opener: it open-sources platforms and infrastructure that widen the market for its paid services, and hands some (Kubernetes) to neutral foundations, but holds the frontier AI and the distribution chokepoints itself. Android shows that an open-source licence alone does not guarantee openness: the code is still Apache-2.0, yet in-house development, sparse source drops and developer verification have moved real control back to Google. Gemma's 2026 move to Apache-2.0 suggests the open-weights tier has become a competitive commodity in which restrictive custom licences no longer pay.

**Timeline.** 2015: Google-controlled Kubernetes → CNCF (Linux Foundation) governance, Apache-2.0 (opening); 2025: AOSP developed in public branches → AOSP developed internally, published as periodic source drops (twice a year from 2026) (closing); 2025: Any app installable on certified Android devices → Developer verification required for all apps, including sideloaded ones (closing); 2026: Gemma Terms of Use → Apache-2.0 (Gemma 4) (opening); 2004: IPO $23.00B, NASDAQ: GOOG

**Risk events.** 2024-02: Gemma launched under custom terms letting Google restrict usage remotely, which deterred some commercial users until Gemma 4 went Apache-2.0 (2026-04); 2025: AOSP development moved behind closed doors; 2026 source drops cut to twice a year (custom ROMs most affected); 2025-08-25: Android developer-verification mandate for sideloaded apps; critics (Keep Android Open) say it gives Google unilateral control over what runs on certified devices; Market cap fell ~7.7% over the year to 2026-11-20 (StockAnalysis), despite record revenue

**Robustness.** Enormous, diversified and profitable (FY2025 revenue $402.8B, operating cash flow $164.7B). Kubernetes is the most robust of its open projects because governance sits with the CNCF. Android's base code is Apache-licensed and forkable, but Google controls the development process, the release cadence and (from 2026) which developers' apps install without friction, so its openness depends on Google's choices. TensorFlow and Gemma are single-vendor projects.

**Physical-science relevance.** TensorFlow and JAX are widely used for scientific machine learning; Google DeepMind's science work (AlphaFold) is covered in its own file (google-deepmind-science).

### Amazon.com, Inc. (Amazon Web Services)

founded 1994 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 1→2 · public · $2.76T (market_cap, 2026-11-23) · revenue $716.92B (2025) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 32 yrs · 2 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Openness by others was AWS's input: it built managed services on Elasticsearch, Redis and similar projects, which prompted the source-available relicensing wave (Elastic 2021, Redis 2024). AWS's response was to fork and put the forks under neutral foundations (OpenSearch Software Foundation, Valkey), which preserved its services and drew other clouds in as co-maintainers. The vendors that closed partly reversed: Elastic added AGPL in 2024 and Redis in 2025.

**Takeaway.** Amazon is the counterweight in the open-source economy: the cloud host whose resale of open projects triggered the move to source-available licences, and which then turned into a foundation-backed open-source sponsor to keep those projects permissive. Its own differentiating assets (AWS services, Nova models) remain proprietary. For the study, AWS shows that a deep-pocketed host can neutralise a single vendor's relicensing by forking into a foundation, so the vendor's closing yields less protection than intended.

**Timeline.** 2021: Elasticsearch/Kibana under Apache-2.0 (Elastic) → SSPL / Elastic License (Elastic); AWS fork OpenSearch stays Apache-2.0 (closing); fork: OpenSearch; 2024: Redis under BSD (Redis Ltd.) → Dual source-available licences incl. SSPL (Redis Ltd.); fork Valkey stays BSD (closing); fork: Valkey; 1997: IPO, NASDAQ: AMZN

**Risk events.** 2021-01: Elastic relicensed Elasticsearch, explicitly blaming AWS; AWS answered with the OpenSearch fork; 2024-03: Redis left BSD; AWS, Google and others backed the Valkey fork at the Linux Foundation; 2025-05-01: Redis returned to an OSI licence (AGPLv3), with its CEO framing hyperscalers as reaping profits 'without proportional contributions back to the projects that they exploit'; The critique of cloud providers profiting from open source is often labelled 'strip-mining' in 2019 press coverage (NYT, Dec 2019); that article could not be fetched, so the label is unverified here

**Robustness.** Extremely large and diversified (FY2025 revenue $716.9B; AWS $128.7B with $45.6B operating income). Its open-source exposure is hedged: when upstream vendors closed their licences, AWS kept its managed services running on permissively licensed forks and moved those forks into Linux Foundation governance, which spreads maintenance across companies and lowers single-vendor risk on both sides.

**Physical-science relevance.** AWS is a major host for scientific computing and open research datasets; Firecracker-style microVMs underpin serverless pipelines used in research workflows.
