### Anthropic, PBC

founded 2021 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→1 · public · $1.17T (market_cap, 2026-11-23) · revenue $4.58B (2025) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 5 yrs · 0 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** Anthropic shows that a closed-weights lab can still shape the open ecosystem: by open-sourcing MCP and then handing it to a neutral foundation, it made its integration approach the industry standard (adopted by OpenAI and Google DeepMind) at little cost to its proprietary model business. Keeping weights closed has not limited its market value: valuations went from $61.5B (Mar 2025) to $380B (Feb 2026) to a $1.17T market cap after its Nov 2026 IPO.

**Takeaway.** Anthropic is the 'closed model, open standards' strategy: it keeps the frontier models proprietary on safety and commercial grounds while open-sourcing the interfaces (MCP) and the science of understanding models (interpretability tools), then putting the most important of these under neutral governance. That combination earned it the network effects of a standard without the competitive exposure of open weights. For the study, it shows that openness at the protocol layer can be strategically decisive even for a lab that releases no model weights.

**Timeline.** 2025: Anthropic-led MCP project → Agentic AI Foundation (Linux Foundation) governance (opening); 2026: IPO $1.10T, Nasdaq: ANT

**Risk events.** Bartz v. Anthropic (filed Aug 2024, pirated books): $1.5B settlement agreed Sept 2025 and approved July 2026, the largest known US copyright settlement; Net losses of about $42.0B (2025) and $80.7B (H1 2026), mostly paper losses on convertible bonds per Wikipedia; H1 2026 revenue $16.3B; March 2026: the Claude Code CLI source code leaked (per Wikipedia); April 2022: $500M of a $580M round came from FTX

**Robustness.** Now a listed company (market cap $1.17T on 2026-11-23, share price $87.64) with very fast revenue growth ($4.6B in 2025, $16.3B in H1 2026) and 5,300 staff, backed by Amazon and Google. Governance is mission-locked by PBC status and the Long-Term Benefit Trust's board-election rights. Its open contributions are robust in a different way: MCP now sits in a Linux Foundation fund co-founded with competitors, so it no longer depends on Anthropic.

**Physical-science relevance.** Anthropic frames 'increasing the rate of scientific progress' as core to its public-benefit mission (Claude for Life Sciences, Oct 2025). MCP gives labs a vendor-neutral interface for connecting models to external data and tools (an inference about applicability, not a sourced deployment count).

### Apple Inc.

founded 1976 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→1 · public · $5.07T (market_cap, 2026-11-23) · revenue $478.83B (2026) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 50 yrs · 1 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** Apple shows that a near-closed strategy can still produce the highest market value: its returns come from integration and control, not from ecosystems built on open code. Where it opened things (Swift in 2015, MLX), it gained developer reach on its own hardware. Its limited participation in open AI has left it reliant on a competitor's frontier models (Gemini) rather than an open community.

**Takeaway.** Apple is the control case for the study: the least open of the big platforms in AI and developer tooling, and the most valuable. Openness at Apple is tactical and peripheral (Swift, MLX, small OpenELM models under a non-OSI licence), and even its open OS core has become less visible over time. Its 2026 reliance on Google's Gemini suggests that a closed strategy without frontier models of its own leaves it buying from rivals, a different risk from that faced by open-ecosystem players.

**Timeline.** 2015: Proprietary Swift (1.0-2.1) → Apache-2.0 Swift (2.2+) (opening); 2023: Darwin promoted as Apple's open-source OS core → Darwin no longer named on Apple's open-source site; incomplete source collections (closing); 1980: IPO $1.78B, NASDAQ: AAPL

**Risk events.** AI dependence on a rival: Jan 2026 multi-year partnership under which Apple's next-generation foundation models were expected to incorporate Google's Gemini models and cloud (Bloomberg reported a ~$1B/year deal on 2025-11-05); at WWDC June 2026 Apple said the new Siri does not run on Gemini but its models are 'refined using outputs from Gemini frontier models'; Declining transparency of Darwin sources since 2005 (and since 2023 on Apple's open-source site)

**Robustness.** The world's most valuable company in this group ($5.07T on 2026-11-23, up 26% in a year; FY2026 revenue $478.8B). Its strength is vertical integration of hardware, OS and services, all proprietary; open projects (Swift, WebKit, MLX) are maintained by Apple without foundation governance. In AI it is a follower, buying or distilling frontier capability from Google rather than building an open ecosystem.

**Physical-science relevance.** MLX makes Apple-silicon laptops usable for local ML on lab data; Swift and Darwin have little direct physical-science role.

### DeepSeek

founded 2023 · CN · origin: corporate · openness 1→3 · private_vc · $70.00B (private_valuation, 2026-10) · funding $7.00B · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 3 yrs · 0 closing / 1 opening moves · latest value ÷ funding raised ≈ 10.0×

**What openness did.** Openness was DeepSeek's route to global relevance: MIT-licensed weights at frontier quality and low cost made R1 the most-downloaded app in the US within a week and wiped about $600B off Nvidia in a day. Openness did not prevent commercial value. Within 18 months the company raised $7B at $52B and then a round at a $70B pre-money valuation, and it keeps releasing V4-class models under MIT. It also triggered strategic responses: US government-device bans and, per CNBC reporting, part of Meta's retreat from open frontier weights.

**Takeaway.** DeepSeek is the strongest evidence in this group that open weights can shift market structure: an MIT-licensed model from a 2023 hedge-fund spin-off moved hundreds of billions in market value, prompted Sam Altman to say OpenAI needed 'a different open source strategy' (Jan 2025), before its Apache-2.0 gpt-oss release (Aug 2025), and fed Meta's rethink and still produced a $52-70B private valuation. Its openness is about weights and licence, not data, and its biggest risks are geopolitical rather than commercial. For the study, it shows that a permissive licence can be a competitive weapon for a challenger rather than a give-away.

**Timeline.** 2025: Custom DeepSeek Model License (weights) → MIT (weights and code), from R1 onward (opening)

**Risk events.** 2025-01-27: R1 release sparked a 17% fall in Nvidia shares (close $118.58) and a loss of close to $600B of market value, the biggest single-day drop in US history; DeepSeek topped ChatGPT as the most-downloaded free app on the US iOS App Store; US National Defense Authorization Act for FY2026 directs removal of DeepSeek and High-Flyer AI from Defense and Intelligence Community devices (research exceptions); Australia bans DeepSeek on government systems; Geopolitical/export-control exposure as a Chinese lab; reported Meta frustration that R1 drew on Llama's architecture fed Meta's turn to closed models (see meta.json); 2026-07: Bloomberg reported IPO preparations, with a listing as soon as 2027

**Robustness.** Small (396 employees, 2026) but now well capitalised: a $7B Series A at a $52B post-money valuation (May 2026, per the FT) and a further round at a $70B pre-money valuation completed in October 2026 (SCMP, The Standard). Because every major model since R1 is MIT-licensed, its work survives independently of the company; dependence on a founder-controlled hedge fund and Chinese regulation are the main risks.

**Physical-science relevance.** MIT-licensed reasoning models that can be run locally are usable for scientific and lab-automation tasks without data leaving the lab; DeepSeek has no dedicated science programme in the pages fetched.

### EleutherAI (EleutherAI Institute)

founded 2020 · origin: community · openness 4→4 · nonprofit · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 6 yrs · 0 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** EleutherAI showed in 2020-2022 that a volunteer community could replicate GPT-3-class research openly (GPT-J, GPT-NeoX-20B), and The Pile became a standard training corpus that seeded much of the open-model ecosystem. Openness of data also exposed it to the copyright backlash, which it answered by building the Common Pile from openly licensed text only. Its influence comes through others' models and research rather than through any market value of its own.

**Takeaway.** EleutherAI is the community-origin case: openness was its entire reason for existing, and it converted into a non-profit institute rather than a company. It shows that open AI science can be produced at low cost by volunteers with donated compute, that open data carries legal risk that full transparency makes visible, and that the fix is better-licensed data rather than closing. For the study, it is a pure public-goods producer whose value appears in downstream adoption, not in a valuation.

**Timeline.** 2025: Training data of mixed provenance including copyrighted works (The Pile) → Only works whose licences permit AI training (Common Pile v0.1) (opening)

**Risk events.** The Pile 'has come under scrutiny for containing copyrighted material' (books and video subtitles); a July 2024 Proof News/Wired investigation found subtitles from over 170,000 YouTube videos, drawing accusations of theft; Dependence on donated compute and sponsor funding, including from AI companies such as Stability AI, which itself went through a 2023-24 cash crisis (see stability-ai.json)

**Robustness.** Low cost base (volunteers plus a small institute staff) and fully open outputs, so its models, datasets and evaluation tools persist regardless of the organisation's fortunes. Financially fragile in principle, being dependent on philanthropic and corporate sponsors and donated compute.

**Physical-science relevance.** Its open checkpoints (Pythia) and openly licensed training data (Common Pile) are the substrate for reproducible research on how language models learn, relevant to any lab that needs auditable models; no physical-science-specific programme in the pages fetched.

### Google DeepMind, AI for Science (AlphaFold) and Isomorphic Labs

founded 2010 · GB · origin: founder_startup · openness 4→2 · acquired · funding $7.70B · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 16 yrs · 1 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** Openness turned AlphaFold 2 into infrastructure: free code, parameters and a CC-BY database of over 200 million structures made it the default tool in structural biology and set up a Nobel Prize within three years. When AlphaFold 3 was restricted to protect the commercial value captured by Isomorphic Labs, the community rebuilt it under MIT/Apache licences within about two years, and Google partially re-opened (Apache-2.0 code). Commercial value is now being pursued privately: Isomorphic has raised $7.7B with no drug yet in human trials.

**Takeaway.** AlphaFold is the cleanest natural experiment in the study: the fully open version (AF2 plus the EMBL-EBI database) produced enormous scientific impact and prestige, while the restricted successor (AF3) mainly spurred open clones and a well-funded proprietary spin-out. For physical-science labs, it shows that open science infrastructure gets adopted fastest when data and weights are under CC-BY/Apache terms and co-hosted by a public institution, and that a corporate owner will close the next generation once a commercial route (drug discovery) appears.

**Timeline.** 2024: Fully open AlphaFold 2 (Apache-2.0 code, CC BY 4.0 parameters, CC-BY database) → AlphaFold 3 via server only, then non-commercial weights obtainable only from Google (closing); fork: Open reimplementations: Protenix (ByteDance, Apache-2.0), OpenFold-3 (AlQuraishi lab, MIT), Boltz-1/2 (MIT); 2025: AlphaFold 3 weights on request only → AF3 publicly available for non-commercial use (Feb 2025); code later Apache-2.0 (opening); 2014: acquisition, Google

**Risk events.** 2024: AlphaFold 3 withheld at launch (server-only), then non-commercial weights; open clones (Protenix, OpenFold-3, Boltz) emerged under permissive licences; GNoME materials-discovery claims (Nov 2023) drew criticism that it did not make 'a useful, practical contribution to the experimental materials scientists'; Isomorphic Labs: raised $7.7B in 2025-26 (no valuation disclosed) but as of Nov 2026 its pipeline 'remained in preclinical development'

**Robustness.** Backed by Alphabet (see alphabet-google.json, $3.26T) and, for Isomorphic, by $7.7B of outside capital plus pharma deals (Eli Lilly and Novartis in Jan 2024, reported as worth nearly $3B combined; Johnson & Johnson in Jan 2026). The AlphaFold DB is co-run with EMBL-EBI under CC-BY-4.0, so the most-used asset is institutionally anchored outside Google.

**Physical-science relevance.** Central. AlphaFold won half of the 2024 Nobel Prize in Chemistry (Hassabis and Jumper) and its database underpins structural biology. GNoME targets inorganic materials discovery, AlphaGenome predicts the effects of DNA sequences on gene regulation (AlphaGenome Atlas, Sept 2026), and Isomorphic Labs applies these models to drug design.

### International Business Machines Corporation (IBM)

founded 1911 · US · origin: corporate · openness 0→2 · public · $220.21B (market_cap, 2026-11-23) · revenue $67.54B (2025) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 115 yrs · 1 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** IBM turned openness into an acquisition strategy: rather than build open communities, it has bought three open-source-origin companies for about $51B combined ($34B, $6.4B and an ~$11B valuation). Red Hat gave IBM a durable subscription business. HashiCorp shows the limit of buying a company that had already closed its licence: the community value had partly moved to OpenTofu. In AI, IBM chose Apache-2.0 Granite models plus transparency claims, a different bet from the closed frontier labs.

**Takeaway.** IBM is the open-source acquirer: having been a proprietary vendor for most of its history, it now buys the companies that monetise open communities (Linux, Terraform, Kafka) and releases its own AI models under Apache-2.0. The market has not rewarded the strategy as richly as the AI-centric giants (about $220B market cap, down ~21% in a year). For the study, IBM shows that openness can be bought as a revenue stream, and that licence closures made before an acquisition (HashiCorp) carry their forks along with them.

**Timeline.** 2023: MPL-2.0 (Terraform and other HashiCorp products) → Business Source License 1.1 (closing); fork: OpenTofu (initially OpenTF, renamed Sept 2023); 2024: Proprietary watsonx foundation models → Granite models open-sourced under Apache-2.0 (opening)

**Risk events.** 2019-07: Red Hat acquisition ($34B), IBM's largest, tying IBM's growth to an open-source business; 2023-08: HashiCorp relicensed Terraform to BUSL and the community forked OpenTofu under the Linux Foundation; IBM bought HashiCorp anyway ($6.4B, closed 2025-02-27); 2025-12-08: agreed to buy Confluent at an $11B valuation; completed 2026-03-17; Market cap down about 20.8% over the year to 2026-11-23 (StockAnalysis)

**Robustness.** A 115-year-old company with $67.5B revenue (2025) and 264,300 employees. Its open-source exposure is now very large (Red Hat, HashiCorp, Confluent), which spreads risk across established communities (Linux, Kubernetes, Kafka). The ownership of the HashiCorp tools is a weak point: the community fork OpenTofu sits in a neutral foundation, so customers have a ready exit if IBM's licensing displeases them.

**Physical-science relevance.** IBM has long-standing research in materials and chemistry (e.g. AI for materials discovery) and quantum computing; Granite's Apache-2.0 licence makes it usable in lab tooling without licence friction.

### Meta Platforms, Inc. (formerly Facebook, Inc.)

founded 2004 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→1 · public · $1.43T (market_cap, 2026-11-20) · revenue $200.97B (2025) · confidence: high · tool: WebSearch (standard and extended modes)

*Computed:* age 22 yrs · 1 closing / 4 opening moves

**What openness did.** Openness made Meta's developer tools (React, PyTorch) industry standards, and Meta then handed them to neutral foundations, which locks in that adoption beyond Meta's control. Releasing Llama weights bought ecosystem reach (1B downloads by March 2025, 1.2B by April 2025) and a commoditize-the-complement position against closed API labs, but did not give Meta a frontier lead, and competitors (notably DeepSeek) built on its work. In 2026 Meta moved its frontier model behind a paid API, a sign that open weights were a tactic rather than a principle once it judged it could compete directly.

**Takeaway.** Meta is the clearest example of commoditize-your-complement openness in AI: its money comes from advertising, so giving away frameworks and model weights cost little and undercut rivals whose business is selling model access. The record splits in two. Developer infrastructure was open-sourced and then put under neutral governance, which makes it robust. Model weights stayed under Meta's own non-OSI licence and were the first thing to close once the strategy changed. For the study, Meta shows that licence and governance, not the label 'open source', decide whether openness survives a change of corporate strategy.

**Timeline.** 2017: BSD+Patents → MIT (opening); 2022: PyTorch governed by Meta (BSD-style) → PyTorch Foundation under the Linux Foundation (same licence) (opening); 2023: Llama 1 non-commercial research licence → Llama 2 Community License (commercial use with 700M MAU cap) (opening); 2025: React governed by Meta (MIT) → React Foundation under the Linux Foundation (MIT) (opening); 2026: Open-weights frontier models (Llama community licence) → Closed-weight frontier model with paid API (Muse Spark) (closing); 2012: IPO $104.00B, NASDAQ: FB (now META)

**Risk events.** 2017-07: Apache Software Foundation classed React's BSD+Patents licence as Category X, forcing the Sept 2017 MIT relicense; 2023-03-03: LLaMA weights leaked via 4chan within weeks of the gated research release; Meta answered with DMCA takedowns, then opened commercial use with Llama 2; 2025: Llama 4's reception and DeepSeek R1's reuse of Llama architecture fed internal doubts about open weights; Meta Superintelligence Labs formed after the $14.3B Scale AI deal; 2026-04-08: Pivot to closed frontier model (Muse Spark); developers who built on Llama face an uncertain open roadmap; 2026: ~8,000-person layoff (May) and a $9.89B charge for an August 2026 multistate attorneys-general agreement (Q3 2026 results)

**Robustness.** Very large, profitable single-vendor company (FY2025 revenue $201B). Its most widely used open projects no longer depend on Meta's strategy: PyTorch (Linux Foundation, since 2022) and React (React Foundation, since 2026) have multi-company boards, and OCP has had its own foundation since 2011. Llama, by contrast, is controlled solely by Meta, its licence is non-OSI, and the April 2026 closed Muse Spark launch shows the open-weights line can be deprioritised at will.

**Physical-science relevance.** PyTorch is the default deep-learning framework for scientific machine learning; Llama weights are widely fine-tuned for domain (including chemistry and materials) assistants because they can be run locally.

### Microsoft Corporation

founded 1975 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→2 · public · $3.60T (market_cap, 2026-11-22) · revenue $331.84B (2026) · confidence: medium · tool: WebSearch (standard and extended modes)

*Computed:* age 51 yrs · 0 closing / 3 opening moves

**What openness did.** Microsoft's move from hostility (2001) to open-sourcing .NET (2014), VS Code (2015) and buying GitHub (2018) coincided with its pivot to the Azure cloud, where hosting open-source and Linux workloads was the business. Opening the developer layer won developer mindshare without giving up the proprietary cloud and productivity services that earn the money; by FY2026 Azure alone passed $100B. In AI it pairs a closed frontier partner (OpenAI) with MIT-licensed small models and tooling.

**Takeaway.** Microsoft is the canonical convert: once its revenue moved from selling Windows licences to renting cloud capacity, open source changed from threat to on-ramp, and it open-sourced its developer stack and bought the platform where open source lives. Its openness is real at the tool and small-model layer and absent at the money-making core, an open-core strategy run at the scale of a $3.6T company. For the study it shows that a firm's stance on openness tracks where its profits come from, not ideology.

**Timeline.** 2014: Proprietary .NET Framework → MIT-licensed, cross-platform .NET Core under the .NET Foundation (opening); 2025: Phi-4 under a research licence on Azure only → Phi-4 weights under MIT on Hugging Face (opening); 2025: Closed GitHub Copilot Chat extension → MIT-licensed Copilot Chat extension, being folded into VS Code core (opening)

**Risk events.** 2001: Ballmer's 'Linux is a cancer' remark crystallised developer distrust that the later open-source turn had to overcome; 2018-10-25: acquired GitHub in a $7.5B stock transaction (including $1.3B cash for vested GitHub equity awards and an indemnity escrow), putting the main hub of open-source development under one vendor; new CEO Nat Friedman pledged GitHub would operate independently in response to developer concerns; AI dependence on a closed partner: FY2026 net income included $4.963B of net gains from its OpenAI investment; FY2026: total headcount fell ~5,000 to ~223,000, the first annual decline in a decade (R&D down ~4,000 from the 2024 peak)

**Robustness.** Highly diversified and profitable (FY2026 revenue $331.8B, net income $133.7B; Azure passed $100B). .NET sits in a foundation and VS Code, TypeScript and Phi are under permissive licences, so they can be forked, but Microsoft controls their roadmaps; the official VS Code builds and Copilot services are proprietary. Owning GitHub gives Microsoft a central position in the open-source supply chain.

**Physical-science relevance.** GitHub hosts most open research software; VS Code and Python tooling are standard in scientific computing; Phi models are small enough to run locally for lab use.

### Mistral AI SAS

founded 2023 · FR · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→2 · private_vc · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 3 yrs · 1 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** Openness made Mistral famous within months of founding and underpinned its pitch as Europe's open alternative; valuations went from about EUR240M (June 2023) to EUR11.7B (Sept 2025) and EUR21-25B (Sept 2026). When it closed its flagships in 2024 it kept the open small models, and when permissive Chinese models raised the bar it re-opened at the frontier tier (Mistral 3, Dec 2025). The licence choice is tactical, tracking competition and customers' wish for on-premises deployment.

**Takeaway.** Mistral is the open-core AI lab: it uses Apache-2.0 releases for reach and sovereignty credentials, and keeps some premier models closed or under custom licences for revenue. Its licence history oscillates (open, then partly closed in 2024, then broadly open again in 2025-26), which shows that for AI labs openness is a competitive setting rather than a fixed identity. For the study, it is the best non-US example of openness used to build a high-valuation challenger quickly.

**Timeline.** 2024: Apache-2.0 for all released models → Proprietary, non-production or research licences for flagship/code models (closing); 2025: Research/non-production licences for large models → Apache-2.0 for the full Mistral 3 family including Large 3 (opening)

**Risk events.** 2024: flagship and code models moved to proprietary, non-production or research licences in the same year as Microsoft's $16M investment (Feb 2024), blurring the company's 'open' positioning; Valuation uncertainty: after the Sept 2026 Samsung transaction (EUR3B), Wikipedia says valuation reached EUR21B, while the cited Les Echos headline (2026-09-08) says nearly EUR25B; at the ECB rate of 1.1355 USD/EUR (2026-09-30) that is about $23.8-28.4B, so latest_value_usd is left null; 2026-03: $830M debt raised for data centres near Paris and in Sweden (Reuters), adding capital intensity

**Robustness.** Fast-growing (1,000+ staff in 2026) and backed by strategic industrial investors (ASML led the EUR1.7B Series C at EUR11.7B post-money on 2025-09-09 with EUR1.3B for ~11%; Samsung in 2026). Its Apache-2.0 models are irrevocably usable by others, so its open contribution is robust even if the company changes course; the business depends on selling premier models and enterprise/sovereign deployments against much larger US rivals.

**Physical-science relevance.** Apache-2.0 models from small (3B) to frontier scale (675B) can be deployed on-premises in European labs and industry (e.g. ASML partnership for semiconductor manufacturing).

### NVIDIA Corporation

founded 1993 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→1 · public · $3.99T (market_cap, 2026-11-23) · revenue $215.90B (2026) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 33 yrs · 0 closing / 2 opening moves

**What openness did.** Closed CUDA has been the most valuable lock-in in computing: open alternatives (OpenCL, ROCm) exist but CUDA-enabled hardware comes only from NVIDIA. NVIDIA opens what sells more GPUs: kernel modules for data-center Linux, Slurm stewardship, and fully documented open models (Nemotron) that customers train and serve on its hardware. Open models elsewhere cut both ways: DeepSeek's efficient open weights briefly wiped about $600B off its value in January 2025, yet its market cap stood near $4T in November 2026.

**Takeaway.** NVIDIA is the complement that profits whenever models are open: the more labs and companies train and run open-weights models, the more GPUs they buy, so NVIDIA now funds one of the most transparent open-model programmes (weights, data, recipes) while keeping CUDA closed. Its openness is therefore strategic and layered: open at the edges and in models, closed at the software platform that locks in its hardware. For the study, NVIDIA shows that a closed platform can coexist with, and benefit from, open science built on top of it.

**Timeline.** 2022: Proprietary Linux kernel driver modules → Dual GPL/MIT open kernel modules (default from R560, 2024) (opening); 2025: Few open models → Large open-weights programme with open data and recipes (Nemotron 3, Dec 2025) (opening); 1999: IPO, NASDAQ: NVDA

**Risk events.** 2025-01: lost about $600B of market value in one day (the largest one-day loss for a US company) after DeepSeek showed a cheaper path to frontier-class models; CUDA lock-in invites compatibility layers (HIP, ZLUDA, SCALE) and open rivals (ROCm, oneAPI); ZLUDA was not released officially by AMD or Intel for lack of a business case; 2025-12: acquired SchedMD, maker of the open-source Slurm workload manager; NVIDIA stated Slurm would remain open-source and vendor-neutral; Market cap down 8.3% over the year to 2026-11-23 (StockAnalysis)

**Robustness.** Dominant and highly profitable (FY2026 revenue $215.9B with ~42,000 employees). Its moat is a closed software platform (CUDA) on top of its hardware; opening the kernel modules and releasing open models with data strengthens the ecosystem around its chips without opening that moat. Single-vendor control of CUDA is the main structural risk for its customers, not for NVIDIA.

**Physical-science relevance.** CUDA underpins most GPU-accelerated simulation, molecular dynamics and scientific ML; Earth-2 (open-sourced weather/climate platform, Jan 2026) and Slurm (the standard HPC scheduler) tie NVIDIA directly to physical-science computing.

### OpenAI (OpenAI Foundation; OpenAI Group PBC)

founded 2015 · US · origin: nonprofit · openness 3→1 · private_vc · $852.00B (private_valuation, 2026-04) · revenue $13.10B (2025) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 11 yrs · 2 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** OpenAI's founding openness attracted talent and legitimacy, but the decisive commercial step was closing the frontier: from GPT-3 onward API-only access underpinned the ChatGPT business ($13.1B revenue in 2025) and a valuation climb from $157B (Oct 2024) to $852B (2026). Competitive pressure from open models (DeepSeek, Jan 2025) pushed it back to a limited open-weights release (gpt-oss, Aug 2025) without opening its frontier. Its restructuring into a PBC drew litigation that failed but kept the openness-versus-mission question public.

**Takeaway.** OpenAI is the archetype of an organisation founded on openness that became closed as its models became valuable. Its name and 2015 pledge to publish code now sit beside an $852B closed-model business, and its one major re-opening (gpt-oss under Apache-2.0) was a response to open-weights competitors rather than a return to its founding model. For the study, it is the clearest example of openness used to launch, then traded for control once capital needs rose, with governance (a non-profit holding special voting rights) kept as the remaining link to the original mission.

**Timeline.** 2019: Default open publication of research and code → Staged/withheld release of GPT-2; later models API-only (closing); 2025: No open-weights LLM since GPT-2 → gpt-oss-120b and gpt-oss-20b under Apache-2.0 (opening); 2025: Non-profit-controlled capped-profit LLC → OpenAI Foundation (26% stake, ~$130B, plus warrant) controlling OpenAI Group PBC; Microsoft ~27% (closing)

**Risk events.** 2025: net loss of about $9B on $13.1B revenue (estimate in Wikipedia infobox); Musk v. Altman: Musk sued over the departure from the non-profit mission, amended in April 2026 to seek reversal of the restructuring, and lost in May 2026 (jury found he sued after the three-year statute of limitations); 2026-06-08: confirmed an IPO filing with the SEC; in September Altman said the IPO was delayed until 2027; Ownership split after recapitalisation: the Foundation's stake is 26% (infobox gives 22.5% after later rounds), so the non-profit's control rests on special voting rights rather than majority equity

**Robustness.** Very well funded ($852B post-money valuation in early 2026; $122B committed in its latest round) but loss-making, and dependent on continued capital and compute deals. The non-profit Foundation controls the PBC board through special voting rights. Its open contributions are few but durable: GPT-2 and gpt-oss weights under permissive terms cannot be withdrawn.

**Physical-science relevance.** OpenAI for Science ('Accelerating scientific discovery') targets mathematics, physics, biology and chemistry; gpt-oss models can be run locally in labs under Apache-2.0.

### Oracle Corporation

founded 1977 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→1 · public · $354.42B (market_cap, 2026-11-23) · revenue $67.36B (2026) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 49 yrs · 2 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** Oracle's record is the textbook case of an acquirer reducing the openness it bought. Closing OpenSolaris and asserting control over Hudson and OpenOffice.org drove developers to forks that kept the open versions alive outside Oracle. Its attempt to enforce copyright on the Java APIs failed at the Supreme Court (2021), which protected API reimplementation, a result that matters for open compatible clones generally. The business stayed proprietary and profitable, but Oracle has no open AI model strategy and gained no community goodwill from the Sun assets.

**Takeaway.** Oracle shows what happens when a proprietary-minded owner acquires open-source assets: the communities fork and the lasting value walks out (MariaDB, LibreOffice, Jenkins, illumos), while the owner keeps the trademarks and a smaller share of the ecosystem. Its AI strategy is pure infrastructure for closed labs, which has made it a leveraged bet on OpenAI rather than a participant in open AI. For the study, Oracle is the clearest closing case among the incumbents, and the forks are the evidence that open licences protect users against a hostile steward.

**Timeline.** 2010: OpenSolaris (mostly CDDL, OSI-approved; some proprietary parts) → Proprietary Oracle Solaris; in-house development, source only after binary releases (closing); fork: illumos (2010); OpenIndiana (launched 2010-09-14); 2011: Hudson (Sun/Oracle-hosted CI server) → Community renamed the project Jenkins (vote 2011-01-29); Oracle kept Hudson (closing); fork: Jenkins; 2011: OpenOffice.org under Oracle → Apache OpenOffice under the Apache Software Foundation (opening); fork: LibreOffice (The Document Foundation); 1986: IPO, NASDAQ (now NYSE: ORCL)

**Risk events.** 2009-2011: community forks after the Sun deal: MariaDB (MySQL), LibreOffice (OpenOffice.org), Jenkins (Hudson), illumos/OpenIndiana (OpenSolaris); Java creator James Gosling resigned April 2010; 2010-08-13: Oracle sued Google over Java APIs; the Supreme Court ruled 6-2 on 2021-04-05 that Google's copying 'constituted a fair use'; 2025-01: Stargate joint venture with OpenAI, SoftBank and MGX ($500B planned over four years); 2025-09: $300B five-year, 4.5 GW contract with OpenAI, concentrating risk in one AI customer; Market cap down 44.7% over the year to 2026-11-23 (StockAnalysis), with large daily swings (CompaniesMarketCap end-of-day ~$306-315B just before)

**Robustness.** Profitable incumbent (FY2026 revenue $67.36B, up 17%; ~141,000 employees), but its 2025-2026 growth story is tied to AI-infrastructure contracts with OpenAI, and the stock fell ~45% in a year. The open projects it inherited proved robust because communities could fork them: MariaDB, LibreOffice, Jenkins and illumos all outlived or rivalled Oracle's own stewardship.

**Physical-science relevance.** Java and MySQL are widespread in laboratory information systems and research data management.

### Stability AI Ltd

founded 2019 · GB · origin: founder_startup · openness 1→1 · private_vc · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 7 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Openness made Stable Diffusion the most widely adopted image model and Stability briefly a $1B unicorn (Oct 2022), but freely downloadable weights generated little revenue while training costs and copyright lawsuits mounted. The company moved to a revenue-threshold licence and a strategic-investor model; meanwhile its own staff spun out a competitor (Black Forest Labs). Open weights created a huge ecosystem that the company itself could not monetise.

**Takeaway.** Stability AI is the cautionary case: open weights won adoption and mindshare almost overnight, but without a paid product or a deep-pocketed parent, openness turned into cost and legal exposure, followed by a cash crisis, a founder exit and a tighter licence. For the study, it shows that open model releases are sustainable for companies that monetise something else (Meta, NVIDIA, cloud providers) or are funded as public goods (Ai2, EleutherAI), and precarious for startups whose only asset is the model.

**Timeline.** None: CreativeML OpenRAIL-M (free commercial use subject to use restrictions) → Stability AI Community License (paid Enterprise licence above $1M revenue) (closing)

**Risk events.** 2023: financial problems caused employees to leave (Freiburg office), and some went on to found Black Forest Labs in 2024; 2024-03-23: founder Emad Mostaque stepped down as CEO; 2024-06-25: Prem Akkaraju (ex-Weta Digital) became CEO with Sean Parker as executive chairman and a new investor round (reportedly $80M); Litigation: Andersen et al. artists' suit (Jan 2023, ongoing in 2026); co-founder Cyrus Hodes' fraud suit (July 2023, allegations); Getty Images UK case won by Stability (High Court, Nov 2025); Getty US case allowed to proceed (April 2026); Last disclosed valuation was $1B (Oct 2022); valuations of the 2024 and 2026 rounds were not disclosed on the pages fetched

**Robustness.** Fragile: a small company (170 staff in 2024) that survived a 2023-24 cash crisis through a leadership change and new money from strategic media investors (record labels, EA) in 2026. Its earlier open weights remain widely used and cannot be withdrawn, so the ecosystem it seeded (fine-tunes, tools) is more robust than the company.

**Physical-science relevance.** Limited; diffusion-model techniques popularised by Stable Diffusion are now used in scientific generative modelling (molecules, materials), but Stability has no science programme in the pages fetched.

### xAI (X.AI Holdings Corp.; subsidiary of SpaceX since Feb 2026)

founded 2023 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 1→1 · acquired · $250.00B (acquisition_price, 2026-02-02) · funding $17.00B · revenue $3.20B (2025) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 3 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves · latest value ÷ funding raised ≈ 14.7×

**What openness did.** xAI used open-weights releases mainly for signalling, publishing models only after they were superseded, while competing with closed frontier models. The releases had little visible ecosystem effect compared with Llama, DeepSeek or Qwen, and the licence got more restrictive over time. Its value creation came from capital and integration with X and SpaceX, not from openness.

**Takeaway.** xAI is the 'open-washing' contrast case: its founder sued OpenAI over abandoning its non-profit founding mission (Musk v. Altman, lost May 2026; see openai.json), yet xAI is closed at the frontier, releasing older weights under a licence that blocks the most valuable reuse (training other models). Its trajectory from 2023 founding to a $250B acquisition price shows that a frontier lab's market value can be built almost entirely without openness. For the study, it shows why the licence of an open-weights release matters as much as the release itself.

**Timeline.** 2025: Apache-2.0 for released weights (Grok-1) → Restrictive Grok 2 Community License (no training of other models, attribution, AUP guardrails) (closing); 2025: merger $80.00B, X Corp.; 2026: acquisition $250.00B, SpaceX

**Risk events.** 2025: operating loss of $6.4B on $3.2B revenue; Founder-controlled entity folded first into X (2025) and then SpaceX (2026), so its open-release decisions depend on one owner; Open-weights licence tightened between Grok-1 (Apache-2.0) and Grok 2 (no training of other models)

**Robustness.** Financially backed by Musk's companies rather than independent: valuations rose from $24B (May 2024) to $50B (Dec 2024), $80B (X merger, Mar 2025) and $250B at the SpaceX acquisition (Feb 2026), with $3.2B 2025 revenue and a $6.4B operating loss. Grok-1 weights under Apache-2.0 are permanently reusable; Grok 2's licence is not OSI and bars using it to improve other foundation models.

## D. Hardware: open-hardware makers and closed comparators (22)

### Adafruit Industries, LLC

founded 2005 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→3 · private_bootstrapped · funding $0.0M · revenue $45.0M (2016) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 21 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Adafruit pairs fully published designs with a large education and media operation: nearly 3,000 tutorials, and a YouTube channel at 450k subscribers (Jan 2025). It grew without outside capital to $22M revenue in 2013, $33M in 2014 and about $45M in 2016. Openness worked as marketing and community-building: the company captures value through retail, tutorials and brand rather than design exclusivity. No quantified clone losses were found, unlike Arduino.

**Takeaway.** Adafruit is the bootstrapped open-hardware model. It is founder-owned, has taken no funding on record, and has operated for 21 years in New York. It publishes CC BY-SA design files and holds more OSHWA certifications (about 413 by 2020) than any other firm studied here. Its scale is modest: the last disclosed revenue was about $45M in 2016, with about 105 staff. It shows open hardware can be durable and profitable without venture capital, by selling convenience, curation and education around designs anyone may copy. The trade-off is that it stays small and private, with no recorded valuation.

**Risk events.** 2020: kept operating through COVID-19 as an essential manufacturing business (about page).; 2024: moved from Manhattan to Industry City, Brooklyn (42K sq ft, per a reference title on Wikipedia).; Key-person concentration: the company is founder-owned ('100% woman owned'), and no succession arrangement was found.

**Robustness.** The company has operated for 21 years (2005-2026) with no outside investors on record, so there was no exit pressure. It earns money both as a manufacturer of its own open designs and as a retailer and distributor of third-party boards such as the Raspberry Pi. The open designs (CC BY-SA) and MIT-licensed CircuitPython (4.6k stars, 1.4k forks) can survive without the company. Founder ownership is the main concentration risk.

**Physical-science relevance.** Adafruit's sensor breakout boards, Feather microcontrollers and CircuitPython drivers (the community has contributed libraries for 488+ sensors and drivers) are widely used for low-cost lab sensing and data logging. Its tutorials lower the barrier for scientists building their own instruments.

### Arduino (Arduino S.r.l. / Arduino LLC; Qualcomm deal announced 2025)

founded 2005 · IT · origin: academic · openness 3→3 · acquired · funding $54.0M · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 21 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Openness made Arduino the default: about 300,000 official boards by mid-2011, about 700,000 by 2013 and about 30M active IDE users by Feb 2020. The same CC BY-SA designs let clones outsell genuine boards about 5-6 to 1 (2023), which hurts a company whose CEO says revenue comes from selling original hardware. Arduino answered with harder-to-copy, more complex boards and moved into enterprise and cloud, funded by $54M of VC (2022-2023). The asset actually fought over in 2008-2016 was the trademark, not the licence.

**Takeaway.** Arduino is the canonical open-hardware success whose value went mostly to the ecosystem and to clone makers rather than to the company. The path runs from academic origin (2005), through a trademark fight that split the brand (2008-2016) and a founder buyback (2017), to modest venture funding ($54M, 2022-2023). It ends in consolidation into a chip vendor: Qualcomm, announced Oct 2025, price undisclosed, closing not confirmed by a primary source here. The published designs have so far stayed open, but the post-announcement Terms of Service (Oct 2025) tightened control of the cloud platform and user content. Openness persisted in the artifacts while control moved to the trademark, the platform and the parent company.

**Timeline.** 2017: Open designs (CC BY-SA), schematics and code published on the website → Many open-source licences, schematics and code reportedly removed from the website (closing); 2016: merger, Arduino SRL (formerly Smart Projects); 2017: acquisition, BCMI (company of founders Banzi, Cuartielles, Mellis, Igoe); 2025: acquisition, Qualcomm Technologies, Inc.

**Risk events.** 2008-2016: trademark split. Gianluca Martino's Smart Projects secretly registered the Arduino trademark in Italy in late 2008. It stopped paying royalties and renamed itself Arduino SRL in 2014. Arduino LLC sued in Jan 2015 and created the Genuino brand in May 2015. A merger into Arduino AG was announced in Oct 2016.; 2017: CEO Federico Musto admitted he never earned the degrees he claimed (reported by Wired), and open-source files were reportedly pulled from the website. The founders' company BCMI bought out the company in July 2017.; Clones: about 1 genuine board is sold for every 5-6 clones worldwide, at under half the price. India, the largest market for IDE downloads (3.2M), is under 1% of customers (CEO Fabio Violante, TechCrunch 2023-12-22).; 2025-10: the Qualcomm acquisition (price undisclosed) puts a chip vendor in control. UNO Q is the first Arduino board with a Qualcomm chip.; 2025-10-29: the new Terms of Service ban reverse-engineering the Platform, take a perpetual, irrevocable licence over user content and allow monitoring of AI use. Community concerns drew Arduino's 2025-11-21 'setting the record straight' post.

**Robustness.** The board designs (CC BY-SA), IDE (GPL/AGPL) and libraries are open and widely copied, so the hardware and toolchain ecosystem could outlive any owner. The brand, Arduino Cloud, App Lab and enterprise lines are company-controlled and now owned by Qualcomm. The Arduino Foundation announced in 2016 was withdrawn, so no neutral governance body exists. Arduino's Nov 2025 Q&A says multi-vendor microcontroller support (ST, Microchip, Renesas) continues.

**Physical-science relevance.** Arduino is the de facto standard microcontroller platform for low-cost lab instrumentation, sensor readout and DIY automation. An Arduino Uno R3 is part of this lab's CubXL hardware.

### Bambu Lab (Shenzhen Tuozhu Technology)

founded 2020 · CN · origin: founder_startup · openness 1→1 · private_vc · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 6 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Bambu Lab grew from a 2020 founding to market leadership while keeping hardware and firmware closed; its only open asset is an AGPL slicer it inherited from Prusa's open PrusaSlicer. Its success, which Josef Prusa blamed in 2025 for making open desktop hardware unviable, suggests that in this market closed, vertically integrated, venture-backed design and pricing beat openness, with openness benefiting Bambu as a free input rather than a strategy.

**Takeaway.** Bambu Lab is the closed comparator that won: a DJI-alumni, VC-backed (IDG Capital) Shenzhen firm that reused open slicer code, kept printers and firmware proprietary, and in January 2025 tightened control further with authorization firmware, accepting a community backlash. Its rise is the main competitive force behind open pioneers' retreat (Prusa) and shows the 'open software, closed hardware' pattern at its most commercially effective.

**Timeline.** 2025: printers controllable over LAN/cloud by third-party tools via the Studio network plugin → authorization-gated control through Bambu software (Bambu Connect beta), with an optional unsupported LAN Developer Mode (closing)

**Risk events.** 2023-08: Bambu Cloud outage caused some printers to print uncontrollably or stop working; 2024-08: Stratasys sued Bambu Lab for patent infringement; 2025-01-16: authorization-control firmware announced; community backlash over third-party lock-out and terms-of-service changes; optional LAN Developer Mode added 2025-01-20; 2025: security vulnerability found in the printers' cloud connection

**Robustness.** Fast-growing and product-prolific (H2D, H2S, H2C in 2025; X2D, A2L, R1 laser and H3C in 2026) with a first flagship retail store in Shenzhen (Sept 2025), but customers depend on a single vendor's cloud and firmware; the 2023 cloud outage and 2025 vulnerability show that dependence. Patent litigation from Stratasys is an open exposure.

**Physical-science relevance.** Widely adopted fast multi-material printers are now common in labs, but closed firmware and cloud-gated control make them harder to integrate into automated, audited lab workflows than open printers.

### Creality (Shenzhen Creality 3D Technology Co., Ltd.)

founded 2014 · CN · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→2 · public · confidence: low · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 12 yrs · 0 closing / 2 opening moves

**What openness did.** Publishing the Ender-3 under GPL-3.0 in 2018 coincided with it becoming a mass-market, heavily modded printer (about 2k stars and 667 forks on GitHub), and the open ecosystem helped Creality's volume strategy. Creality's openness has been partly reactive: firmware sources for later models appeared after launch amid GPL-compliance criticism, and newer hardware designs stay closed.

**Takeaway.** Creality is the volume-oriented Chinese counterpart to Prusa's story: it began closed and GPL-noncompliant, opened one hit design (Ender-3, 2018) and later its Klipper firmware, and used that openness to fuel a huge modding community while keeping newer hardware proprietary, reaching a Hong Kong listing in 2026. Openness here worked as a marketing and ecosystem tool for a low-cost manufacturer rather than as a core principle.

**Timeline.** 2018: unpublished Marlin-derived firmware and closed designs → Ender-3 published under GPL-3.0 (design and firmware) (opening); 2023: closed K1 firmware at launch (April 2023) → K1 Klipper firmware source published (GPL-3.0) (opening); 2026: IPO, Hong Kong Stock Exchange (HKEX)

**Risk events.** 2018-2022: documented GPL violations involving Marlin firmware and other GPL software; 2022-03-25: Artec 3D sued Creality in the US District Court (E.D.N.Y.) alleging copying of Artec Studio code; described as pending; Competitive pressure from Bambu Lab's closed, integrated printers prompted Creality's K1/K2 lines (2023-2025); 2026: listed on the Hong Kong Stock Exchange (per company milestone page), adding public-market disclosure and pressure

**Robustness.** Very large volume (4.4 million printers shipped 2020-2024 per Wikipedia; the company says more than 6 million shipped to over 140 countries) and a broad, fast-refreshing product line (K2, Hi, SPARKX, K3 in 2025-2026); listed on HKEX in 2026. Openness is mainly at the firmware level, driven partly by GPL obligations and community pressure.

**Physical-science relevance.** The open, GPL Ender-3 is a common low-cost base for lab and teaching modifications (e.g. custom tool heads, bioprinter conversions), helped by its published design and firmware.

### Framework Computer Inc.

founded 2020 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 1→2 · private_vc · funding $45.2M · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 6 yrs · 0 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** Framework uses openness of interfaces and documentation (CC BY 4.0 CAD, open EC firmware, expansion-card reference designs) as a product feature for repairability and a third-party module ecosystem, while keeping core board designs and BIOS closed. It has grown from one laptop to four product lines in five years on modest funding (about $45M disclosed), but openness has not removed the firmware-maintenance burden that drew its main criticism in 2024.

**Takeaway.** Framework is a US example of partial, interface-level openness as a business strategy: publish the module interfaces, CAD and EC firmware so customers and third parties can repair and extend the machine, but keep the mainboard design and BIOS proprietary. It sits at level 2 and shows that 'open where it builds an ecosystem, closed where it protects the core' can sustain a VC-backed hardware startup through 2026, with firmware update cadence as the main weakness.

**Timeline.** 2022: Proprietary EC firmware; only expansion-card developer resources open → EC firmware under BSD-3-Clause; mainboard documentation and CAD published (opening)

**Risk events.** 2021-2022: early AMD shipments delayed by electrical and firmware issues; 2024: Ars Technica reported Framework had fallen short on timely BIOS and firmware updates (company acknowledged; progress noted June 2025); 2025-10: controversy over sponsoring politically polarising software projects; 2026: Laptop 13 Pro shipping slipped from late June to late July over manufacturing issues; 2026-08: notified all customers that names, email and postal addresses and phone numbers were exposed through a zero-day attack on its business-intelligence provider Metabase; payment data not included; 2026-11: memory and storage price increases after using up earlier, lower-cost DDR5 inventory

**Robustness.** Relies on contract manufacturing and closed UEFI firmware from Insyde, so long-term support depends on the company, but published CAD, pinouts and the open EC firmware let owners and third parties repair, reuse and extend hardware independently. Small VC base (about $45M disclosed) for a multi-product hardware maker.

**Physical-science relevance.** Repairable, documented mainboards can be reused as embedded lab computers (Framework publishes printable cases for bare mainboards); open EC firmware and Linux-first support (Laptop 13 Pro shipped Ubuntu-first in 2026) suit research users.

### LulzBot (Aleph Objects, Inc.; since 2019 FAME 3D LLC)

founded 2011 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→3 · acquired · confidence: low · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 15 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Maximal openness gave LulzBot strong reviews and a loyal following but did not protect it from cash-flow failure in 2019, when it laid off about 80% of staff amid a desktop-printer market being undercut on price. Openness did make the assets easy to transfer: FAME 3D bought the brand and continued the open line, so the product outlived the company.

**Takeaway.** LulzBot is the purest test of fully free hardware as a business: it reached Inc. 5000-scale revenue but collapsed in 2019 for ordinary cash-flow reasons, and its open designs then let a regional manufacturer (FAME 3D) revive the line, which still publishes GPL source for current printers in 2026. Openness neither caused nor prevented the failure; it made the product line resilient to the company's death.

**Timeline.** 2019: acquisition, Fargo Additive Manufacturing Equipment 3D, LLC (FAME 3D)

**Risk events.** 2019-10: cash-flow problems; Aleph Objects laid off 91 of its 113 employees; 2019-11: assets and LulzBot brand sold to FAME 3D; operations moved from Loveland, CO to Fargo, ND

**Robustness.** The brand survived its original company's collapse because the designs, firmware and documentation were openly published and could be carried on by a buyer; under FAME 3D it still ships current printers (TAZ 8, Workhorse 2, Mini 3, SideKick, TAZ Pro) and maintains public source repositories. Scale is small and financials are private.

**Physical-science relevance.** Fully documented, freely licensed printers (with FSF certification) suit labs that need auditable, modifiable equipment and long-term repairability.

### MakerBot Industries, LLC

founded 2009 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→0 · acquired · $403.0M (acquisition_price, 2013-06) · funding $13.0M · confidence: medium · tool: WebSearch

*Computed:* age 17 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves · latest value ÷ funding raised ≈ 31.0×

**What openness did.** Open RepRap-derived designs and Thingiverse gave MakerBot a large community and rapid sales (22k printers by 2013), turning about $13M of venture money into a $403M stock exit four years after founding. Closing in 2012 alienated RepRap developers, and it did not protect the business: after the acquisition the closed 5th-generation line and Smart Extruder failures led to layoffs, a lawsuit and Stratasys writing off all MakerBot goodwill by the end of 2015.

**Takeaway.** MakerBot is the canonical 'open, then closed, then absorbed' arc: openness and community created the brand and the exit value, and closing (just before the Stratasys deal) traded community goodwill for proprietary control that did not prevent rapid value destruction. Within seven years of the $403M deal it was folded into Ultimaker as a minority-owned brand.

**Timeline.** 2012: open-source RepRap-derived designs (Cupcake, Thing-O-Matic) → closed hardware and software (Replicator 2, MakerWare) (closing); 2013: acquisition $403.0M, Stratasys Ltd.; 2022: merger, Ultimaker B.V. (NPM Capital)

**Risk events.** 2012: Replicator 2 closed; RepRap community backlash and 'Occupy Thingiverse'; 2012: TangiBot Kickstarter clone of the open Replicator cited as a trigger for closing; 2015: Smart Extruder clogging problems; two rounds of layoffs and declining revenue (Fortune); 2015-07: securities class action against Stratasys over MakerBot disclosures (dismissed with prejudice by July 2016); 2015: Stratasys goodwill impairment of $150.4M on MakerBot in Q1; no MakerBot goodwill left by 2015-12-31; 2016-04: MakerBot laid off its manufacturing workforce and outsourced printer production to China

**Robustness.** No longer independent: a brand inside privately held UltiMaker (NPM Capital majority, Stratasys minority). Closed products depend on UltiMaker for software and cloud support (published end-of-support dates). Thingiverse, the community asset it built while open, is the most durable legacy.

**Physical-science relevance.** Early open MakerBots and Thingiverse helped spread desktop 3D printing into university labs and makerspaces; the current brand targets K-12 education (MakerBot Nebula learning platform).

### Open Compute Project Foundation

founded 2011 · US · origin: corporate · openness 4→4 · foundation · confidence: medium · tool: WebSearch + WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 15 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Publishing Facebook's in-house designs created a multi-vendor market: OCP hardware sales were reported above $1.2B in 2017, Omdia estimated $16.1B in 2020 and $21.7B in 2021, and an OCP-commissioned IDC study (Oct 2026) put spending on OCP-recognized equipment at about $154B in 2025 heading toward $892B by 2030. Openness here served buyers: hyperscalers shared designs to commoditise suppliers and cut costs (Facebook claimed 24% lower build cost and 38% better energy efficiency at Prineville). The open licences did not stop trade-secret litigation from a third party (BladeRoom, 2015-2018).

**Takeaway.** OCP is the buyer-led model of open hardware: the largest customers publish designs so that many manufacturers compete to build them, and a foundation keeps the specs neutral. It shows open hardware can reach a market measured in hundreds of billions of dollars of spending when demand is concentrated in a few giant buyers, while the value accrues to those buyers and to vendors rather than to any open-hardware company. Market figures are forecasts commissioned by OCP itself and measure spending on 'OCP-recognized' equipment, not OCP revenue.

**Risk events.** 2015-2018: BladeRoom Group sued Facebook and Emerson alleging its prefabricated data-centre trade secrets were disclosed through OCP; settled confidentially mid-trial in April 2018; Governance concentration: the 2026 board is seven large-company seats plus one individual, with Meta in the chair; 2024-2026: AI rack power densities (92 kW+ High Power Rack, Open Rack V3 power updates) push specs to evolve quickly, raising the cost of keeping open specs current

**Robustness.** High. A member-funded nonprofit with 400+ member companies (Feb 2025) and many independent suppliers building to shared specs, so no single vendor's failure ends the designs; but agenda-setting depends on a handful of hyperscalers (Meta, Microsoft, Google) whose needs drive which specs are contributed.

**Physical-science relevance.** OCP racks, servers and accelerator modules are the hardware substrate for hyperscale and AI computing, which increasingly hosts scientific simulation and ML workloads; open rack and power specifications let research computing centres buy commodity, multi-vendor gear.

### OpenBCI

founded 2013 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→2 · confidence: low · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 13 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Kickstarter funding, open designs and OSHWA certification built OpenBCI a research and maker community that publishes peer-reviewed work on its hardware. The company then moved up-market with Galea, which won CES 2023 Innovation Awards and appears not to be open. That suggests openness served as the entry and community strategy while the higher-value product is kept closed, like the Prusa and MakerBot pattern but without an acquisition. No financial data were found to measure the effect.

**Takeaway.** OpenBCI is the open-science hardware archetype: a crowdfunded (2013) Brooklyn startup whose open EEG boards and GPL-licensed 3D-printable headset made research-grade biosensing affordable. It has stayed small and private, and its recent focus is a premium, apparently closed multimodal headset (Galea) for XR and defence-adjacent markets. Openness created the research user base. Whether it also funds the company could not be determined, because no funding or revenue figures were reachable without search.

**Timeline.** 2020: Open hardware product line (Cyton/Ganglion/Ultracortex design files public; OSHWA-certified) → Galea premium headset with no public design files found (closing)

**Risk events.** Ganglion price rose from $99 (Kickstarter) to $249.99 and then to $499.00 (Wikipedia).; The 8-bit board (ATmega328P) was deprecated.; Wikipedia flags the OpenBCI article for notability, noting that its claims lack independent sources.; 2020-2024: the company moved up-market to Galea, a premium multimodal headset partly aimed at XR and defence (Unity Aerospace & Defense award). Beta pre-orders opened Aug 2024.

**Robustness.** OpenBCI is a small private company. The older boards and headset are openly licensed and OSHWA-certified: Ultracortex is GPL-3.0 (412 stars, 150 forks) and the GUI is MIT (986 stars, 330 forks), so research labs can keep replicating and maintaining them without the company. The newer Galea line appears to depend on the company. Funding and finances are not public. Activity continued into 2025 (Cyton library updated 2025-07-28).

**Physical-science relevance.** OpenBCI hardware is low-cost research-grade EEG/EMG used in neuroscience and BCI research. OpenBCI keeps a list of peer-reviewed publications that used its products (docs.openbci.com links to openbci.com/citations).

### Oxide Computer Company

founded 2019 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 2→2 · private_vc · funding $834.0M · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 7 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Oxide treats open firmware and software as a transparency and trust feature: its Series B post says prospective customers often examined the source code itself, and the founders credit open firmware and OCP for making the company feasible. Openness did not extend to the hardware designs, which remain the proprietary product. Funding grew from $89M before mid-2025 to about $834M by October 2026, so open firmware has not hindered fundraising.

**Takeaway.** Oxide is the 'open firmware, closed boards' pattern at rack scale: everything from the service-processor OS to the control plane is MPL-2.0 on GitHub, but revenue comes from selling proprietary integrated racks. It is a US example of a long, capital-heavy hardware build-out (four years to first product) that by 2026 reported taxable profit and backlog-driven fundraising, and it builds directly on the Open Compute Project's open rack ideas.

**Risk events.** 2019-2023: about four years of development before first product; $89M raised before the Series B; 2025-2026: demand exceeding supply and a large order backlog required large inventory financing (Series C $200M, Series D $445M plus debt facilities); Hardware designs and supply chain are proprietary and single-vendor, so customers depend on Oxide's survival for hardware even though the software is open; 2026: reported reaching profitability earlier in the year and scaling manufacturing capacity 20x in 12 months, with demand still exceeding supply (Series D release, 2026-10-09); 2026-11: Micron invested through Micron Ventures (amount undisclosed) as Micron memory and storage ship in Oxide's second-generation rack

**Robustness.** Software and firmware are public under MPL-2.0, so customers could in principle maintain the stack, but the hardware is proprietary and made only by Oxide. Financially stronger in 2026: it reported paying income tax for the first time on operating profit and raised $645M in disclosed rounds in 2026 from existing investors plus Atreides Management and AMD Ventures, followed by an undisclosed Micron Ventures investment in November 2026.

**Physical-science relevance.** Sells on-premises cloud racks to government and national-lab customers (Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory announcement, Nov 2024; Carahsoft public-sector partnership, Sep 2026); relevant to research computing that cannot use public cloud.

### Pine64 (Pine Store Limited)

founded 2015 · HK · origin: founder_startup · openness None→2 · funding $1.7M · confidence: low · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 11 yrs · 0 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** Pine64 outsourced most software development to an open-source community (17 OS releases for the PinePhone per Wikipedia), which let a small company sell Linux phones, laptops and watches it could not have supported alone. Its hardware openness is partial (schematic PDFs, no layouts, no open licence), and the dependence on volunteers is also its main weakness. It remains operating and launching products in 2026 on Kickstarter-scale initial funding. It discontinued the PinePhone Pro in August 2025 for weak sales and in July 2026 ruled out a PinePhone 2 for now, citing financial risk.

**Takeaway.** Pine64 is the 'community supplies the software, company supplies cheap hardware' model: openness lives mostly in community-maintained software, while hardware is documented (schematics, now 3D files) but not openly licensed. Founded in California and moved to Hong Kong in 2019-2020, it has survived a decade on crowdfunding and sales rather than VC, at small scale.

**Timeline.** 2026: Schematic PDFs only → Schematics plus full 3D (mechanical) files for PinePhone and PinePhone Pro (opening)

**Risk events.** 2016: Pine A64 Kickstarter deliveries overshadowed by delays and shipping problems; 2019-2020: corporate base moved from Fremont, California (Pine Microsystems Inc., dissolved Jan 2020) to Hong Kong (Pine Store Ltd); orders governed by Malaysian law as of late 2020; Ongoing: software support depends on volunteer community projects rather than the company; 2026: PineTime revision needed to replace an end-of-life display part; 2025-08: PinePhone Pro discontinued because it 'didn't sell well enough to keep production going'; spare parts promised for up to two years, original PinePhone to stay in production about two more years; 2026-05: disclosed a Desktop PinePower V2 bug where some USB-C ports can report 0V with a device attached

**Robustness.** Hardware is made and sold only by Pine64, but most software is community-maintained open source that would outlive the company, and published schematics help repair and porting. Small, low-margin business with no disclosed outside investment; still shipping new products in 2026 (PineVoice speaker, PineTime Pro samples).

**Physical-science relevance.** Low-cost ARM and RISC-V boards (Star64, Ox64) and the open-firmware Pinecil soldering iron are usable in lab automation, teaching and instrument prototyping.

### Prusa Research a.s.

founded 2012 · CZ · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→2 · private_bootstrapped · revenue $157.0M (2024) · confidence: medium · tool: WebSearch + WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 14 yrs · 1 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** Open GPL designs and the RepRap community gave Prusa fast, cheap brand growth from a one-person start-up to roughly CZK 3.65B revenue (2024) with no disclosed outside capital. From 2023 the company progressively withheld hardware files, citing 1:1 clones and Chinese patent filings, yet Hackaday notes the feared Prusa clones are hard to find; the bigger competitive threat became closed, subsidised Chinese printers (Bambu Lab) that reused Prusa's open slicer. Closing did not prevent slowing sales and the first layoffs in 2026, suggesting price competition rather than cloning was the binding constraint.

**Takeaway.** Prusa is the clearest case of an open-hardware pioneer retreating to 'open software, source-available hardware': it kept firmware (GPL) and slicer (AGPL) open but moved its newest printers' CAD to a custom non-commercial Open Community License. Openness built the brand and community cheaply, but it did not protect against well-funded closed competitors, and the protective licence came after market share had already shifted.

**Timeline.** 2023: GPL design files (i3 up to MK3) → STL-only printable parts, withheld electronics and Nextruder design files (MK4) (closing); 2025: withheld CORE One CAD → Open Community License (non-commercial-sale, source-available) (opening)

**Risk events.** 2023-03: Prusa blog cites 1:1 commercial clones, stripped copyright headers and local patents filed on open-source work; 2023: MK4 launched with design files withheld (STL only); PCB layouts withheld to avoid clone boards; 2024-11: CORE One announced as closed hardware; Hackaday: 'Prusa's open source hardware dream quietly dies'; 2025-08: Josef Prusa post 'open hardware desktop 3D printing is dead' blames below-cost Chinese printers and state-reimbursed Chinese patent filings; 2026-03: first-ever layoffs, about 70 non-production roles of about 1,144, as sales targets were missed; Competitive pressure from closed Bambu Lab, whose Bambu Studio slicer derives from Prusa's open PrusaSlicer

**Robustness.** Founder-controlled and bootstrapped (no disclosed outside funding), profitable but thin 2024 net margin (CZK 47.3M net on CZK 3.65B revenue); vertically integrated (Prusament filament, Printables community site) with a Delaware factory (Prusa USA, formerly Printed Solid, renamed July 2026) serving a market that is about 35% of revenue. Its open software (PrusaSlicer) is widely forked, so that part would outlive the company.

**Physical-science relevance.** RepRap-lineage open FFF printers are a standard tool for fabricating custom labware and open scientific hardware; GPL-era i3 designs remain reusable by labs, while OCL designs allow lab use and spare parts but not commercial resale.

### Raspberry Pi Holdings plc (and the Raspberry Pi Foundation)

founded 2012 · GB · origin: nonprofit · openness 1→2 · public · $1.19B (market_cap, 2026-11-23) · revenue $323.2M (2025) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 14 yrs · 0 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** Raspberry Pi shows that closed silicon and closed boards on top of an open software stack can scale: over 75M units by March 2026 and $323.2M revenue in 2025. Industrial buyers, who made up 70% of 2024 sales, are buying supply continuity rather than design openness. The step-by-step opening of the graphics stack (2012-2014), the BSD-licensed Pico SDK and the Apache-2.0 Hazard3 core grew the developer ecosystem without giving away the board designs. The sources read document no exact-copy clone problem of the kind Arduino reports.

**Takeaway.** Raspberry Pi is the clearest success of 'open software, closed hardware' in this group. A charity founded it, it went public in 2024 at about $689M, and it was worth about $1.19B on 2026-11-23, profitable on hardware sales alone. Openness sits in the OS, drivers and microcontroller SDK. The SoC, boot firmware and board designs stay proprietary and single-sourced from Broadcom and Sony, which protects margins but leaves the platform dependent on one vendor. Foundation ownership locks in the mission but is being diluted by share sales. For the study it is the counterexample to the idea that the leading maker-hardware firms are open hardware: one of the most valuable firms in the space is not open hardware at all.

**Timeline.** 2014: Closed VideoCore IV graphics stack (binary userland until Oct 2012) → BSD-3-Clause graphics stack plus documentation (opening); 2024: IPO $689.0M, London Stock Exchange (ticker RPI)

**Risk events.** 2024: the IPO cut the Foundation's stake. It held 77.31% of Raspberry Pi Holdings before listing (prospectus) and 49.08% in the months after (Wikipedia text; the infobox says 41%).; 2026-04: the Foundation agreed to sell 10.9M more shares (Halifax, cited by Wikipedia).; 2026: an AI-driven global memory-chip shortage raised component costs. The company used debt facilities for strategic inventory and warned that first-half unit economics would moderate (Kalkine, 2026-11-02).; 2026: share-price volatility. The 52-week range was 253.8-1,082p, with a record in June 2026, and the price was 469.4p on 2026-11-23.; Single-source dependencies: SoCs come from Broadcom and most manufacturing is outsourced to Sony.; The proprietary boot firmware means the platform cannot be fully rebuilt from source.

**Robustness.** The company is listed and profitable (FY2025 net income $21.7M). The Foundation is still the largest holder, so the education mission keeps an anchor, but it is selling down. The installed base is very large: over 75M boards by March 2026, and 70% of 2024 sales went to industrial customers. The silicon is a single-vendor dependency: the closed Broadcom SoC and proprietary firmware mean no second source can rebuild the boards, and the open software stack is what keeps users' work portable.

**Physical-science relevance.** Pis are a standard low-cost controller for lab automation, data logging and camera streaming. This lab's CubXL controller is a Pi 5. Pico/RP2040 boards are common in DIY instruments, and the Pico SDK is permissively licensed.

### RepRap project

founded 2005 · GB · origin: academic · openness 4→4 · nonprofit · confidence: low · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 21 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Releasing everything under the GPL with community governance made RepRap, by reprap.org's own account (citing a 2013 survey), the origin of the great majority of 3D printers made in the world, including the founding designs of MakerBot, Prusa Research and Ultimaker. Openness maximised diffusion but captured little value for the project itself: its one commercial arm, RepRapPro, closed in 2016, and the value accrued to companies built on its designs.

**Takeaway.** RepRap is the open-hardware 'commons' baseline: an academic, grant-funded, GPL project with community governance that created an industry (MakerBot, Prusa, Ultimaker, LulzBot all descend from it) while capturing almost none of the money. It shows the time pattern clearly: enormous early diffusion, then commercial descendants closing up as markets matured, with the commons surviving as knowledge rather than as an organization.

**Risk events.** 2012: MakerBot, the best-known RepRap spin-off, closed its Replicator 2 designs; 2016-01-15: RepRapPro Ltd (RepRap Professional, the UK commercial arm) ceased trading, citing market congestion; RepRapPro China continued; 2023-2025: commercial descendants (Prusa) retreat from open hardware citing clones and Chinese patents

**Robustness.** No single owner or company to fail: GPL designs and a community wiki mean the knowledge persists regardless of any firm. Activity has shifted to descendant community projects (Wikipedia describes Voron 2.4, 2020, as a resurgence of the RepRap approach) and to firmware projects such as Marlin and Klipper. The reprap.org main page was last edited 2025-10-15.

**Physical-science relevance.** Seeded low-cost desktop fabrication in research labs; reprap.org cites a study finding a RepRap an economically attractive investment for the average US household, and the open-labware movement builds on RepRap-class printers.

### RISC-V International (formerly RISC-V Foundation)

founded 2015 · CH · origin: academic · openness 4→4 · foundation · revenue $8.6M (2024) · confidence: high · tool: WebSearch + WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 11 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Opening the ISA under a neutral foundation turned a Berkeley teaching and research project into a standard with roughly 4,100-4,500 member organisations by 2024 and commercial backers from SiFive to Nvidia. Neutral governance mattered enough that members moved the body to Switzerland in 2020 to protect openness from US trade policy. Openness also made RISC-V the target of US export-control debates (2023-2024) precisely because Chinese firms can adopt it freely.

**Takeaway.** RISC-V International shows the 'open standard, closed products' division of labour in hardware: a lean nonprofit (about $8.6M revenue in 2024) maintains a fully open, royalty-free specification while companies compete with proprietary implementations on top. It is the most robust governance form in this group, and its main risks are geopolitical rather than commercial.

**Risk events.** 2019-2020: moved incorporation from Delaware to Switzerland over fears of US trade restrictions on an open standard; 2023-11: eighteen US lawmakers urged export controls on RISC-V technology to limit Chinese use; 2024-04: US Commerce Department said it was reviewing risks of China's RISC-V use; no formal restriction on the standard found in these searches; 2026: approved as an ISO/IEC JTC1 PAS submitter, opening a route to an ISO standard (not yet ratified per searches)

**Robustness.** High. A member-governed nonprofit owns the standard, specifications are CC BY 4.0, there are thousands of member organisations, and many competing implementers (SiFive, Andes, Alibaba T-Head, Nvidia, Western Digital, Google, and open cores such as Rocket and CVA6), so no single company's failure endangers the ISA. Main exposure is geopolitical: export-control pressure and fragmentation through proprietary extensions.

**Physical-science relevance.** A royalty-free ISA lets universities and labs tape out custom processors and instrument controllers without licence fees; open cores are widely used in research FPGAs and accelerators.
