### Seeed Technology Co., Ltd. (Seeed Studio)

founded 2008 · CN · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→3 · confidence: low · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 18 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Seeed built a Shenzhen business around open hardware and maker infrastructure and sells mostly overseas (about 95% of customers). It publishes schematics and KiCad projects, takes no patents, and relies on manufacturing scale as its advantage. Its openness is more pragmatic than that of its US peers: design files are often posted without an explicit licence, and it has no OSHWA certifications in the 2016-2020 list. The newest product (reCamera Pro) has its sources withheld at launch. With no financials found, the effect of openness on its growth can't be quantified.

**Takeaway.** Seeed is the Shenzhen counterpart to Adafruit and SparkFun: an open-hardware-first company that also sells manufacturing services and runs maker community infrastructure. It has lasted 18 years. Its competitive edge comes from Shenzhen supply-chain scale rather than design exclusivity or patents, so open design files cost it little. Its openness is real but informal: files are published, licences are often missing, and it holds no OSHWA certifications. The pages reachable without search gave no funding, revenue or valuation figures, so its size relative to its US peers is unknown here.

**Risk events.** Export dependence: about 95% of customers are overseas (Nanfang Daily, quoted on the about page), which exposes Seeed to trade policy. No specific tariff event was verified.; No patents on original technology (Nippon, quoted on the about page), so the designs can be freely copied and improved by competitors.; Partial openness on new products: the reCamera Pro's editable PCB and mechanical sources are withheld until an approved hardware release.

**Robustness.** Seeed has operated for 18 years (2008-2026) and was still publishing product news in November 2026. It earns money from its own modules and boards and from manufacturing services (Seeed Fusion PCB/PCBA). It also anchors community infrastructure: Chaihuo Makerspace and Maker Faire Shenzhen, which has had over 500,000 cumulative participants. It maintains 513 public GitHub repositories. Its main advantage is Shenzhen manufacturing and supply-chain scale rather than IP.

**Physical-science relevance.** Its low-cost sensor modules, XIAO microcontrollers and edge-AI cameras (reCamera, with KiCad sources) are used for environmental monitoring and lab prototyping. The published KiCad projects let researchers adapt the boards.

### SiFive, Inc.

founded 2015 · US · origin: academic · openness 3→2 · private_vc · $3.65B (private_valuation, 2026-04) · funding $766.0M · revenue $38.2M (2023) · confidence: high · tool: WebSearch + WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 11 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves · latest value ÷ funding raised ≈ 4.8×

**What openness did.** Building on an open, royalty-free ISA let three academics found a processor-IP company that drew a reported $2B Intel approach within six years and a $3.65B valuation by 2026. Openness of the ISA lowered barriers for SiFive and for its competitors alike, so SiFive moved quickly from open RTL to proprietary cores to capture value. Revenue is still small relative to valuation ($38M in 2023), suggesting value rests on the expected growth of the RISC-V ecosystem rather than on SiFive's own open designs.

**Takeaway.** SiFive is the clearest example of open-standard, closed-implementation hardware: the open RISC-V ISA created the market, while SiFive's own open-source platform was archived once it had served as a launch vehicle. It shows that an open specification can support a multi-billion-dollar US company, but the money comes from proprietary IP, long timelines and repeated large VC rounds, not from selling open designs.

**Timeline.** 2021: Open-source Freedom SoC platform (Apache-2.0), actively maintained → Freedom repository archived; product line consists of proprietary licensed cores (closing)

**Risk events.** 2021: Intel reportedly offered more than $2B to acquire SiFive (Bloomberg, anonymous source); talks collapsed and SiFive stayed independent; 2022: sold its OpenFive custom-silicon unit to Alphawave IP for $210M; 2023: laid off about 20% of staff (roughly 130-140 people) and refocused product lines; 2023: revenue of $38.2M against a $113.1M operating loss (Bloomberg, from documents it reviewed); 2026: raised $400M Series G, described by the CEO as its final private round before an IPO

**Robustness.** The ISA it depends on is governed by RISC-V International rather than SiFive, so customers can switch to other RISC-V core vendors or open cores; SiFive's moat is implementation quality, not control of the standard. Heavily VC-funded and loss-making as of 2023, but well capitalised after the 2026 round.

**Physical-science relevance.** RISC-V cores appear in research instruments, accelerators and microcontrollers; the open ISA and open cores such as Rocket enable academic chip tape-outs without licence fees.

### Sofar Ocean Technologies (formerly OpenROV)

founded 2011 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→1 · private_vc · funding $8.7M · confidence: low · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 15 yrs · 2 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** Openness gave OpenROV a global builder community (30+ countries) and early backing ($1.3M from True Ventures and National Geographic). Scale came only after it moved to a ready-to-use ROV and then, through the 2019 merger, to a proprietary sensor-network and data business. Sofar then used openness strategically at the interface layer (Bristlemouth, Apache-2.0 firmware, with ONR, NOAA and philanthropic partners) to grow an ecosystem around its closed core.

**Takeaway.** Sofar/OpenROV traces the full arc from open-hardware DIY project to closed, VC-backed data company. It began in 2011 with public CAD under a non-commercial CC licence and a community in 30+ countries. The 2019 merger made it a business of Spotter buoys and data software sold to shipping and the Navy. It closed the product and later opened a standard (Bristlemouth) where interoperability serves it, the 'open the interfaces, close the product' pattern. Its CC BY-NC-SA licence also shows a common open-science compromise that does not meet the OSHW definition. Funding after the 2019 $7M Series A, and any valuation, could not be found without search.

**Timeline.** None: Open DIY kit (CAD, electronics and software published) → Trident ready-to-use ROV with only an interfacing development kit (MIT) published (closing); 2019: OpenROV (open kits plus Trident) → Sofar Ocean (proprietary Spotter sensor network and data products) (closing); 2021: Closed Spotter platform interfaces → Bristlemouth open hardware-interface standard (Apache-2.0 firmware) (opening); 2019: merger, Spoondrift Technologies

**Risk events.** 2019: the merger pivoted the company from open, consumer ROVs to a proprietary ocean-data business. Part of the Series A was earmarked for developing safeguards for Trident drones (TechCrunch).; Customer concentration in government and defence: ONR, US Navy and NOAA appear among its customers and partners (website, 2026).; Open OpenROV design repos have gone unmaintained since 2019-2022 (hardware last updated 2020-02-27; electronics 2022-08-11).

**Robustness.** Sofar is a private, VC-backed company that says it runs 'the largest privately-owned ocean sensor network in the world': 2.5K+ Spotters, 1.5M real-time observations a day and 25M+ hours of observations. Its value sits in proprietary sensors, data and routing software sold to shipping fleets and government. The old OpenROV designs remain public (CC BY-NC-SA) but unmaintained. Bristlemouth spreads interoperability across a multi-partner open standard, though the pages fetched name no governance body.

**Physical-science relevance.** Directly relevant to oceanography. Spotter buoys supply wave, weather and subsurface observations used by NOAA and the US Navy, and National Geographic was one of the biggest Trident buyers. Bristlemouth aims at plug-and-play interoperability for marine scientific instruments.

### SparkFun Electronics

founded 2003 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→3 · confidence: high · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 23 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** SparkFun shows that open hardware plus no patents is compatible with long-term operation. It has run for 23 years with about 140-150 staff (2015-2018) while publishing everything, and it holds the second-largest OSHWA portfolio in this group (about 264 by 2020). Like Adafruit it stayed mid-sized and private. Value comes from in-house manufacturing, curation and education, not exclusivity. Its recorded legal disputes are about trademark and trade dress (SPARC, Fluke), not about copying of its designs.

**Takeaway.** SparkFun is the long-lived, mid-sized open-hardware manufacturer. It was founded by a student in 2003 and still operates from Colorado, publishing schematics and EAGLE files for 400+ in-house designs, with an explicit no-patents policy and an early role in writing the OSHW definition. No valuation, funding or revenue is public, and no recent headcount was found beyond about 140 in 2018. It supports the pattern that open hardware firms can be durable but stay small and private. The trademark and trade-dress disputes (SPARC 2009-2010, Fluke 2014) show that brand, not design IP, is where open-hardware firms meet legal risk.

**Risk events.** 2009-10-16: SPARC International sent a cease-and-desist over the SparkFun name and domain. The two signed a trademark coexistence agreement on 2010-04-02.; 2014-03-07: US Customs seized a shipment of multimeters whose yellow jackets resembled Fluke's trade dress. SparkFun had them destroyed, and Fluke later sent genuine units as goodwill.; 2016: founder Nathan Seidle stepped down as CEO and Glenn Samala took over.; No-patent policy: by design there is no IP barrier to copying the products.

**Robustness.** SparkFun has operated for 23 years (2003-2026) as a privately held company and makes 400+ open designs in-house. Its value lies in manufacturing, curation, documentation and education rather than IP, since it holds no patents. Every design is published and many are OSHWA-certified, so the hardware could outlive the company. Funding history and finances are not public.

**Physical-science relevance.** SparkFun breakout and development boards (sensors, GNSS, motor drivers) are common building blocks in low-cost scientific instruments, and the published schematics allow them to be adapted for lab use.

### System76, Inc.

founded 2005 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 1→2 · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 21 yrs · 0 closing / 2 opening moves

**What openness did.** System76's openness grew over time rather than shrinking: from reselling Linux PCs (2005) to its own OS (2017), open firmware, an in-house desktop with an open board (2018) and an OSHWA-certified keyboard (2021). Openness is its brand differentiator in a niche Linux market, and it has kept the company independent for two decades, but there is no public evidence that it produced large scale; revenue and headcount are not disclosed.

**Takeaway.** System76 is a long-lived US example of an open-software-first hardware company that opened progressively, ending up with open OS, open firmware and some fully open hardware (Launch, OSHWA US001062), while its laptops remain closed ODM designs. Its longevity (since 2005) without disclosed outside funding contrasts with VC-backed peers, though its ownership and finances are not public.

**Timeline.** 2018: Resold/ODM hardware with closed designs → In-house Thelio desktop with open (GPL-3.0) Thelio Io board design (opening); 2021: No fully open-hardware product → Launch keyboard: OSHWA-certified open hardware (US001062) (opening)

**Risk events.** Laptops depend on ODM (Clevo-type) designs, limiting how open the main product line can be; an in-house 'Virgo' laptop was announced in April 2023; 2021-2025: multi-year rewrite of its desktop environment (COSMIC) in Rust; Pop!_OS 24.04 shipped only in December 2025 with COSMIC as default

**Robustness.** Over 20 years of continuous operation as a privately held niche OEM. Its software (Pop!_OS, COSMIC) and firmware are open and could be maintained by others; open-hardware products (Launch, Thelio Io) are fully reproducible, but most laptops rely on closed ODM designs and supply.

**Physical-science relevance.** Linux workstations and open-firmware laptops are common in research computing; Pop!_OS has been positioned for 'developers, makers, and computer science professionals'.

### UltiMaker (Ultimaker B.V.)

founded 2011 · NL · origin: founder_startup · openness 2→1 · private_pe · funding $62.4M · confidence: medium · tool: WebSearch + WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 15 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Ultimaker started from the RepRap/fablab community and published its first two printers' designs, though only under non-commercial CC BY-NC terms. Its most successful open asset is software: making Cura open (now LGPL-3.0) made it a de facto standard slicer with about 1 million users by 2017, which supported printer sales even as later printer designs were not published. Openness in software, not hardware, appears to be what gave UltiMaker durable reach.

**Takeaway.** UltiMaker is the 'open slicer, closed printers' pattern from the European side: its RepRap-community origins and non-commercially licensed early designs gave way to proprietary professional printers, while LGPL Cura became the ecosystem anchor. Consolidation (the 2022 MakerBot merger under NPM Capital) rather than openness determined its corporate path.

**Timeline.** None: published CC BY-NC printer designs (Original, Ultimaker 2) → no published design files for later printers (Ultimaker 3 / S-series / Factor) (closing); 2022: merger, MakerBot (Stratasys)

**Risk events.** 2022: merger with MakerBot under private-equity majority owner NPM Capital; Stratasys minority; Published end-of-support dates for older printers' software (e.g. Replicator+ support ended 2026-01-01; Method X 2027-06-01); 2026-11-14: Ultimaker2 open-design GitHub repository archived (read-only)

**Robustness.** Privately held with deep-pocketed owners (NPM Capital, part of SHV; Stratasys), and pivoting toward industrial, defense (Factor 4 Plus, May 2026; exclusive Americas distributor for Tectonic-3D materials, April 2026) and education (MakerBot). Cura is LGPL-licensed and widely forked, so the most-used part of its ecosystem would survive the company.

**Physical-science relevance.** Cura is one of the most widely used open slicers (1.4 million jobs a week in 2020 per Wikipedia) and is used across academic labs; 2026 activity includes medical (wound-care MOU with Bioactivx) and materials work.

### Atinary Technologies

US · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→0 · private_vc · confidence: low · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Atinary commercialized the self-driving-lab optimization layer as closed, no-code SaaS and trademarked the field's name. It differentiates through usability and integrations with closed instrument makers rather than openness. Its 2026 tie-up with Bruker and Chemspeed points to an emerging closed, vendor-integrated SDL stack. No evidence was found of open releases or of openness affecting its growth.

**Takeaway.** Atinary is the closed 'SDL brain' vendor. Its founders have worked on AI-driven R&D since 2017, coined and trademarked 'Self-Driving Labs', and sell no-code optimization SaaS that plugs into Chemspeed, Bruker, ABB and others. Its 2026 Boston lab and Bruker collaboration show lab-automation incumbents assembling proprietary end-to-end SDL stacks. That contrasts with the open-source optimizers and orchestration tools common in academic SDLs, which Atinary does not contribute to.

**Risk events.** 2026: opened a Self-Driving Labs facility in Boston (Feb-Mar 2026, approx.); Jun 2026: named a World Economic Forum Technology Pioneer; Jul 2026: Gartner Hype Cycle sample vendor; Aug 2026 (approx.): Bruker strategic collaboration integrating SDLabs with Chemspeed automation, Bruker instruments and SciY software; Oct 2026: ISPE RAYA award with ABB Robotics (Physical AI)

**Robustness.** A small private software vendor whose position increasingly rests on integration with large closed hardware ecosystems (Bruker/Chemspeed, ABB, Mettler Toledo, Agilent). That brings distribution but also dependence. Funding and headcount are undisclosed; the team spans 13 nationalities.

**Physical-science relevance.** High. Use cases include perovskite solar cells (MIT Buonassisi lab; Solaronix pilot), catalysis (ETH), process chemistry (Snapdragon Chemistry), materials discovery (SwissCAT+), pharma (Takeda) and flavours and ingredients (dsm-firmenich).

### Benchling, Inc.

founded 2012 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→0 · private_vc · $6.10B (private_valuation, 2021-11) · funding $406.0M · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 14 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves · latest value ÷ funding raised ≈ 15.0×

**What openness did.** Benchling got the network effects open source usually provides by giving its closed product away free to academics (7,500+ institutions), so trained users carried it into industry: free but closed. It reached a $6.1B valuation in 2021 with no open-source component. That shows a closed SaaS can win the academic-to-industry funnel when the free tier is generous.

**Takeaway.** Benchling is the closed-SaaS benchmark for lab software. It is founder-led (MIT/YC 2012), raised about $406M, was valued at $6.1B at the 2021 peak and has about 810 employees. It has never open-sourced its product, and its free academic tier did the community-building job that open source does for others. The lack of any public valuation, IPO or reported down round since 2021 makes its current value unknown. Fetched sources could not confirm 2023-2026 restructuring.

**Risk events.** Rounds: Series A $7M (2016), B $14.5M (2018), C $34.5M (2019), D $50M (2020), E $200M (2021), F $100M (2021) at $6.1B (Bloomberg, 18 Nov 2021); Nov 2021: Wikipedia, citing a Reuters report from anonymous sources, says Benchling filed Form S-1, but SEC EDGAR lists no S-1 or DRS for Benchling, Inc. (CIK 1687403), only a 2016 Form D, so any filing was a confidential draft; no IPO as of 2026; No post-2021 valuation, tender or down round found in fetched sources (WebSearch budget exhausted before this company was searched)

**Robustness.** A large installed base: as of 2025, more than 200,000 scientists, 1,300+ companies and 7,500+ academic institutions (Wikipedia). Lock-in is high because data and workflows live in a proprietary cloud. There is no open-source fallback if the vendor changes terms. It remains a private company with ~810 employees (2026).

**Physical-science relevance.** Low. Benchling is built for biology and biotech R&D. For physical-science SDLs it matters mainly as the dominant closed lab-data layer that automation and AI vendors integrate with through its API, and as the commercial comparator for open ELN and LIMS tools.

### Emerald Cloud Lab, Inc.

founded 2010 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→1 · private_vc · confidence: medium · tool: WebSearch (+ WebFetch of cited pages)

*Computed:* age 16 yrs · 0 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** The closed, vertically integrated model (proprietary language, operators and facility) let ECL sell lab capacity as a subscription, but adoption required scientists to learn a proprietary language. ECL's 2023 'open source for research use' release of SLL was explicitly aimed at lowering that barrier. SLL then became the language of the NSF GEMSTONE testbed (2026), which suggests that partial opening helped win public-infrastructure roles. There is no evidence yet of an outside developer community.

**Takeaway.** Emerald Cloud Lab is the surviving US closed cloud lab. It sells subscription access to a centralized robotic facility and has opened only its programming layer, for research use under an unnamed licence. Its biggest strategic wins, the $40M Carnegie Mellon university cloud lab and the NSF GEMSTONE node, came through academic partners that asked for open standards and data. So the open parts of the ECL ecosystem are increasingly paid for by universities and NSF, while the facility, scheduling software and operations stay proprietary.

**Timeline.** 2023: Proprietary SLL → SLL open source for research use (licence unnamed) (opening)

**Risk events.** Feb 2023: announced relocation of the facility from South San Francisco to a 700%-larger site in Austin, TX (move completed ~Jul 2023); Apr 2023: WARN notice for 30 layoffs at the South San Francisco HQ (reported via LeadIQ); anonymous Glassdoor reviews describe overhiring, two rounds of layoffs and a costly move (unverified); CMU Cloud Lab ($40M, announced Aug 2021 for summer 2022) slipped; reported open in spring 2024 (single source); Customer lock-in to a proprietary language and single centralized facility; public list price for a basic startup plan ~$30,020/month (Oct 2026)

**Robustness.** A single private operator of a closed, centralized facility, with no disclosed valuation or recent funding. Customer protocols are written in ECL's SLL, so lock-in is high. Some redundancy comes from the CMU Cloud Lab, a university-owned second site on ECL's platform with 130+ instrument types and 24/7 operation, and from SLL being released for research use. Anchor public partnerships (CMU, NSF GEMSTONE $20M grant to a Stanford-led team, with ECL as host lab) diversify revenue.

**Physical-science relevance.** High. ECL offers 230+ (2023) to 740+ (2026, per GEMSTONE) programmable instrument types for chemistry and life-science experiments, and the CMU Cloud Lab explicitly serves chemical, life and materials science research. ECL is the starting site of the NSF programmable cloud laboratory testbed (GEMSTONE, kickoff Aug 2026), making it de facto national infrastructure for remote physical experiments even though the platform is closed.

### EvolutionaryScale, PBC

US · origin: corporate · openness 1→2 · acquired · funding $142.0M · confidence: low · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* 0 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** EvolutionaryScale launched with a restrictive, non-commercial 'community' licence designed to protect a VC business (API access to the 98B model). After it joined the non-profit Biohub, the smaller models moved to MIT. The open-weight releases (ESM3-open, ESM C) are what most researchers can actually use; commercial value was meant to come from the closed frontier model. The VC-funded API model did not persist as an independent company.

**Takeaway.** EvolutionaryScale is the counter-example to 'start open, then close'. It began as a Meta FAIR open-science project (ESM), raised a $142M seed as a company with non-commercial open weights, and within about two years was folded into the non-profit Biohub, where its open models moved to MIT. For the study it suggests that frontier scientific foundation models may find a more stable home in philanthropic or non-profit structures than in VC-backed API businesses. Its timeline (relicence date, deal terms) still needs confirming.

**Timeline.** 2026: EvolutionaryScale Community License (non-commercial) → MIT (opening); None: acquisition, Biohub (biohub.org)

**Risk events.** Origin risk: the ESM team was disbanded at Meta (FAIR) before the spin-out; 25 Jun 2024: $142M seed led by Nat Friedman, Daniel Gross and Lux Capital, with Amazon and NVentures; 2025-2026 (date unconfirmed): ceased independent operation and became part of the non-profit Biohub; licences moved to MIT

**Robustness.** As an independent VC-backed company it lasted roughly two years. The science and models now sit inside a philanthropically funded non-profit (Biohub), with weights for smaller models under MIT. That makes the models more robust to any single organization's fate (GitHub repo ~3.0k stars, ~408 forks) than they were under the non-commercial licence.

**Physical-science relevance.** Low to moderate. This is biology (proteins), not physical science. It matters to the study as an AI-for-science lab whose models were trained on public-scale protein sequence data: the homepage cites 2.78 billion natural proteins and 771 billion tokens, but fetched pages do not name the source databases. It started with restrictive weights and opened them after moving to a non-profit.

### Isomorphic Labs Limited

founded 2021 · GB · origin: corporate · openness 0→1 · private_vc · funding $7.70B · confidence: high · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 5 yrs · 0 closing / 2 opening moves

**What openness did.** Isomorphic inherits the brand and science of DeepMind's AlphaFold 2, which open public data (the PDB) and an open database (AlphaFold DB, CC BY 4.0) made famous. It has commercialized it as a closed drug-design company, releasing AF3 only under non-commercial weights terms after delay. Investors evidently do not penalize this: closed IP plus Alphabet compute attracted $7.7B in 2025-2026. Openness here built the reputation; closure is the business.

**Takeaway.** Isomorphic Labs is the clearest case of 'open public data in, closed models out'. AlphaFold was trained on the PDB, a decades-old open archive. Its open AlphaFold 2 and DB won a Nobel and built trust, yet the commercial successor (AF3, IsoDDE) ships with non-commercial weights or not at all. Investor appetite for that closed position is enormous: $600M in 2025, then $2.1B and $5B in 2026, the largest AI-for-science financing in this group, still with no clinical asset.

**Timeline.** 2024: Closed (AF3 paper/server only) → AF3 code and weights for non-commercial use on request (opening); 2026: AF3 code non-commercial → AF3 code Apache-2.0 (weights unchanged) (opening)

**Risk events.** 31 Mar 2025: first external round, $600M led by Thrive Capital; Jan 2026: Max Jaderberg appointed president, succeeding Colin Murdoch; J&J multi-target collaboration; 12 May 2026: $2.1B Series B led by Thrive Capital (Alphabet, GV, MGX, Temasek, CapitalG, UK Sovereign AI Fund); 17 Nov 2026: $5B Series C co-led by Greenoaks and Sequoia, with a16z and Alphabet; Nov 2026: pipeline still preclinical; first-in-human trials pending with no date

**Robustness.** Financially very robust: Alphabet backing, about $7.7B of announced external capital since 2025, and pharma partners (Eli Lilly and Novartis, Jan 2024; Novartis expanded Feb 2025; J&J, Jan 2026; MSD). But the engine and pipeline are entirely proprietary, unvalidated in the clinic, and AF3 weights cannot be used commercially, so no commercial community fork of its core models is possible.

**Physical-science relevance.** Moderate. Structural biology and medicinal chemistry rather than materials: AF3-class models predict protein-ligand, nucleic-acid and ion interactions. For this study it is the archetype of an AI lab built on open public data. Wikipedia says AlphaFold was trained on over 170,000 Protein Data Bank structures, yet Isomorphic keeps its derived models closed or restricted.

### Opentrons Labworks, Inc.

founded 2014 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→2 · private_vc · $170.2M (private_valuation, 2026-10) · funding $206.1M · confidence: medium · tool: WebSearch (+ WebFetch of cited pages)

*Computed:* age 12 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves · latest value ÷ funding raised ≈ 0.8×

**What openness did.** Openness was the founding go-to-market: a $2,000 open-hardware OT-One crowdfunded from the DIYbio/academic community built the brand ('the PC of biotech labs', TechCrunch 2016) and a user-written protocol ecosystem. Opentrons then closed the hardware with the OT-2 (2018) while keeping the software open, which preserved scriptability and SDL integration without handing designs to cloners; no fork of the robot emerged. The 2021 peak ($1.8B) and the 2022-2025 collapse (~$162M) were driven by COVID testing and its end, not by the openness choice.

**Takeaway.** Opentrons is the canonical open-hardware lab-automation test case, and its arc is 'open to enter, close the hardware to scale': the open OT-One won early users cheaply, but revenue came from a closed-hardware OT-2/Flex whose open Apache-2.0 API is the remaining open asset and the reason it dominates academic self-driving-lab builds. Its valuation fell about 91% from the 2021 SoftBank round to the 2025 Series D, a COVID boom-and-bust rather than a verdict on openness. In 2026 it repositioned as 'open, AI-native' infrastructure connecting AI to the wet lab, marketing 'open' as API access rather than open designs.

**Timeline.** 2018: Open hardware files (OT-One, Apache-2.0) + open software → Proprietary hardware (OT-2) + open software (closing)

**Risk events.** 2018: OT-2 launched as closed hardware; open-hardware status lost (software stayed Apache-2.0); Sep 2021: $200M Series C led by SoftBank Vision Fund 2 at ~$1.8B (Bloomberg), up from ~$90M in 2020, on COVID-testing growth; Dec 31 2022: Pandemic Response Lab (subsidiary ReOpen Diagnostics, NYC COVID testing) wound down, all ~185 jobs eliminated (Bloomberg); 2022-2025: headcount drifted from ~276 to ~223 (Revelio Labs); anonymous Glassdoor reviews describe repeated layoffs incl. the PRL division (unverified); Jul 2025 (announced 30 Jan 2026): CEO changed from co-founder Jon Brennan-Badal to James Atwood (ex-PerkinElmer/MOBILion, GM of robotics unit since Apr 2023); 20 Nov 2025: Series D/D-1 (~$20M, BroadOak, SOSV) at ~$162M post-money per Forge, roughly 91% below the 2021 valuation (my arithmetic)

**Robustness.** Single-vendor company with no foundation, but the whole control stack (app, robot server, Python API, labware schema) is Apache-2.0 on GitHub, so protocols and integrations could outlive the firm. Current hardware designs are proprietary, so the community could not build or repair OT-2/Flex robots independently. Large installed base (>11,000 robots in >80 countries, 14 of top-15 biopharmas, every top-20 US research university per the 2026 release) and survival through a ~91% valuation reset suggest a durable niche.

**Physical-science relevance.** Built for life-science liquid handling, but the OT-2 is one of the most common low-cost liquid handlers in academic materials/chemistry automation: e.g. precursor preparation for high-throughput sonochemical synthesis (RSC Digital Discovery 2023), CdSe nanocrystal synthesis paired with the Jubilee platform (MRS 2024), and Turkevich gold-nanoparticle teaching protocols (LAPSE 2026). Limited to pipettable liquids (no powders/solids). The requesting lab (BYU VCL) itself runs an OT-2 in its color-mixing SDL.

### Orbital Industries (formerly Orbital Materials)

GB · openness 2→2 · private_vc · confidence: low · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Orbital is this group's clearest example of an AI-for-materials startup releasing permissive open weights. Its models were trained partly on open public datasets (OMol25), and the releases built scientific credibility and adoption (~630 stars, ~94 forks). Monetization moved to proprietary layers (CurieOS licences, cooling hardware): an open-model, closed-product split. The 2026 pivot to data-centre cooling shows the open models alone were not the business.

**Takeaway.** Orbital (Materials, now Industries) shows that an AI-for-materials startup can stay genuinely open at the model layer: Apache-2.0 Orb potentials trained on open DFT datasets such as OMol25. It still has to find revenue in closed products, here an agentic R&D platform licensed to BASF and PFAS-free GPU cooling for AI data centres. Openness bought reputation and community, not revenue, and the company's rebrand to an 'AI Industrial' signals where it expects the money.

**Risk events.** By Nov 2026: renamed Orbital Industries and repositioned from materials discovery to an 'AI Industrial' building physical products (rename date not found); Aug 2026: launched CurieOS; first product built with it is GPU cooling hardware (Orbital IT), i.e. a pivot toward data-centre infrastructure; 5 Aug 2026: BASF Environmental Catalyst and Metal Solutions licensed CurieOS for automotive-emissions catalyst discovery (terms undisclosed)

**Robustness.** The open Orb models are widely forked and Apache-licensed, so they would outlive the company. The business itself is a VC-backed, single-company proprietary platform that has pivoted toward data-centre hardware. Funding size and runway are unknown.

**Physical-science relevance.** Very high. Its open universal interatomic potentials (Orb, OrbMol) are used for atomistic simulation of crystals, molecules and polymers. It does catalyst discovery for automotive emissions (BASF ECMS) and invented a PFAS-free heat-transfer material for GPU cooling.

### Periodic Labs

founded 2025 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→0 · private_vc · funding $300.0M · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 1 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Closed from day one. The thesis is that the open literature is a poor training signal (it omits negative results), so the moat is proprietary experimental data from its own autonomous labs plus a large model post-trained on that data. The founders came from open-publishing research cultures (GNoME, MatterGen, attention), but none of Periodic's outputs are open. There is no evidence yet on whether this helps or hurts its science.

**Takeaway.** Periodic Labs is the clearest bet that physical-science AI value lies in closed, self-generated lab data. It raised $300M at founding, the largest round in this group for a pure materials-discovery startup, and its only public outputs a year later are blog posts and a claimed 1-trillion-parameter model it has not released. Its founders' previous work at Google and OpenAI built on and fed open materials science, yet Periodic itself returns nothing open. It is the reference point for a 'closed AI-for-materials' model.

**Risk events.** 30 Sep 2025: $300M founding round led by a16z (backers per periodic.com: a16z, Felicis, DST, NVentures, Accel, Jeff Bezos, Elad Gil, Eric Schmidt, Jeff Dean); 15 Sep 2026: introduced Periodic Neon, claiming it beats leading closed frontier models on X-ray diffraction analysis (no benchmark numbers published); No disclosed materials discoveries, revenue or valuation as of Nov 2026

**Robustness.** Very well capitalized for a one-year-old company, with elite AI and materials talent and a scientific advisory board (Bertozzi, Kanatzidis, Kivelson, Shen, Wolverton). But it has a single-company proprietary stack, no product revenue disclosed, and its scientific claims are not independently validated. If it failed, nothing it built would be reusable by others, because nothing is open.

**Physical-science relevance.** Very high. It states it starts 'with the physical sciences': powder-synthesis labs aimed at superconductors, magnets and heat shields. Periodic Neon targets X-ray diffraction analysis, and the company deploys custom agents with industry partners such as a semiconductor manufacturer working on chip heat dissipation. It is the purest materials-science entrant in the 2025-2026 AI-for-science funding wave.

Its funding ($55M seed+) is modest next to Periodic Labs and Lila, but government contracts give it a defense-materials niche.

**Physical-science relevance.** Very high. It runs autonomous alloy and materials discovery (a Latent Space article, Jun 2026, reports 1,200 alloys in six months), works on refractory and aerospace materials, hydrogen-purification materials (Sandia) and semiconductor and energy materials, and does automated microstructure analysis.

### Recursion Pharmaceuticals, Inc.

founded 2013 · US · openness 0→1 · public · $1.66B (market_cap, 2026-11-23) · revenue $74.7M (2025) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 13 yrs · 0 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** Recursion uses openness as a benchmark, community and recruiting tool around a closed core. It has released datasets since 2019 and models later, mostly under non-commercial terms, explicitly representing under 1% of its data. That seeded ML benchmarks and competitions, but its value is the proprietary dataset and pipeline. Openness did not shield it from the 2024-2026 TechBio downturn (merger, 20% layoffs, founder-CEO transition, market cap below its IPO level).

**Takeaway.** Recursion is the public-market test of the 'automated lab plus AI' thesis. After 13 years and the Exscientia merger it has about $75M in revenue, a net loss of roughly $645M (2025) and a $1.66B market cap, below its 2021 IPO level. Its open-science activity (RxRx, OpenPhenom, Boltz-2) is real but deliberately peripheral: curated, mostly non-commercial releases of under 1% of its data, sometimes trained on public consortium data, while the data that matter stay proprietary. It anticipates the 'open papers and benchmarks, closed data' pattern of the 2025-2026 AI-for-science labs.

**Timeline.** 2019: Proprietary → Curated sample datasets under CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 (opening); 2021: IPO $2.92B, Nasdaq (RXRX)

**Risk events.** 20 Nov 2024: completed acquisition of Exscientia by UK scheme of arrangement (0.7729 RXRX shares per Exscientia share; 102.1M shares issued); 10 Jun 2025: ~20% workforce reduction (~$11M charges) under a 'streamlined operating strategy' and post-integration transitions; runway guided into Q4 2027; cash burn guided below $390M for 2026; FY2025: revenue $74.7M, net loss $644.8M (TTM to 30 Sep 2026: revenue $68.2M, net loss $475.7M); FY2025 10-K: REC-2282, REC-994 and REC-3964 discontinued or seeking partners; 1 Jan 2026: co-founder Chris Gibson stepped down as CEO (became Chair); Najat Khan became CEO; Market cap: ~$2.92B at the 2021 IPO, $2.89B at end-2021, $1.46B end-2022, $2.67B end-2024, $1.66B on 23 Nov 2026

**Robustness.** Public and well partnered: more than $500M in cumulative upfront and milestone payments, including Roche/Genentech ($213M) and Sanofi ($134M), with Bayer and Merck KGaA also partners. It runs an NVIDIA-built supercomputer (BioHive-2) and automated labs doing over 2M experiments a week. Cash burn is heavy and value depends on clinical readouts for five wholly owned clinical programs. The proprietary phenomics dataset is the core asset, and the open releases are sized so as not to give it away.

**Physical-science relevance.** Low to moderate. The work is biology and drug discovery, not physical science. But Recursion is the leading example of an industrialized, closed-loop automated-lab 'data factory' feeding ML models, the architecture that materials-focused entrants (Lila, Periodic Labs) now pitch. Its RxRx strategy, releasing a non-commercial sample of under 1% of its data as benchmarks, is a template others copy. OpenPhenom also shows reliance on public consortium data (JUMP-CP) alongside proprietary data.

### Schrödinger, Inc.

founded 1990 · US · origin: academic · openness 0→1 · public · $1.83B (market_cap, 2026-11-23) · revenue $255.9M (2025) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 36 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Schrödinger has stayed closed for 35 years. Its moat is validated physics (force fields, free-energy methods) built on academic code it licenses exclusively, not open code. Open academic engines did not displace it; its own 10-K treats them as competitors lacking commercialization intent. The open PyMOL core keeps a broad user base and brand funnel for the paid version. No evidence was found that openness or closedness drove its 2025 retreat from wholly-owned clinical trials, which followed clinical safety events.

**Takeaway.** Schrödinger is the closed, physics-based, academically derived incumbent that AI-for-science entrants are measured against. It is 35 years old, public since 2020, earned $256M revenue in 2025 and sells to materials customers, yet it loses money and was worth about $1.8B in November 2026, below its end-2021 and end-2023 values. In 2025 it pulled back from running its own clinical trials toward software and partnering. That suggests its durable business is selling closed simulation tools, even as open academic engines cover parts of the same stack for free.

**Timeline.** 2020: IPO, Nasdaq (SDGR)

**Risk events.** Aug 2025: discontinued SGR-2921 (CDC7 inhibitor) after two deaths in AML patients where the drug was considered a contributing factor. Beyond finishing Phase 1 of SGR-1505 and SGR-3515 it 'do[es] not intend to initiate additional clinical trials' on its own and seeks partners (FY2025 10-K); FY2025: revenue $255.9M (software $199.5M, +11%), net loss ~$103M; Market cap: $2.47B (end-2021), $1.33B (end-2022), $2.58B (end-2023), $1.41B (end-2024), $1.32B (end-2025), $1.83B (23 Nov 2026); Depends on an exclusive, royalty-bearing Columbia University licence for core codes

**Robustness.** A public company with recurring software revenue across pharma and materials customers, plus partnerships including a $19.5M Gates Foundation-funded predictive-toxicology initiative (2024). Its key IP comes from a single university licence. Formats and workflows are proprietary, so customers are locked in. Open academic engines (OpenMM, OpenFF, GROMACS) are credible substitutes for parts of the stack, and open-source PyMOL would survive the company.

**Physical-science relevance.** High. The same physics platform is sold to materials customers in OLED displays, aerospace and defence polymers, semiconductors, oil-and-gas catalysis, batteries and consumer goods (FY2025 10-K). Projects include a Gates Ventures battery-simulation agreement (2020, extended 2023) and a Copernic Catalysts collaboration on e-fuel and bulk-chemical catalysts (2023). In 2013 it ran a then-record 156,000-core cloud screen of 205,000 materials molecules (Wikipedia).

### Strateos, Inc. (formerly Transcriptic)

founded 2012 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 1→1 · confidence: low · tool: WebSearch (+ WebFetch of cited pages); budget exhausted after 8 queries

*Computed:* age 14 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Transcriptic tried to seed an ecosystem with an open protocol standard (Autoprotocol, BSD) while keeping the lab and execution proprietary. The standard did not become an industry lingua franca, and the company still had to restrict Autoprotocol submission to paying customers. The 2023 retreat to private on-site labs points to unit-economics and demand problems with centralized hosted labs, not to openness, as the decisive factor.

**Takeaway.** Strateos (ex-Transcriptic) is the cautionary tale among US cloud labs. Over a decade it raised roughly $80M+ in announced rounds, won Eli Lilly as an anchor partner, and opened only a peripheral protocol standard. In April 2023 it abandoned the hosted cloud-lab model for on-site private-lab consulting, and since then its web presence has gone stale and its ownership is unconfirmed. Its open Autoprotocol library outlived the business model but never achieved network effects, which suggests an open interface standard alone cannot rescue a capital-intensive, closed, centralized lab service.

**Timeline.** 2019: merger, 3Scan

**Risk events.** 2012-2016: raised ~$27M as Transcriptic (seed $1.2M 2012; $8.5M 2015; $13.4M 2016, Data Collective); Jun 2019: merged with 3Scan to form Strateos; Jan 2020: Eli Lilly and Strateos launched an 11,500 sq ft remote-controlled robotic lab in San Diego (>100 instruments, 5M-compound store); Jun 2021: closed a $56M Series B (Lilly among investors); 18 Apr 2023: 'strategic shift' away from hosted cloud lab toward designing and deploying customers' own on-site private cloud labs on its LodeStar software, plus a staffing reorganization; 2023-2026: website appears unmaintained (footer (c) 2022, last news item Feb 2023, contains spam text); aggregators conflict (Craft 'Active'; PitchBook 'acquired by Daiichi'; another directory says acquired by Multiply Labs Dec 2023). Neither acquisition is confirmed by a primary source; a targeted search found no Multiply Labs release mentioning Strateos.

**Robustness.** Fragile. Hosted cloud-lab capacity depended on a single pharma partner (Lilly's San Diego lab) and the company retreated from hosted labs in 2023. Its one open asset, Autoprotocol, is a BSD library and standard that can outlive the company but saw limited outside adoption (Emerald's SLL and others did not adopt it). After 2023 the company appears dormant or absorbed, with no primary-source confirmation either way.

**Physical-science relevance.** Moderate. The focus was biology and drug discovery (small-molecule screening, medicinal chemistry, imaging), but Transcriptic/Strateos was, with Emerald, one of the two pioneering US commercial cloud labs. Its trajectory is the main failure case for the hosted cloud-lab model that materials self-driving-lab efforts (and NSF's programmable cloud lab network) are now revisiting.

## F. Physical science: scientific software and open-science infrastructure (23)

### Ansys, Inc. (now part of Synopsys)

founded 1970 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→0 · acquired · $35.00B (acquisition_price, 2025-07) · revenue $2.54B (2024) · confidence: high · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 56 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Ansys never opened its core products; value accrued to shareholders through an IPO and a ~$35B sale rather than to a commons. Closed licensing produced a high-revenue, high-valuation business, but the community has no recourse if the acquirer reprioritises products, which the FTC divestitures and post-merger restructuring illustrate.

**Takeaway.** Ansys is the high-water mark for closed physical-science software value: a 1970 founder startup that became a $35B acquisition. Its trajectory (VC buyout, IPO, strategic sale to an EDA firm) is the commercial template against which open simulation infrastructure such as OpenFOAM and LAMMPS can be compared: far more capital captured, but control concentrated in a single owner.

**Timeline.** 1994: acquisition, TA Associates; 1996: IPO $46.0M, NASDAQ (ANSS); 2025: acquisition $35.00B, Synopsys

**Risk events.** 28 May 2025: FTC consent order required divestiture of Ansys PowerArtist (plus Synopsys optical and photonic tools) to Keysight as a condition of the merger; EU, UK and China reviews also required.; Nov 2025: reports that Synopsys would cut about 10% of its workforce and close sites in post-acquisition restructuring, largely in fiscal 2026.; Absorption into a chip-design (EDA) company: first joint product was 'multiphysics fusion' EDA software (Mar 2026), signalling a shift of focus toward semiconductor and AI-chip design.

**Robustness.** Commercially very robust (55 years, $2.54B revenue in 2024) but entirely vendor-controlled; product direction now set by Synopsys's EDA strategy. Users have no fork rights; open alternatives exist only for parts of the portfolio (e.g. OpenFOAM for CFD).

**Physical-science relevance.** Dominant commercial engineering-simulation suite for structural, fluid, thermal and electromagnetic physics used across aerospace, energy, automotive and semiconductor engineering.

### The Apache Software Foundation (ASF)

founded 1999 · US · origin: community · openness 4→4 · foundation · $2.2M (annual revenue, IRS Form 990, fiscal year ending April 2025 (expenses $2,334,727), 2025-04) · revenue $2.2M (2025) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 27 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** The ASF shows that neutral, volunteer governance can steward hundreds of critical projects on about $2M a year, because value creation is distributed to employers of contributors. The same lean model means it cannot fund maintenance directly, unlike the much richer Linux Foundation.

**Takeaway.** The ASF is the low-budget, high-legitimacy end of open governance: roughly $2.2M a year in revenue supports 290+ projects, about one-hundredth of the Linux Foundation's $221M. For science it is a reminder that foundation budgets measure overhead, not the value of what they steward.

**Risk events.** FY2025 (to April 2025) deficit: revenue $2.22M vs expenses $2.33M; net assets $4.21M.; A very small budget relative to the project portfolio (290+ projects, 10,200+ committers) means the ASF relies on volunteers and employer-paid contributors rather than funding developers itself.

**Robustness.** Extremely durable governance model (27 years) with 1,190+ members and 10,200+ committers; projects are maintained largely by contributors paid by their employers, so the foundation's small budget is not a single point of failure. Still adding top-level projects (Auron, Reqsign, ResilientDB announced 14 Oct 2026).

**Physical-science relevance.** Supplies the data layer for large-scale science and AI pipelines (Spark, Arrow, Parquet, Kafka) rather than domain physics codes.

### COMSOL (COMSOL AB / COMSOL Group)

founded 1986 · SE · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→0 · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 40 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** COMSOL shows closed physics software can stay independent: unlike Ansys it has not been acquired or gone public. Its AI strategy is to plug external LLMs into a closed product rather than to open data or code.

**Takeaway.** COMSOL is the private, founder-controlled closed comparator: 40 years old, 700+ employees and still independent, but opaque (no revenue disclosed). For the study it represents closed software whose longevity comes from steady licence sales rather than an exit, with AI added as a feature on top of proprietary solvers.

**Risk events.** No public financial disclosure found; continuity rests on founder-led private ownership (founders still CEO of COMSOL Group and president of COMSOL AB).

**Robustness.** Founder-led for ~40 years with 700+ staff and 16 offices plus distributors; no forks possible. Version 6.4 (listed as stable 18 Nov 2025) added an NVIDIA cuDSS GPU solver, LLM-assisted simulation (chatbot connecting to GPT-5, Gemini, DeepSeek and other OpenAI-API-compatible models) and DNN surrogate-model tools.

**Physical-science relevance.** Widely used in academic and industrial physics and engineering for coupled electromagnetics, heat transfer, structural mechanics, acoustics, chemical engineering and electrochemistry (e.g. battery modelling).

### DOE CODE (DOE Office of Scientific and Technical Information) and national-lab software release policy

founded 2017 · US · origin: government · openness 3→3 · government · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 9 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Policy has made open source the default outcome for DOE software that is released: 5,505 of 5,879 inventoried releases (93.6%) are open source, mostly under permissive licences (BSD-3-Clause 1,929, MIT 1,137, Apache-2.0 378). The registry measures release volume, not use or maintenance, and annual additions roughly halved between 2020-2022 and 2023-2024.

**Takeaway.** DOE CODE quantifies the US government's open-source science output: about 5,900 inventoried releases since 2017, led by LLNL (826), PNNL (721), Sandia (698) and LBNL (641), 94% of them open source and mostly permissively licensed. It shows national labs default to openness once code is released, but its own data also show a recent slowdown and a stale public inventory.

**Risk events.** Inventory staleness: DOE's public code.json was last updated 28 Apr 2025, and the DOE CODE news page has no items after Oct 2023.; Release volume fell after 2022: records created per year were 1,051 (2020), 859 (2021), 839 (2022), 446 (2023) and 588 (2024).; Open release depends on per-lab sensitivity and export-control review; closed or limited-availability software is also registered.

**Robustness.** Federal mandate (labs are required to announce software), DOIs for tagged releases, archived repositories updated daily, and a GitLab service for controlled collaboration. Depends on OSTI appropriations and on labs complying.

**Physical-science relevance.** The official channel through which national-lab physical-science software (for example Sandia's Trilinos packages such as Teuchos, the first record in the inventory, and LLNL, LANL and ORNL simulation codes) is announced, archived and cited; a measurable proxy for government open-source output.

### Enthought, Inc.

founded 2001 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 2→1 · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 25 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Open-source work built Enthought's reputation and seeded SciPy and its conference, but the company did not capture the scientific-Python distribution market it pioneered and has moved to closed, client-specific AI services. The open commons it helped start outlived its own open product line.

**Takeaway.** Enthought is a case of an open-science-software pioneer that monetised through services and distributions, then pivoted to proprietary AI consulting for materials and chemistry R&D. It illustrates that seeding open infrastructure (SciPy) creates large public value that the originating company need not capture.

**Risk events.** Pivot away from the Python-distribution business (Canopy/EPD no longer featured) toward custom AI and digital-transformation services for industrial R&D.

**Robustness.** 25 years in business as a private services firm. The open libraries it seeded (SciPy) now live in community projects independent of Enthought, so their survival does not depend on the company.

**Physical-science relevance.** Helped create the scientific Python stack (SciPy) used throughout physical science; now sells AI, surrogate modelling, Bayesian optimisation and 'Materials by Design' services to materials, chemicals, semiconductor and energy R&D organisations, including a 2025 R&D Innovation Summit in Tokyo.

### Gaussian, Inc.

founded 1987 · US · origin: academic · openness 1→0 · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 39 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Closing the code in 1987 created a durable licence business, but the exclusion of competing developers made Gaussian a long-running symbol of how closed scientific software can restrict benchmarking and critique, prompting a WATOC protest and Nature coverage. No source found quantifies whether the restrictions shifted users to other codes.

**Takeaway.** Gaussian shows the sharpest governance risk of closed scientific software: the vendor can decide who may use, test and compare its code, and did so against competing method developers. It is long-lived and commercially stable at modest academic prices ($2,500 to $6,000 per US academic licence), but its licence terms, not its science, are what the community remembers.

**Timeline.** 1987: Academic QCPE distribution / Carnegie Mellon licensing → Proprietary Gaussian, Inc. licence (closing)

**Risk events.** 'Banned by Gaussian' controversy: the licence bars researchers who develop competing software; an anonymous site, bannedbygaussian.org, listed scientists and institutions it said were barred.; 2000: in a WATOC referendum, 23 of 28 executive board members opposed such restrictive licences.; 2004: Nature news coverage of the bans (Nature 429, 231). Gaussian's president Michael Frisch called the claim that whole universities are refused 'an outright lie' and said only the competing group is excluded (response page last updated 27 Feb 2014).; No major new version since Gaussian 16 (2017) was found.

**Robustness.** Single private vendor controls the code; users cannot fork it, and competitors' groups can be excluded. Long commercial continuity (since 1987) and a large installed base in chemistry, but scientific reproducibility depends on licence access.

**Physical-science relevance.** One of the most widely used quantum-chemistry packages in chemistry and molecular materials science; the commercial reference for molecular DFT and ab initio methods.

### Globus (University of Chicago)

founded 2010 · US · origin: academic · openness 3→1 · nonprofit · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 16 yrs · 1 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Globus moved from open software to a freemium hosted service to become financially sustainable, a rare example of academic infrastructure deliberately 'closing' the product while keeping access free for most researchers. The move worked at scale (1 exabyte and 150,000+ users by 2020) but left the open code to a community fork.

**Takeaway.** Globus is the freemium counter-model in open science infrastructure: a university-operated service that charges institutions for advanced features while keeping basic transfers free for non-profit researchers. It shows that 'open science' infrastructure can be sustained by subscriptions rather than grants, at the cost of open-source control.

**Timeline.** None: Open-source Globus Toolkit → Hosted subscription service (closing); fork: Grid Community Toolkit (GCT)

**Risk events.** Abandoning the self-hosted open toolkit for a hosted service pushed parts of the grid community to maintain their own fork (GCT).; 27 May 2026: Globus began offering free Standard subscriptions to non-profits with annual research budgets under $4 million, shifting more of the cost onto larger subscribers.

**Robustness.** Run by a university as non-profit infrastructure with a subscription base, Internet2 NET+ membership (Jan 2025) and deep integration with DOE facilities (Argonne's APS and ALCF, NERSC's Perlmutter, OLCF's Frontier). Single operator, closed service: users depend on UChicago's continued operation.

**Physical-science relevance.** The standard tool for moving large instrument and simulation data between US national labs, light sources and universities; used for near-real-time X-ray (XPCS) data flows from Argonne's upgraded APS to supercomputers (Jan 2026) and alongside Argonne's AI inference service for science (May 2026).

### The HDF Group

founded 2006 · US · origin: academic · openness 3→3 · nonprofit · $3.0M (annual revenue, IRS Form 990, fiscal year ending Dec 2025 (92.6% program services, 0.4% contributions), 2025-12) · revenue $3.0M (2025) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 20 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Opening HDF and spinning it out of NCSA created a durable de facto standard, but the nonprofit captures little of the value: 2025 revenue was $3.0M, 93% from paid services, and shrinking. It is a clear case of critical open infrastructure whose users rarely pay for the maintainer.

**Takeaway.** The HDF Group shows the 'services nonprofit' model for academic-origin open infrastructure: 20 years independent of NCSA, but revenue has drifted down to about $3M and 17 staff while HDF5 underpins petabytes of science and AI training data. Longevity is real, but financial robustness is thin.

**Risk events.** Revenue has fallen from about $4.6-4.7M a year (2016-2020) to $2.5M (2024) and $3.0M (2025), with deficits in 2024 and 2025; net assets dropped from $2.45M (2023) to $1.62M (2025).; Almost no donation income (contributions $13,149 in 2025) despite HDF5 being embedded in NASA, netCDF-4 and many scientific workflows.

**Robustness.** HDF5 is deeply embedded (NASA EOS data, netCDF-4 is built on HDF5) and openly licensed, so the format would survive the organisation; but the canonical library is maintained by a small staff funded mostly by service contracts.

**Physical-science relevance.** A core storage format for simulation and instrument data in physics, climate, earth observation and materials science (including synchrotron and microscopy data), and increasingly used for AI/ML data pipelines (the group sells 'HDF5 for AI/ML' services).

### Jubilee (Machine Agency, University of Washington)

founded 2019 · US · origin: academic · openness 3→3 · nonprofit · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 7 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Open, certified designs let other labs adapt Jubilee for chemistry and materials automation (science-jubilee), at a fraction of the cost of commercial robots. Without a company or foundation behind it, supply and maintenance rest on individual builders and one university group.

**Takeaway.** Jubilee shows the academic open-hardware route into self-driving labs: a university-designed, OSHWA-certified toolchanger that labs can build for about $1,350, with almost no commercial ecosystem. It is cheap and adaptable but thin on long-term maintenance and supply.

**Risk events.** Main hardware repository last pushed 12 May 2024, suggesting core design development has slowed; activity has shifted to tools and science-jubilee.; Single named kit vendor (Filastruder frame kit, from Feb 2022), so builders largely source parts themselves.

**Robustness.** Fully published design (466 commits, 630 stars, 126 forks) that anyone can build for about $1,350 per unit excluding tools; survives independently of the lab, but has a small commercial supply chain.

**Physical-science relevance.** A low-cost, open tool-changing gantry that labs use as a configurable self-driving-lab platform (liquid handling, imaging, sample preparation), complementing closed commercial liquid handlers.

### Kitware, Inc.

founded 1998 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 3→3 · $39.0M (annual consolidated revenue, stated as 'more than $39 million' (year not given on the page), 2026-11) · revenue $39.0M (None) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 28 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Openness is the business model: permissively licensed tools created demand for Kitware's paid customisation, and government-funded development (DOE for ParaView, National Library of Medicine for CMake/ITK, Army SBIRs) seeded products that became de facto standards. Growth has been steady but modest (~200 staff, >$39M revenue) compared with closed simulation vendors.

**Takeaway.** Kitware is the best US example of a services company sustaining open scientific infrastructure for decades without VC, an exit or relicensing: government R&D contracts paid to build open tools, and the tools' ubiquity sells more contracts. The model is durable but caps growth at services scale.

**Risk events.** Business depends heavily on government R&D contracts (defense, intelligence, energy, health agencies are named customer sectors); no specific 2025-2026 event was confirmed.

**Robustness.** Independent and private for 28 years; permissive licences and very wide adoption (CMake is used by KDE, Blender, Netflix, the ATLAS experiment and others) mean the projects would outlive the company. Offices in several US states and Lyon, France.

**Physical-science relevance.** VTK and ParaView are the standard open visualization stack for large simulations at DOE labs: ParaView began in 2000 as a Kitware-Los Alamos collaboration funded by the DOE ASCI Views program, with Sandia joining in 2005. Tomviz serves electron tomography.

### LAMMPS (Large-scale Atomic/Molecular Massively Parallel Simulator), Sandia National Laboratories

founded 1995 · US · origin: government · openness 1→3 · government · confidence: medium · tool: WebSearch

*Computed:* age 31 yrs · 0 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** Opening the code under the GPL in 2004 turned a lab-internal CRADA code into a community platform: hundreds of outside contributors added physics packages, and the code grew ~25-fold in size. The trade-off is maintenance strain: the developers report that features are rewarded but upkeep is not, and that funding does not cover long-term-support releases.

**Takeaway.** LAMMPS shows a national-lab code outliving the industrial CRADA that created it by opening up: 30 years on it is a GPL community platform and a default MD engine for ML potentials, with no revenue model. Its fragility is people, not licence: a small DOE-funded core team carries maintenance for a very large user base.

**Timeline.** 2004: No-cost non-open licence (LAMMPS 99/2001, contested) → GPLv2 (opening)

**Risk events.** No long-term-support releases 'due to limited workforce and funding' (developers, 2025).; Expected leadership transition as senior developers retire; maintenance earns little academic credit.; Testing infrastructure partly depends on a Temple University cluster that could become unavailable.

**Robustness.** GPLv2 code on GitHub with 430+ contributors and 92 optional packages; core team spread over five institutions; packaged by Linux distributions. Active cadence (stable release 30 Sep 2026, feature release 13 Nov 2026, ~7,400 commits between stable releases). Core funding still flows mainly from DOE through Sandia.

**Physical-science relevance.** One of the standard open classical MD engines for materials science, chemistry and soft matter; the main host for running machine-learned interatomic potentials at scale on DOE supercomputers. R&D 100 award 2018.

### The Linux Foundation

founded 2007 · US · origin: corporate · openness 4→4 · nonprofit · $220.7M (annual revenue, IRS Form 990, fiscal year ending Dec 2024 (expenses $204,369,300; net assets $144.6M), 2024-12) · revenue $220.7M (2024) · confidence: high · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 19 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** The Linux Foundation shows open source monetised collectively: companies pay for neutral governance of shared code, which has made it the default home when AI firms open up standards (Anthropic's MCP, OpenAI's AGENTS.md, Block's goose in Dec 2025). Openness here strengthens member companies' ecosystems; it is not a public-science funding model.

**Takeaway.** With $221M revenue (2024), the Linux Foundation dwarfs the science-relevant open foundations in this group (NumFOCUS $8.4M in 2024; the ASF $2.2M in FY2025; the RCSB PDB's federal award is about $49M over five years). In 2024-2026 it absorbed AI-era projects (PyTorch, Jupyter, MCP), so corporate-funded foundations, not science agencies, increasingly govern the open tools that AI-for-science depends on.

**Risk events.** Dependence on corporate membership and events; priorities are set by member companies (business-league status).; Rapid expansion into AI governance: Agentic AI Foundation (9 Dec 2025), React Foundation intent (7 Oct 2025, from Meta), Tokenomics Foundation (4 Aug 2026), Jupyter Foundation (19 Nov 2024).

**Robustness.** Very high: revenue grew from $177.1M (2022) to $196.0M (2023) to $220.7M (2024) with surpluses each year; more than 1,000 corporate members and more than 1,000 hosted projects (per Wikipedia, 2025). Projects are legally separated into sub-foundations.

**Physical-science relevance.** Indirect but growing: hosts the PyTorch Foundation (the dominant AI framework used in scientific ML), LF Energy, and, through LF Charities, Project Jupyter. Its agentic-AI standards (MCP) are already used to connect AI agents to lab and data tools.

### The Materials Project (Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory)

founded 2011 · US · origin: government · openness 4→4 · government · confidence: high · tool: WebSearch

*Computed:* age 15 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Open, free, CC BY data turned a single-lab DFT effort into shared infrastructure: users grew from >20,000 (2016) to ~120,000 (early 2020) to >650,000 (Jan 2026), and the platform has >32,000 citations. Openness also made it default AI training data for industry labs (DeepMind, Microsoft, Toyota Research Institute), which in turn contributed data back (DeepMind ~400,000 compounds); the GNoME dataset on MP is restricted to non-commercial use.

**Takeaway.** The Materials Project is the clearest physical-science case of government-funded open data becoming AI training infrastructure: a DOE lab project with no revenue model now underpins commercial materials-AI efforts at Google DeepMind and Microsoft. Its value is enormous in usage terms but invisible financially (no published budget), and its main risks are federal-funding dependence and creeping licence fragmentation when industry contributes data on its own terms.

**Risk events.** Single-sponsor dependence: funded by the DOE Office of Science (BES; also JCESR support); no annual budget is published.; Licence fragmentation after the GNoME contribution: a non-commercial subset now sits beside CC BY 4.0 data, and direct lakehouse access bypasses the API's licence controls.

**Robustness.** Data mirrored on the AWS Open Data Registry and the platform migrated to cloud (MongoDB, AWS, Datadog) with a reported 99.98% uptime. Core codes (pymatgen) are open source and widely reused. Stewardship is still concentrated in one lab and one federal sponsor.

**Physical-science relevance.** The most-cited computed materials database: >200,000 inorganic materials and >577,000 molecules. Its relaxation trajectories (MPtrj) are the standard training set for universal machine-learned interatomic potentials and the Matbench Discovery benchmark. Google DeepMind used it to train initial GNoME models; Microsoft used it for MatterGen. Its DFT calculations are run with the proprietary VASP code.

**What openness did.** NumFOCUS turned volunteer scientific-Python projects into entities able to receive grants and donations; revenue rose from $4,690 (2012) to $9.3M (2022). The open model's weakness shows in 2023-2024: spending outran income while the stack it supports became foundational for AI and data science.

**Takeaway.** NumFOCUS is the main US nonprofit sustaining the scientific Python stack, at about $8-11M a year, small relative to the commercial AI value built on NumPy, pandas and scikit-learn. Its 2023-2024 deficits and Jupyter's move to the Linux Foundation suggest that community fiscal sponsors are being outcompeted by better-funded corporate-backed foundations.

**Risk events.** Two consecutive operating deficits: FY2023 revenue $7.25M vs expenses $9.95M; FY2024 revenue $8.38M vs expenses $11.09M. Net assets fell from $11.50M (2022) to $5.67M (2024).; Project Jupyter, once the best-known scientific Python project, is not on NumFOCUS's current sponsored-project list; its donations are now handled by LF Charities under the Linux Foundation's Jupyter Foundation (Nov 2024).

**Robustness.** Diversified across 63 sponsored projects, but its own finances depend on grants, corporate sponsorship and events, and reserves roughly halved in two years. Projects can leave for other fiscal hosts, as Jupyter's move to the Linux Foundation shows.

**Physical-science relevance.** Fiscal home for core physical-science and numerical libraries: NumPy, SciPy, Astropy, SunPy, yt, Cantera (combustion chemistry), PyBaMM (battery modelling), PETSc, FEniCS, MDAnalysis, TARDIS, Gammapy and others.

### OpenFlexure Microscope (OpenFlexure Project)

founded 2016 · GB · origin: academic · openness 3→3 · nonprofit · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 10 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Openness turned an academic flexure-stage design into a globally manufactured instrument: twelve independent vendors (including IO Rodeo in the US and TauLab and LabCrafter in the UK) sell kits or assembled units, and local production in Tanzania and Kenya followed. Openness also shifts regulatory burden to manufacturers, so clinical use waits on vendor-level certification.

**Takeaway.** OpenFlexure is the clearest open-hardware example in this group: grant-funded academic design, commercialised by independent small vendors rather than a spin-out, with reach (50+ countries) far beyond what the university team could manufacture. Its limit is that open designs do not carry medical certification, which still needs a liable manufacturer.

**Risk events.** Medical use: the project is not a legal manufacturer and a design cannot itself be certified; in-vitro-diagnostic certification must be pursued by individual manufacturers with ISO 13485/14971 systems (work under way with Bongo Tech & Research Labs, Tanzania; no certification confirmed).; Funding is a sequence of UK research grants and prizes, not a revenue stream for the core team.

**Robustness.** Fully open designs with 12 independent vendors on four continents, so production does not depend on the university team. Built and used in more than 50 countries; more than 100 units were produced locally in Tanzania and Kenya by 2020.

**Physical-science relevance.** Low-cost automated optical microscopy for labs and education; a reference design for open lab instrumentation and for automated/AI microscopy (e.g. EPSRC-funded malaria-diagnostics work).

### OpenFOAM (OpenFOAM Foundation / CFD Direct; OpenCFD Ltd under ESI Group and Keysight)

founded 2004 · GB · origin: academic · openness 0→4 · foundation · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 22 yrs · 0 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** Opening FOAM in 2004 gave it a user base the Foundation says broadly matches major proprietary CFD packages 'despite having only a small fraction of their resources'. Openness also made it acquirable piecemeal (SGI, ESI, Keysight bought the company and trademark, not the code) and led to fragmentation into competing lines, while maintenance remains chronically underfunded.

**Takeaway.** OpenFOAM is the open CFD comparator to Ansys: comparable reach, but its maintainers run on about EUR 250k a year while Ansys sold for $35B. GPL licensing protected the code through three changes of corporate owner and a developer split, but did not solve sustainability; the Foundation's own 2025 appeal says funding must double.

**Timeline.** 2004: Proprietary FOAM (Nabla Ltd) → GPL (OpenFOAM) (opening); fork: openfoam-extend / foam-extend; 2011: acquisition, Silicon Graphics International (SGI); 2012: acquisition, ESI Group; 2023: acquisition, Keysight Technologies

**Risk events.** 2015-2016 split: Weller and Greenshields left OpenCFD to form CFD Direct (Mar 2015); two parallel 'OpenFOAM' release lines plus foam-extend now coexist.; Copyright (Foundation) and trademark (OpenCFD, now Keysight-controlled) are held by different parties.; Nov 2025: the Foundation called its EUR 250k/yr maintenance funding 'inadequate' and set a 2026 target of EUR 500k; CFD Direct, which maintains openfoam.org, is 'a company of 5 people'.; Sep 2024: ESI's CEO resigned as a director of OpenFOAM Foundation Ltd.

**Robustness.** Code is GPL and has been forked repeatedly, so it cannot be closed; but each main line depends on a small team (CFD Direct: 5 people) funded by maintenance plans (EUR 5,000 / 25,000 / 100,000 per year tiers). The corporate parent of OpenCFD changed three times (SGI, ESI, Keysight) without the code disappearing.

**Physical-science relevance.** The leading open-source CFD code, used across academic fluid mechanics, combustion, multiphase flow and industrial engineering; the main open alternative to Ansys Fluent and other commercial CFD.

### Project Jupyter

founded 2014 · US · origin: academic · openness 3→4 · foundation · $6.0M (joint philanthropic grant (Helmsley Trust, Moore Foundation, Sloan Foundation), 2015; no current annual budget found, 2015) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 12 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Openness and a standard file format let Jupyter become universal (about 10 million notebooks on GitHub by January 2021; the VS Code Jupyter extension passed 40 million downloads by July 2022) and let clouds build commercial notebook services on it. Philanthropic grants funded the core early on, and the 2024 Linux Foundation move tries to convert corporate dependence into sustained funding.

**Takeaway.** Jupyter is the archetype of academic open-science software becoming industry infrastructure for the AI era: a physics student's tool became the front end of Google Colab and SageMaker. Its sustainability path ran from philanthropy (Sloan, Moore, Helmsley) to a corporate-funded Linux Foundation entity, showing how value captured by big platforms is partly routed back through foundations.

**Risk events.** Dec 2022: original steering council dissolved and replaced by a new governance model (Executive Council, Software Steering Council).; Commercial clouds ship Jupyter-derived front ends (Google Colab, Amazon SageMaker, Azure), so much of the value is captured by platforms that are not obliged to fund upstream; the Nov 2024 Jupyter Foundation (AWS, Google, Meta, Bloomberg as premier members) is the response.

**Robustness.** High: permissive licence, an open notebook standard, multi-company funding (AWS, Bloomberg, Google, Microsoft, Quansight, QuantStack and others) and a Linux Foundation-hosted foundation. Embedded in DOE facilities (NERSC, where Jupyter accounted for roughly 20-25% of user interaction in 2021) and in every major cloud ML platform.

**Physical-science relevance.** The default interactive environment for computational science and ML; born in a physics PhD and used at DOE facilities such as NERSC and KBase. Nature (2021) named it one of ten computer codes that transformed science.
