### Quantum ESPRESSO (Quantum ESPRESSO Foundation)

founded 2002 · IT · origin: academic · openness 3→4 · foundation · confidence: medium · tool: WebSearch

*Computed:* age 24 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Open licensing from the start let a national-centre code become a community 'distribution' with contributors across Europe and the US, and the 2009 reference paper has roughly 28,000 Scopus citations. The cost of openness is that it earns no licence revenue, so development is paced by EU grant cycles rather than sales, unlike its closed competitor VASP.

**Takeaway.** Quantum ESPRESSO is the open, foundation-governed counterpart to VASP: comparable scientific reach (tens of thousands of citations) funded by European public research money and a non-profit that holds the copyright. It is a non-US comparator showing that the open model in DFT depends on public HPC programmes such as MaX/EuroHPC rather than on licence sales.

**Risk events.** Core development is financed through successive, time-limited EU Centre of Excellence grants (MaX: H2020 824143; EuroHPC 101093374, EUR 8.5M total across several codes).; Small coordinating team (the user guide names two coordinators, Paolo Giannozzi and Pietro Delugas) supported by MaX and CINECA.

**Robustness.** GPL code with foundation-held copyright and multi-institution membership in Europe and the US; installed as standard on major HPC centres (NERSC, LUMI, OSC) with NVIDIA and AMD GPU builds. Stable release 7.5 (19 Aug 2025).

**Physical-science relevance.** One of the two or three most widely used DFT codes in condensed-matter physics and materials science; the open alternative to VASP for plane-wave pseudopotential calculations, and a common engine for high-throughput workflows (e.g. AiiDA, Materials Cloud) and ML training-set generation.

### RCSB Protein Data Bank and the Worldwide Protein Data Bank (wwPDB)

founded 1971 · US · origin: government · openness 4→4 · government · $49.4M (multi-year federal award for RCSB PDB core operations, 2024 to 2028 (not annual; prior 2019-2023 period was $34.3M). RCSB separately estimates archive replacement cost at >$27B., 2025-01) · confidence: high · tool: WebSearch

*Computed:* age 55 yrs · 0 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** Open, community-mandated deposition built a 55-year, curated, uniform archive (247,287 structures at 1 Jan 2026) that later became the training set for AlphaFold2. The 2024 Chemistry Nobel laureates credited the open PDB as essential. Openness also yields heavy reuse (4.7 billion downloads across wwPDB in 2025) and many redistributors, which in turn makes the archive hard to lose.

**Takeaway.** The PDB is the strongest evidence that long-lived, government-founded open scientific data pays off on timescales no market would fund: a 1971 national-lab archive costing roughly $10M a year in US federal support became the substrate for a Nobel-winning AI system 50 years later. Its robustness comes from international replication and a public-domain licence, not from any single institution.

**Timeline.** 2021: wwPDB historical data-access licence → CC0 1.0 (opening)

**Risk events.** 1998 federal re-competition moved US management from Brookhaven to the RCSB consortium; the archive survived the hand-off.; Funding depends on five-year federal awards (NSF DBI-2321666, DOE DE-SC0019749, NIH NCI/NIAID/NIGMS).; Archive-format transition: switch to PDBx/mmCIF and extended PDB IDs as four-character IDs run out.; Traffic now includes bot and AI-agent load (>6.7 billion requests in 2025).

**Robustness.** Very high. The archive is held jointly by wwPDB data centres in the US, Europe and Asia, released under CC0, redistributed by 520 external resources, and served through AWS Open Data (s3.rcsb.org). It has outlived its original host lab and a change of US management.

**Physical-science relevance.** Structural biology, chemistry and biophysics. About 82% of X-ray structures released in 2025 came from US labs that used DOE synchrotron or XFEL facilities, so it is also the main public output channel for DOE light sources. PDB structures were the training set for AlphaFold2, RoseTTAFold and OpenFold.

*Computed:* age 37 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Staying closed lets VASP fund a dedicated developer team from licence fees rather than grants, and it remains a reference code with more than 1,400 licensed research groups. The cost is restricted reproducibility and reuse: groups cannot share the code, and in 2026 the vendor moved to prevent AI tools from ingesting it. Its outputs nonetheless flow into open databases such as the Materials Project.

**Takeaway.** VASP is the clearest closed counterpoint in physical-science software: an academic code that commercialised through group licences, kept source confidential, and in 2026 explicitly barred AI training on its code. It shows that closed scientific software can be long-lived and high-impact, while open data built on its outputs (Materials Project) ended up becoming the AI training resource.

**Risk events.** 2026: licence enforcement tightened; HPC centres report a switch from group-based checks to personal licence-key validation (VASP 6.6.1 already; 6.5.1 from 1 Oct 2026).; 16 Jun 2026: guidelines restrict AI coding tools on VASP source, reflecting the risk that LLMs could clone the code.; Jul 2026: serious force bug in VASP 6.6.0 (spin-polarized meta-GGA) required an urgent 6.6.1 patch; groups whose licence did not cover 6.6.1 had to apply a patch file.

**Robustness.** Commercially self-sustaining for ~35 years with a single development centre in Vienna (Kresse group). Users cannot fork or redistribute; continuity depends on the company and its licence terms. Open alternatives (Quantum ESPRESSO, ABINIT) exist but VASP results and PAW datasets underpin much published and database work.

**Physical-science relevance.** A dominant closed DFT code in materials science. The open Materials Project states that it uses 'DFT as implemented in the Vienna Ab Initio Simulation Package (VASP)', so a flagship open database (and the AI models trained on it) rests on a closed upstream engine.

### Zenodo (CERN / OpenAIRE)

founded 2013 · CH · origin: government · openness 4→4 · government · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 13 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Free, no-requirements deposit made Zenodo a default home for 'long-tail' research outputs: it crossed 1 petabyte in 2021 with 15 million yearly visits and 55 million unique downloads. Openness also invites spam, which has led to verification-based de-ranking that disadvantages unaffiliated researchers.

**Takeaway.** Zenodo shows the 'public lab as host' model: an EU-commissioned, CERN-operated repository that is free to use because it rides on CERN's physics infrastructure and EC grants. Its July 2026 AI notice states that uploaded files are not used to train AI models, a deliberate contrast with how other open archives have become AI training data.

**Risk events.** Spam and abuse: since at least Nov 2025 Zenodo de-ranks records and communities from accounts that are not manually verified (institutional affiliation and email expected), and such records may not be indexed by external search engines; users without affiliations complained in early 2026 (GitHub issue #2604).; Runs as a 'marginal activity' on CERN infrastructure with EC project funding, so it has no independent revenue.

**Robustness.** Files sit in CERN's EOS storage with two replicas per file on different servers plus daily incremental backups to a Ceph cluster at a separate site ~3.5 km away. Retention is 'for the lifetime of the repository', tied to CERN, whose experimental programme is defined for 20+ years.

**Physical-science relevance.** Default DOI-minting archive for physics and materials datasets, simulation outputs and software releases (via GitHub integration), born at the world's largest particle-physics lab; widely used for supplementary data and ML datasets.

## G. Additive manufacturing, lab-automation incumbents and open-science publishing infrastructure (added after the video) (21)

### 3D Systems Corporation

founded 1986 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→0 · public · $254.5M (market_cap, 2026-11-23) · revenue $386.9M (2025) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 40 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** 3D Systems used patents as its main moat (against EOS in the 1990s and Formlabs in 2012), which delayed but did not stop competition once key patents expired and crowdfunded entrants appeared. The closed, acquisition-driven strategy did not protect value: market cap fell from $9.55B (end-2013) to about $0.25B (Nov 2026) while lower-cost and open-ecosystem competitors grew.

**Takeaway.** 3D Systems is the original closed AM incumbent: founded on Hull's 1986 SLA patent, enforced against newcomers, and enlarged through acquisitions in the 2012-2014 bubble. Its decline since then (a market cap down roughly 97% from the 2013 peak, divestiture of its software in 2025) shows that patents and proprietary ecosystems gave a head start, not durability. For a lab, its sale of the 3DXpert metal-AM software is a reminder that closed tool chains can change hands or disappear.

**Risk events.** 2012-2014: acquisition spree (Z Corp $137M, Jan 2012; Simbionix $120M and LayerWise, 2014; botObjects, 2015) during the 2013 3D-printing bubble; year-end market cap peaked at $9.55B (2013) then fell to $974M by end-2015; Nov 2012: sued Formlabs and Kickstarter over a stereolithography patent; dismissed/settled 2014 (featured in Netflix's Print the Legend); 2015-2016 and 2020: CEO turnover (Reichental out Oct 2015; Joshi Apr 2016; Graves May 2020); 2021: SPAC-era rerating to a $2.75B year-end market cap, then down to $227.9M by end-2025 (StockAnalysis); Jun 2023: made a ~$1.2B offer for Stratasys during the Stratasys/Desktop Metal/Nano Dimension fight; not completed; 2024: revenue $440.1M with a net loss of $255.6M

**Robustness.** Forty-year-old public company with a large installed base and a healthcare segment ($179.6M in 2025), but shrinking: revenue down from $440.1M (2024) to $386.9M (2025), software assets sold, and market cap about $255M (2026-11-23), roughly 97% below its 2013 year-end peak. Users depend on proprietary printers, materials and (now divested) software, with no open fallback.

**Physical-science relevance.** Its DMP line (from the 2014 LayerWise acquisition) is a closed metal laser powder-bed fusion platform used for aerospace and medical metal parts, and 3D Systems originated SLA and patents on SLS that shaped who could enter the market. For LPBF aluminium-alloy development it is a closed comparator: process parameters and build software are vendor-controlled, and the 2025 sale of its 3DXpert metal-AM software shows how tool chains can be divested out from under users. Specific DMP aluminium alloys were not confirmed in fetched pages.

### Aconity3D GmbH

DE · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→0 · confidence: low · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Aconity3D built its niche on process openness rather than open licensing: by selling machines whose parameters, scan strategies and data are fully accessible, it serves universities and institutes that need to publish process data, a market the 'validated parameter set' incumbents (EOS, Velo3D) serve poorly. Without financial disclosures its commercial outcome cannot be quantified.

**Takeaway.** Aconity3D shows a third path between open-source hardware and closed incumbents: proprietary machines sold as open research instruments, with every laser parameter and sensor stream exposed for analysis and publication. For a lab developing LPBF aluminium alloys and aiming for self-driving experiments, that open process access, not open CAD files, is the openness that matters, and it is a criterion to require when buying closed instruments.

**Robustness.** Small private company with no disclosed funding, revenue or headcount; it appears stable (2026 product launches, a second user conference in May 2026, Formnext 2026, ISO 9001, America Makes membership, a US subsidiary in El Paso). Because users get full parameter and data access, the process knowledge generated on its machines stays with the user and is publishable, which reduces lock-in compared with recipe-based vendors even though the machines themselves are proprietary.

**Physical-science relevance.** The closest commercial match to an open-science LPBF alloy lab. Research configurations target 'new alloys and materials, superalloys and intermetallics, and graded or multi-material parts'; the AconityMINI research configuration shrinks the build to 55 mm diameter for 'material development with minimal powder'; MIDI/MIDI+ offer high-temperature preheating (up to 1,200 C / 1,000 C) for crack-prone alloys. Free access to every parameter, scan vector and synchronised sensor stream is what closed-loop or self-driving LPBF experiments (e.g. Bayesian optimisation of Al-alloy process windows) require. Listed users are mostly research institutions (ETH Zurich Inspire, IWT Bremen, UniBw Munich, FAU Erlangen-Nuernberg, Innsbruck, UTEP's W.M. Keck Center, TII). No aluminium-specific examples were given on the fetched pages.

### arXiv

founded 1991 · US · origin: government · openness 4→4 · nonprofit · revenue $6.4M (2025) · confidence: high · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 35 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Free reading and submission made arXiv the default preprint venue for physics, math and CS. It holds 2.9M+ papers, has served 6 billion downloads and reaches 5M+ monthly active users, on a budget of about $6.7M (FY2025) with 28 staff. Openness combined with AI has become a strain: submissions doubled in two years, mostly in CS, forcing quality gates, rate limits and bans that make the server less open to new authors. Independence from Cornell, backed by $17.2M from three philanthropies, aims at a sturdier funding base, but it removes the university's in-kind subsidy.

**Takeaway.** arXiv is the long-lived success of open, nonprofit science infrastructure: in 35 years it went from a national-lab physics archive to an independent nonprofit running on a budget under $9M. Its 2025-26 history shows a new risk to openness in the age of AI. Generative tools inflate submission volume faster than moderation can scale, so 'free to submit' now comes with rate limits and quality gates. Its robustness model is diversified support from member libraries, Simons, Schmidt Sciences, NASA, NSF, XTX and Siegel, rather than one patron.

**Risk events.** FY2025 (to 30 Jun 2025): expenses $6.71M against revenue $6.41M, a $297k deficit covered from operating reserves. The FY2026 budget plans $8.85M of expenses and a $465k deficit.; Volume shock: submissions rose from 244,031 (2024) to 284,486 (2025), +16.6%. Sept 2026 set a record of 40,363, double Sept 2024 (20,569), and cs.AI grew more than sixfold in two years.; 31 Oct 2025: stopped accepting CS review and position papers not vetted by a journal or conference, citing AI-generated submissions. May 2026: one-year submission bans for unchecked LLM output.; 1 Oct 2026: stopgap limit of two submissions per submitter per calendar month. Sept 2026 submissions generated almost 9,000 support tickets.; 1 Jul 2026: separated from Cornell, whose in-kind contribution (administration, HR, IT, facilities) was budgeted at $1.89M, or 22% of FY2026 revenue. Our assessment: the new nonprofit must replace that support.; 23 Sep 2026: $17.2M in multiyear (three to five year) commitments from Simons Foundation International, XTX Markets and Siegel Family Endowment

**Robustness.** 35 years of operation through two changes of host (LANL to Cornell in 2001, Cornell to independence in 2026). Revenue is diversified: 278 members, whose fees were 56% of FY2025 gifts and member contributions, plus Simons, Schmidt Sciences, NASA and NSF sponsored funding and new multiyear philanthropy. The operation is lean, with 28 staff and a budget under $9M, but it ran deficits in FY2025 and FY2026 and must now cover overheads Cornell used to absorb. Content is mirrored and bulk-downloadable, and its submission rules are tightening under AI-driven volume.

**Physical-science relevance.** arXiv began as a physics server, and physics was still 30% of 2025 submissions (CS 46%, math 15%). Condensed-matter and materials preprints, including ML-for-materials and self-driving-lab work, often appear there first. For an LPBF alloy lab it is the default free venue for fast, citable dissemination, and its CC licence options let authors make text reusable. Its 2025-26 struggle with AI-generated submissions shows the costs AI imposes on open science infrastructure.

### Automata Technologies Ltd

GB · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→0 · private_vc · funding $135.0M · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Automata uses 'open' as a marketing term for API access while keeping hardware and software proprietary, and its funding ($135M across 2022-2026) has come from VCs and a closed-instrument strategic (Danaher). There is no evidence that openness, in the licensing sense, has played any role in its growth.

**Takeaway.** Automata is the venture-backed newcomer in lab automation: architects turned robot-arm makers turned life-science bench builders, with at least $135M raised since 2022 and a 2026 AI-lab pitch backed by Danaher. Like Opentrons' 2026 messaging, it redefines 'open' as API access rather than open licences, so labs gain scriptability but not the ability to fork or repair the system.

**Risk events.** Pivot from affordable general-purpose robot arms (Eva) to life-science lab benches; Feb 2022: $50M Series B (Octopus Ventures lead; Hummingbird, Latitude, ABB Technology Ventures, Isomer, In-Q-Tel) on COVID-era NHS testing work ('nearly a million tests' in recent months); Oct 2023: $40M round backed by Dimension and A.P. Moller Holding; Jan 2026: $45M Series C led by Dimension with Danaher Ventures (plus a strategic partnership with Danaher), repositioned as 'the operating system for AI-ready labs'

**Robustness.** VC-funded and still raising every 2-3 years; no revenue or valuation disclosed. Customers named include the Francis Crick Institute, Royal Marsden, Earlham Institute, bit.bio, Lonza and 'five top pharma companies', and Danaher (a large closed-instrument conglomerate) is now a strategic investor. Proprietary hardware and software mean customers depend on Automata's survival, partly offset by a Python SDK and API.

**Physical-science relevance.** Built for biology (cell culture, genomics, NHS pathology), but its design pattern, a modular bench with track transport that integrates third-party instruments and is driven by software with an API and AI connectivity (MCP, NVIDIA collaboration in 2026), is the same architecture materials self-driving labs need. No materials-science or LPBF use was found; relevance to the Digital Alloy Lab is as a commercial comparator for orchestration rather than as a tool.

### Carbon, Inc.

founded 2013 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→0 · private_vc · $2.50B (private_valuation, 2019-12) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 13 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Carbon kept everything closed and monetised proprietary resins, enforcing patents (EP 3158400). Staying private and closed spared it the SPAC collapse that hit Desktop Metal, Markforged and Velo3D, but its valuation has not been re-established since 2019; openness played no visible role in either direction.

**Takeaway.** Carbon is the private, closed, materials-first AM unicorn: a 2013 chemistry startup valued at about $2.5B in 2019 that never went public, raised a small $60M insider round in 2025 and in 2026 still enforces its foundational dual-cure patent. Its CEO's view that materials, not printers, decide the opportunity is the same thesis the Digital Alloy Lab works on, but applied to proprietary resins instead of open alloy data.

**Risk events.** 2014-2019: large VC rounds (four rounds 2014-2017; $81M in Sept 2016; a $260M round in 2019) reaching a valuation of about $2.5B; CEO Ellen Kullman said an IPO was 'not a short term' matter (Dec 2019); 2019-2026: CEO changes from Joseph DeSimone to Ellen Kullman (Nov 2019) to co-founder Philip DeSimone (CEO by 2026); Nov 2025: a $60M round from existing investors (Sequoia, Silver Lake, adidas, Baillie Gifford, Madrone, Northgate) with no valuation disclosed, while 'nearing cash-flow-positive operations'; Jan 2026: CEO Phil DeSimone called 2024-2025 an 'embarrassment' for AM (SPAC listings of Desktop Metal and Markforged, Nano Dimension/Stratasys fights) and expected venture capital to 'completely come to a halt'; Sept 2026: EPO Board of Appeal upheld its dual-cure patent against an unnamed competitor

**Robustness.** Private, never listed, so it avoided the SPAC-era crash, and it keeps insider support (Sequoia, Silver Lake) and production customers (adidas, Riddell, dental labs). But its 2025 round was small relative to the 2019 raise and came with no valuation, and users depend on proprietary resins and cloud software from a single vendor.

**Physical-science relevance.** Polymer-only (photopolymer and dual-cure resins); no metals, so not relevant to LPBF alloy development directly. Relevance to a materials lab is as an example of chemistry-led AM (CLIP came out of polymer chemistry and was published in Science in 2015) and as a source of lattices, fixtures and fluidic parts for automated rigs. Its strategy statement, 'The materials, not the printer, dictate your opportunity', mirrors the alloy-first view of the Digital Alloy Lab, but Carbon keeps those materials proprietary.

### Center for Open Science (COS) / OSF

founded 2013 · US · origin: academic · openness 4→4 · nonprofit · funding $69.0M · revenue $10.2M (2024) · confidence: high · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 13 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Being free, open-source and nonprofit, OSF grew in 14 years to more than 1 million users, almost 300,000 registrations, about 400,000 public projects and 29 million shared files, and COS's replication projects reshaped norms in psychology. But openness at zero price created costs without revenue. Private storage and spam absorbed resources, earned revenue stayed at 10-20%, and after the NSF bid failed COS cut OSF back. Open licensing protected the code (Apache-2.0) but not the hosted service.

**Takeaway.** COS shows the funding ceiling of philanthropy-built open infrastructure. About $69M of contributions and grants (2013-2024, Form 990s), with the Arnold Foundation the largest listed sponsor every year to 2019, built the best-known open-science platform. Yet in 2026 OSF stopped accepting new projects because COS could not raise $4-5M a year to run it. Its response is instructive: keep the unique, low-cost functions (preregistration, preprints, linking), hand storage to CERN-backed Zenodo and others, and fund the continuity of public records. A lab relying on any single free open-science service should keep its own copies, with DOIs on durable repositories.

**Risk events.** Funder concentration: the Laura and John Arnold Foundation (Arnold Ventures from 2019) was the largest listed sponsor in every year 2013-2019, at $1.87M-$6.21M a year (peak $6.21M in 2017); An NSF mid-scale infrastructure proposal, submitted several times, was not funded. COS says it would have covered the OSF resource gap for five years.; 11 Aug 2026: COS announced it would narrow OSF. Keeping the full platform would need $4-5M a year, about half of COS's operating budget, and earned revenue (mostly several dozen institutional members) covers only 10-20%.; 16 Nov 2026: no new OSF projects or components. 19 Feb 2027: all public and private projects become read-only. Private projects will be removed at a later date, with 12 months' notice.; Costs of free openness: 63% of projects (>650,000) were never made public, almost 200,000 users used OSF only for private storage, and spam prevention took more resources than expected; $450,000 committed to a continuity fund for public OSF content, with an expanded Internet Archive partnership planned

**Robustness.** COS is financially stable as an organisation: 2024 revenue was $10.2M, expenses $8.8M and net assets $13.4M, up from $4.6M in 2023. It has SOC 2 accreditation (2023) and a Platinum transparency rating. The platform itself proved fragile: about 95% of 2024 revenue was contributions and grants, and when large infrastructure grants did not come, COS cut OSF back to registrations, preprints and linking. The Apache-2.0 code can be self-hosted or forked, and public content is protected by a continuity fund and Internet Archive copies, but the hosted service's scope depends on grant income.

**Physical-science relevance.** OSF is used most in psychology and the social sciences (its origin) but is multidisciplinary. Among its community preprint servers is engrXiv for engineering (2016). For physical-science labs its main lesson is about infrastructure risk: a free, grant-funded service holding 29 million shared files is ending new file storage, and COS points users to Zenodo (CERN). In our assessment, OSF Registries (preregistration), which continues, suits self-driving-lab campaigns that want to fix their objectives and analysis plans before running.

### Desktop Metal, Inc.

founded 2015 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→0 · acquired · funding $438.0M · revenue $148.8M (2024) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 11 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Openness played no role: Desktop Metal was closed from founding and competed on patents (>1,000 by 2023) and proprietary software. Its arc was driven by capital markets: a $2.5B SPAC valuation, an acquisition spree, a failed Stratasys merger, a contested sale to Nano Dimension and a Chapter 11 filing within about five years of listing. Proprietary control did not protect value, and customers had no open fallback when the vendor failed.

**Takeaway.** Desktop Metal is the clearest bust of the 2020-21 AM SPAC wave: an MIT-founded, $438M-venture-funded closed metal-AM company that listed at up to $2.5B in December 2020, lost most of its value, was bought by Nano Dimension for $5.295 a share in April 2025 and filed for Chapter 11 in July 2025. For a lab buying closed metal-AM tools, it shows that a vendor's survival risk becomes the lab's: sintering recipes, software and service disappear with the company unless the lab owns open process data.

**Timeline.** 2020: merger $2.50B, Trine Acquisition Corp. (SPAC, NYSE: TRNE); 2025: acquisition, Nano Dimension Ltd.; 2025: bankruptcy

**Risk events.** Dec 2020: listed through the Trine SPAC at an implied equity value up to $2.5B before its high-volume Production System shipped in volume; 2021: acquisition spree, including EnvisionTEC (Feb 2021), Adaptive3D, Aerosint, Aidro, several dental labs, and the ExOne brand; May-Sep 2023: all-stock combination with Stratasys (combined value ~$1.8B, Desktop Metal holders ~41%) voted down by Stratasys shareholders and terminated; 2024: revenue fell to $148.8M from $189.7M (2023); GAAP net loss $219.5M; year-end cash and short-term investments only $20.4M (preliminary, 8-K 2025-03-26); Jul 2024-Apr 2025: Nano Dimension acquisition contested in court (a 2025-03-24 decision went against Nano, per Markforged's 10-K) before closing on 2025-04-02; Jul 2025: Chapter 11 filing three months after the takeover, with a plan to sell assets (Bloomberg: 'facing liquidation'); an IndustryWeek caption (Jan 2026) refers to 'Desktop Metal's sale to Arc Public Benefit Corp.' (details not confirmed)

**Robustness.** Low. Every layer (printers, firmware, Live Suite software, sintering recipes) was proprietary and single-vendor, so the installed base depends on whoever acquired the assets in the 2025 bankruptcy, which could not be confirmed. Nothing was released openly that the user community could maintain.

**Physical-science relevance.** Sinter-based metal AM (binder jetting, bound metal deposition) rather than laser powder-bed fusion: parts are printed green and sintered, so microstructures and alloy design rules differ from LPBF. The 10-K lists 'more than 20 standard metals, including aluminum, titanium, stainless steels, tool steels' for binder jetting, and the P-1 was positioned for 'process and materials development', which made it one of the few commercial metal systems sold for alloy R&D. No open API or SDL integration was documented. For a LPBF aluminium-alloy lab it is a closed comparator and a warning about vendor continuity.

### Digital Science (Holtzbrinck Publishing Group), incl. Figshare

founded 2011 · GB · origin: founder_startup · openness 1→1 · acquired · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 15 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Open licences on content and free individual deposits drove Figshare's growth to over 8 million openly available research outputs and 150+ partner organisations by 2024, and earned it a place in NIH's GREI. Keeping the platform closed let Digital Science sell institutional repositories as SaaS, so Figshare is 'open science on closed infrastructure', unlike Zenodo (open-source InvenioRDM, CERN-operated).

**Takeaway.** Digital Science shows the commercial route to open-science infrastructure: a publisher-owned group (Holtzbrinck) that backed a PhD student's data-sharing tool in 2012 and turned it into institutional SaaS, while keeping the content openly licensed and the code closed. Researchers get free, DOI-backed open data deposit; institutions and funders get a vendor, not a commons, so portability rests on DOIs and APIs rather than forkable code.

**Timeline.** 2012: acquisition, Digital Science (Holtzbrinck Publishing Group)

**Risk events.** Open-science infrastructure owned by a publishing group: Digital Science's parent Holtzbrinck also owns 53% of Springer Nature; Jul 2026: CEO Daniel Hook (11 years) moved to a new Chief Scientific Officer role at Holtzbrinck; President Steve Leicht became CEO; Closed code means the platform cannot be forked or self-hosted if the owner changes course; mitigated by DOIs, an open API and CLOCKSS archiving of content

**Robustness.** Commercially sustained (institutional subscriptions and Figshare+ charges) inside a large, long-lived family-owned media group, with public funding on top: a 2022 NIH Generalist Repository Ecosystem Initiative (GREI) award alongside Dryad, Mendeley Data, Dataverse, Zenodo, OSF and Vivli. Content is portable (DataCite DOIs, OAI-PMH, documented API) and CLOCKSS is an additional host, but the software is single-vendor and closed.

**Physical-science relevance.** Figshare is one of the generalist repositories that funders such as NIH recognise and a common home for publishers' supplementary data (PLOS integration since 2013). For a materials lab its free tier (20 GB files, CC0 datasets with DOIs) and Figshare+ (multi-TB) suit large microscopy, CT or process-monitoring datasets; for the Digital Alloy Lab that could include LPBF build logs and melt-pool imaging, though Zenodo offers similar terms on open-source, publicly run infrastructure. Digital Science's Overleaf (AGPL core) and Dimensions are also widely used for writing and literature analytics.

### EOS GmbH Electro Optical Systems

founded 1989 · DE · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→0 · private_bootstrapped · revenue $394.1M (2022) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 37 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** EOS shows that a closed, patent-licensed and family-owned LPBF incumbent can be durable: it never relied on open source or outside capital and has stayed independent since 1997 while US SPAC-funded rivals collapsed. Its closedness shapes the research ecosystem, as users must buy parameter sets and software plans, and it has added APIs and data access only as interfaces.

**Takeaway.** EOS is the reference closed LPBF incumbent: a Munich family company that commercialised DMLS in 1996, settled its patent war with 3D Systems by cross-licensing, and sells machines, materials and 'validated' parameters from a single source on subscription. Its durability (about EUR 360M revenue, no outside investors) contrasts with the SPAC-funded US metal-AM entrants. For an LPBF aluminium-alloy lab, EOS sets the closed, validated-parameter benchmark (AlSi10Mg, AlF357, Al5X1, Al2139 AM, Constellium CP1), and its parameter and software model is the opposite of open science.

**Risk events.** 1993-2004: patent litigation with 3D Systems over laser sintering (and EOS suit against DTM from 2000), settled in 2004 by cross-licensing; EOS pays royalties to 3D Systems on certain US laser-sintering system sales; 1997: sold its stereolithography business to 3D Systems for about $3.25M and refocused on laser sintering; 2020: group revenue fell from EUR 361.6M (2019) to EUR 275.6M, recovering to EUR 318.6M (2021) and EUR 360.3M (2022); 2023-2026: competition from lower-cost and Chinese LPBF makers and the bust of US metal-AM SPACs (Desktop Metal, Velo3D) did not visibly affect EOS, which reported large production orders in 2026 (VulcanForms 200 M4 ONYX systems; Beehive Industries >$50M)

**Robustness.** High for a private hardware maker: 37 years old, family-controlled (no VC or public-market pressure), ~EUR 360M revenue and ~1,420 staff (2022), a large production site (Gernlinden, capacity up to 1,000 systems a year) and presence in 35 countries. The flip side for users is single-vendor dependence: validated parameters, monitoring and software plans are proprietary and subscription-based.

**Physical-science relevance.** Central to LPBF: EOS commercialised DMLS (EOSINT M250, 1996), and the EOS M 290 is the listed machine for every one of its aluminium materials. Its aluminium portfolio includes AlSi10Mg (also from 100% recycled feedstock), AlF357 (AMS 4289), Al5X1, Al2139 AM and, since July 2026, Constellium's CP1, i.e. high-strength Al alloys of the kind the Digital Alloy Lab develops. But parameter sets are sold as 'validated EOS Material Sets' within proprietary software, so new-alloy work on EOS machines relies on vendor licences and interfaces; in-situ optical-tomography monitoring and APIs exist but are not open-licensed, which limits closed-loop self-driving-lab use compared with open-parameter research machines (e.g. Aconity3D).

### Hamilton Company (Reno, NV) and Hamilton AG (formerly Hamilton Bonaduz AG, Switzerland)

founded 1950 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→0 · private_bootstrapped · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 76 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Hamilton grew for decades with closed products and family ownership, and its claim to be the largest global provider of automated liquid handling shows openness was not needed for market leadership. The open-source STAR driver built outside the company is a case of users opening a closed instrument themselves; there is no evidence Hamilton opposed or supported it.

**Takeaway.** Hamilton is the closed, family-owned incumbent of liquid handling: founded in a garage in 1950, never listed or VC-funded, and still privately held with ~3,000 staff. Its STAR robots are proprietary, yet the open PyLabRobot driver lets labs program them in MIT-licensed Python and port protocols to other brands, showing how open software can wrap a closed instrument without the vendor's involvement.

**Risk events.** 1974: founder-to-son succession (Clark to Steve Hamilton); family ownership retained through 2026; 2013: second European production site opened in Timișoara, Romania (syringes and lab instruments); 2020-2021 COVID surge: ventilator deliveries more than tripled in 2020, Process Analytics output rose up to fivefold, 300 jobs added in Graubünden and a ventilator plant built in Reno in four months (post-pandemic effects not reported on fetched pages)

**Robustness.** A 75-year-old family-owned group with ~3,000 staff at 22 sites and about 150 patents a year, diversified across lab automation, syringes, storage, sensors and ventilators; no outside investors or debt pressure were found. Its liquid handlers are closed, but community open-source drivers (PyLabRobot's STAR backend) mean protocols written in open Python can run on STARs and be moved to other robots, which reduces customer lock-in without Hamilton's help.

**Physical-science relevance.** Hamilton syringes are standard consumables in chromatography and analytical chemistry, and STAR liquid handlers are workhorses of high-throughput biology. For self-driving labs, the STAR is the main example of a closed commercial robot made scriptable by an open community driver: PyLabRobot advertises that switching a protocol from a Hamilton STAR to an Opentrons or Tecan is a one-line backend change. Direct relevance to LPBF aluminium-alloy development is low (powder and heat-treatment workflows rather than liquids), apart from wet-chemistry steps such as preparing etchants or dilutions for analysis.

**What openness did.** Openness plays no visible role: Kardex is a century-old line of proprietary storage systems that grew through acquisitions and, since 2021, by reselling a partner's closed AutoStore technology. Its record 2025 bookings show demand for automation (reshoring, labour shortages) can be met entirely by closed products.

**Takeaway.** Kardex represents the closed, mature logistics-automation incumbents that labs end up buying: a 1898 filing-system brand reborn as a Swiss listed company (EUR 850M revenue, ~2,960 staff in 2025) selling proprietary vertical lift modules and AutoStore systems. It is profitable and stable but entirely closed, so labs that automate storage depend on its interfaces and service.

**Timeline.** 1989: IPO, SIX Swiss Exchange

**Risk events.** 2025: net profit fell 48.3% to EUR 41.8M after a one-time, non-cash write-off of about EUR 39M of loans to Rocket Solution, a start-up it first invested in in 2020 and took majority control of in 2025; 2025: free cash flow fell 77.4% to EUR 20.5M; US demand lagged expectations and the company flags uncertainty around tariffs and global trade; Market cap fell from CHF 2.31B (end-2021) to CHF 1.17B (end-2022) and was CHF 1.72B on 2026-11-23; Growing reliance on a partner's technology: AutoStore-based Kardex AS Solutions was the fastest-growing unit in 2025 (revenue +24.7%, bookings +59.1%)

**Robustness.** A profitable listed company (2025 EBIT EUR 101.2M, 11.9% margin; record bookings EUR 981.6M and backlog EUR 599.2M; net cash EUR 136.5M) with 26.5% of revenue from life-cycle services on an installed base. Its systems are closed, so service and software support depend on Kardex.

**Physical-science relevance.** Vertical lift modules are compact, automated storage for parts and consumables; Kardex lists healthcare/hospitals and pharmaceuticals among its industries, but its pages do not mention laboratories or sample storage. For a self-driving or high-throughput materials lab, a VLM is a plausible buffer for consumables, build plates, sample coupons or sealed powder containers between process steps (an inference, not a documented lab deployment). Its controls are closed, so integration into an open SDL orchestrator would go through Kardex's proprietary interfaces.

### Markforged Holding Corporation (MarkForged, Inc.)

**What openness did.** Markforged was closed by design and relied on patents both offensively and defensively; the patent strategy cost it a $17.3M verdict and an ~$25M settlement in 2024. Openness had no visible role in its rise (SPAC at ~$2.1B) or fall (resold for $42.5M in 2026); the decline tracked weak revenue growth, losses and post-SPAC re-pricing rather than licensing choices.

**Takeaway.** Markforged is a SPAC-era closed-hardware story: about $137M of venture money and a ~$2.1B SPAC valuation in 2021, then a 1-for-10 reverse split, a patent loss, a $116M take-private by Nano Dimension in 2025 and a $42.5M resale to Stratasys in 2026. Its proprietary, cloud-tied printers changed hands twice in thirteen months, which is the vendor-continuity risk a lab takes on when it buys closed AM hardware.

**Timeline.** 2021: merger $2.10B, one (SPAC sponsored by A-star, led by Kevin Hartz; NYSE: AONE); 2025: acquisition $116.0M, Nano Dimension Ltd.; 2026: acquisition $42.5M, Stratasys Ltd.

**Robustness.** Low as an independent entity: two changes of owner in about a year, with the composites business heading into Stratasys and the binder-jetting line staying with Nano Dimension. Customers rely on closed, cloud-connected software and proprietary consumables, so continuity depends on the new owner's support decisions. An installed base in '10,000 facilities across 70 countries' (2021) gives the acquirer a reason to keep supporting it.

**Physical-science relevance.** Not a laser powder-bed fusion vendor. Its metal line (Metal X: 17-4 PH, copper, H13, Inconel 625, D2; FX10 Metal Kit; PX100 binder jetting) is extrusion- or binder-based plus sintering, and it offers no aluminium alloy; its continuous-carbon-fibre parts are marketed to 'replace machined aluminum parts'. Relevant to a lab mainly for strong fixtures, grippers and enclosures for automated rigs, not for alloy development. Closed cloud software limits scripted, self-driving-lab use.

### OpenCalphad

founded 2013 · DE · origin: academic · openness 3→3 · confidence: low · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 13 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** OpenCalphad shows expertise from the closed incumbent's own lineage flowing back into an open project: its first version was co-written by a developer whose name matches one of Thermo-Calc's co-originators. Openness gave it a steady following (253 stars, 86 forks) and 13 years of activity. Without institutional backing it remains a small, single-maintainer effort, and there is no evidence that it has taken users from the commercial tools.

**Takeaway.** OpenCalphad is the 'scientists' open CALPHAD': a GPL-style Fortran code started at a German materials institute in 2013, co-written by Thermo-Calc-generation CALPHAD veterans, and kept alive by an informal international collaboration. It proves an open, independent CALPHAD engine is feasible. Like pycalphad it lacks open assessed databases and AM process models, and it relies on one maintainer's repository, so it complements rather than replaces commercial tools for an LPBF alloy lab.

**Risk events.** The code lives in a personal repository (github.com/sundmanbo) with one lead developer, so the bus factor is high. The website reads 'Last updated Mars 2025', but the repository was still being pushed on 2026-11-20.; Licence ambiguity: GitHub detects no licence, and GPL-3 is inferred from a PDF in the repository.; No current funding is named. The project began under ICAMS's Sapiens project, and no later grants appear on fetched pages.

**Robustness.** Open source with 86 forks, so the code survives any one person. It depends on a small group of senior CALPHAD scientists for development and on volunteer goodwill (a community Gitter room and a third-party Windows GUI, 'OpenCalphad CAE'). There is no institution, foundation or revenue behind it.

**Physical-science relevance.** OpenCalphad is a free CALPHAD code for multicomponent equilibria, property and phase diagrams and thermodynamic database development. It offers OpenMP parallelism and an application interface (OCASI) for coupling to other simulation software, and it states a particular interest in descriptions of the pure elements from 0 K. For LPBF aluminium alloy work it is an alternative equilibrium engine to Thermo-Calc when open databases are used, but it has no AM-specific module. Our assessment is that its Fortran command-line interface fits Python-based self-driving-lab pipelines less directly than pycalphad does.

### OurResearch (legal name Impactstory; now operating as OpenAlex)

founded 2011 · US · origin: nonprofit · openness 4→4 · nonprofit · funding $9.5M · revenue $4.3M (2025) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 15 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** CC0 data and open code let OpenAlex replace MAG at launch and win institutional adopters that a proprietary index at its price could not: Sorbonne dropped Scopus for it (Dec 2023), the Leiden Ranking adopted it (2024) and the French ministry backs it. Openness did not pay for itself, though. The nonprofit ran deficits, had negative net assets in FY2024 and relied on grants for 56% of FY2025 revenue. It now charges for API convenience while keeping the data CC0, an 'open data, paid service' model.

**Takeaway.** OurResearch shows a small nonprofit building core open-science infrastructure on a few million dollars a year (FY2025 revenue $4.3M, 56% of it grants) by selling services around CC0 data. Its finances were fragile, with negative net assets in FY2024, until large grants such as Arcadia's $7.5M arrived, and it now prices heavy API use. For a lab, OpenAlex is the open literature database that this very project uses, and its CC0 snapshot protects users from any later pricing change.

**Risk events.** FY2020-FY2021: no grant income. Revenue of $0.74M-$1.15M came entirely from program services (Unsub/Unpaywall), and net assets fell to $182k (FY2021).; Dec 2021: Microsoft Academic Graph retired. OurResearch launched OpenAlex as its open replacement in January 2022.; FY2024: net assets negative (-$126,729) despite $3.55M revenue. They recovered to $1.17M in FY2025.; Mar 2024: $7.5M Arcadia Fund grant to establish OpenAlex as an open alternative to commercial products. The French Ministry of Higher Education and Research pledged support in 2024.; Top-paid officer per Form 990 summaries changed from co-founder Jason Priem (CEO, FY2022) to a CTO (FY2023-24) and a COO, Kyle Demes (FY2025). The founders' current roles were not confirmed.; 2025-26: usage-based API pricing for OpenAlex ('$1 a day free', prepaid usage, annual plans)

**Robustness.** CC0 data and a downloadable whole-database snapshot mean OpenAlex's content survives the organisation, since anyone can re-host it (e.g. SemOpenAlex's 26 billion RDF triples). The organisation itself is small and was fragile: net assets were negative in FY2024 and grants were 56% of FY2025 revenue. Funding is now more diversified (Arcadia, Schmidt Sciences, Wellcome-funded work, the French ministry, California Digital Library, plus paid tiers) and it is hiring (Nov 2026).

**Physical-science relevance.** OpenAlex is the open replacement for Microsoft Academic Graph and an alternative to Scopus and Web of Science for bibliometrics. This study's own scripts (scripts/openalex_trends.py) use the OpenAlex API to measure open-access shares of US Physical Sciences, Materials Science and Chemistry output by institution type. For materials and self-driving-lab research it supplies CC0 metadata for literature mining and for assembling paper corpora. Unpaywall's open-access locations let a lab find legal full texts. In 2026 OpenAlex added software as a work type (Schmidt Sciences grant), which helps credit lab code.

### Papers with Code (Meta AI)

founded 2018 · GB · openness 3→3 · defunct · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 7 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Open data and free access made Papers with Code a standard reference for ML state of the art (its GitHub organisation still has about 6,000 followers) and attracted Facebook AI, whose researchers used the platform and its data for work such as AxCell and Galactica's citation benchmark. Openness did not secure the service itself: it ended at the owner's discretion in 2025. The CC BY-SA licence is why its contents outlived it.

**Takeaway.** Papers with Code is the clearest case of open science infrastructure held by a big tech owner: a 2018 project whose CC BY-SA leaderboards became the standard record of ML progress, whose team moved inside Facebook AI and trained Meta's Galactica science LLM on the literature, and which was left to decay and redirected to Hugging Face in mid-2025. The open data licence let the community archive and revive it; the corporate hosting is what failed.

**Timeline.** None: acquisition, Facebook AI Research (Meta); 2025: shutdown, Meta (owner); Hugging Face (redirect target)

**Risk events.** Single corporate owner: a free community resource run inside Meta AI, with no foundation, contract or funding of its own; 2022: the team's Galactica model (Meta AI, trained on 48 million papers among 106 billion tokens) was available for only three days from 15 November 2022 before being withdrawn over inaccurate and racist output; June 2025: spam replaced dataset titles and descriptions and leaderboard entries were deleted, then the site returned 502 errors; Aug 2025: paperswithcode.com redirected to Hugging Face's Trending Papers, ending the leaderboards, with no announcement found on fetched pages; Community response: alternatives and revival projects built on the CC BY-SA dumps (GitHub issues #117 and #122, 2025; pwc-archive on Hugging Face powering paperswithcode.co)

**Robustness.** The service proved fragile: when its corporate owner stopped maintaining it, it degraded and disappeared within about two months in 2025, and Hugging Face's Trending Papers replaces only the paper-and-code feed, not the leaderboards. The open licence made the data robust: the final CC BY-SA snapshot (576k papers with abstracts plus links, evaluation tables, methods and datasets) is mirrored on Hugging Face and reused by revival sites.

**Physical-science relevance.** Papers with Code was the main public ledger of machine-learning results: AxCell (2020) describes it as a result-aggregation tool whose leaderboards were filled by crowdsourced contributions and semi-automatic extraction. ML-for-science work, including materials informatics and self-driving-lab models, depends on such shared baselines, so its loss removes a neutral place to compare methods (an inference; no materials-specific leaderboards were checked). Its team's Galactica model was trained on 48 million papers plus millions of compounds and proteins, an early attempt at an LLM interface to physical-science knowledge that Meta withdrew after three days. For the Digital Alloy Lab it is a cautionary example: reproducibility infrastructure hosted by one company can vanish, while openly licensed data survives.

### pycalphad (PyCalphad Development Team)

founded 2014 · US · origin: academic · openness 3→3 · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 12 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** MIT licensing and a citable software paper made pycalphad the open reference implementation of CALPHAD in Python: 433 stars, 145 forks and about 50,000 PyPI downloads a month (Nov 2026), which is large for a niche metallurgy code. Openness let it be embedded freely in scripts and ML pipelines. Development is still concentrated in two people, and it has not displaced Thermo-Calc, whose value lies in assessed databases and integrated modules.

**Takeaway.** pycalphad is the open counterpart to Thermo-Calc. Born of a NASA fellowship and MIT-licensed, it does the core CALPHAD equilibrium calculations in Python and reads the incumbent's TDB format. It shows how an open tool can cheaply win scripting and automation use, while the closed vendor keeps the high-value data. Its fragility is people, not licence: two maintainers wrote about three-quarters of the commits, and no sustained funding is visible.

**Risk events.** Bus factor: two maintainers, richardotis (1,314 commits) and bocklund (215), wrote about three-quarters of the repository's 2,070 commits. The next human contributor has 8 (GitHub contributors API, Nov 2026).; The only funding acknowledged on fetched pages is a NASA graduate-research fellowship (NNX14AL43H). No current institutional or sustained funding was found.; Still pre-1.0 after about 12 years: the latest stable release on PyPI is 0.12.0 (requires Python >=3.11), with active development continuing (last push 2026-11-22).

**Robustness.** MIT-licensed code, 145 forks and a citable software paper mean the library cannot be withdrawn. Its practical continuity, though, depends on two maintainers and on volunteer or academic time. It has no foundation, company or recurring funding visible. Its value also depends on openly published thermodynamic assessments (TDB files), because commercial vendor databases are not part of it.

**Physical-science relevance.** pycalphad is an open CALPHAD engine for phase-equilibrium and phase-diagram calculations in multicomponent alloys, including Al systems (the documentation shows Al-Fe and Al-Ni examples). As an MIT-licensed Python library it can run inside automated alloy-design and self-driving-lab loops at any scale without per-seat licences, and it reads Thermo-Calc TDB files, so openly published assessments can be reused. For LPBF aluminium alloy development it can compute equilibrium phase fractions and phase diagrams from open TDBs. It offers no equivalent of Thermo-Calc's process-level Additive Manufacturing Module, and it does not include commercial databases.

### Stratasys Ltd.

founded 1989 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→0 · public · $468.6M (market_cap, 2026-11-23) · revenue $572.0M (2024) · confidence: high · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 37 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Stratasys has stayed closed and patent-driven (FDM, ~2,700 patents, the 2024 Bambu suit) and consolidated through acquisitions (Objet, MakerBot, Origin, Xaar 3D, MarkForged). That kept it the largest polymer AM incumbent, but did not protect its value: market cap fell from $6.57B (2013) to $0.47B (2026). Its partial opening, an 'Open' material tier sold through a paid OpenAM licence, is a response to customer demand for material freedom rather than a change of model.

**Takeaway.** Stratasys is the surviving closed polymer-AM incumbent: a 1989 FDM-patent company that grew through mergers, bought MakerBot just after MakerBot closed its designs, fought off or failed in three 2023 consolidation attempts, and in 2026 buys distressed peers (MarkForged for $42.5M) with a debt-free balance sheet. It shows that closed incumbents can outlast SPAC-era entrants, but at a fraction of their 2013 value, and that 'open' in its vocabulary means licensed access to third-party materials.

**Timeline.** 1994: IPO, Nasdaq (SSYS); 2012: merger $3.00B, Objet Ltd.

**Risk events.** 2013: bought MakerBot for $403M in stock at the peak of the consumer 3D-printing boom (year-end 2013 market cap $6.57B); MakerBot goodwill later written off and MakerBot merged into UltiMaker (2022, 46.5% stake); 2014-2016: market cap fell from $6.57B (end-2013) to $861M (end-2016); 2023: contested consolidation: all-stock Desktop Metal merger (combined ~$1.8B) voted down and terminated in September; hostile tender offer by Nano Dimension (owner of ~14.5%) expired in July without purchases; ~$1.2B offer from 3D Systems in June; Nano proposed $16.50/share in December; Aug 2024: restructuring with a ~15% workforce reduction; Aug 2024 patent suit against Bambu Lab; Apr 2025: $120M PIPE from Fortissimo Capital (11,650,485 shares at $10.30); Q3 2025: $33.9M impairment of the UltiMaker equity investment

**Robustness.** The most durable of the AM incumbents: profitable on a non-GAAP basis, $210.7M cash and no debt at 2026-09-30, ~2,700 patents, a broad technology portfolio and a strategic investor (Fortissimo). But revenue has drifted down and it remains a consolidation target. Users are locked into proprietary printers, materials and software; the OpenAM tier loosens materials, not designs.

**Physical-science relevance.** Almost entirely polymer: its own description is 'a leader in polymer 3D printing solutions', and Wikipedia records no metal printing business. Relevance to an LPBF aluminium-alloy lab is indirect: FDM/PolyJet/SAF parts for fixtures, fluidics and labware in automated or self-driving-lab rigs, and the OpenAM 'Open' tier as an example of a closed vendor selling parameter and material freedom as a paid licence. Its FDM patents historically shaped the open RepRap ecosystem, which is not documented in the fetched pages.

### Tecan Group AG

founded 1980 · CH · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→0 · public · $3.61B (market_cap, 2026-11-23) · revenue $1.05B (2025) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 46 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Tecan shows that a fully closed instrument incumbent can last decades and grow by acquisition: no openness was needed for its 1990s-2021 expansion. Its recent stress came from the end of pandemic demand (sales -23% from 2022 to 2025, market cap -77% from end-2021 to end-2025), not from open competitors. Openness reaches its machines only from outside, through community drivers such as PyLabRobot.

**Takeaway.** Tecan is the archetypal closed liquid-handling incumbent: founded by four engineers in 1980, listed since 1999 and built on proprietary Genesis, Freedom EVO and Fluent platforms plus OEM manufacturing. Its 2021 COVID peak and largest-ever acquisition were followed by four years of falling sales and a 77% fall in market value to end-2025. For an open lab, Tecan robots are usable mainly through community open-source drivers that Tecan does not sponsor.

**Timeline.** 1999: IPO, SWX Swiss Exchange (SIX: TECN)

**Risk events.** 1989: financial difficulties led to a temporary suspension of share trading; 1990 restructuring sold many subsidiaries to focus on laboratory automation; 2021: COVID-era peak; sales grew 29.5% to CHF 946.6M and Tecan made its largest acquisition ever (Paramit, US contract manufacturer, completed August 2021; price not found on fetched pages); 2022-2025: sales fell from CHF 1.14B (2022) to CHF 882.5M (2025), with declines of 6.1%, 13.0% and 5.5% in 2023-2025; trailing 12 months to June 2026 CHF 870.5M (-4.0%); Market cap fell from CHF 7.04B (end-2021) to CHF 1.63B (end-2025), about -77%, before recovering to CHF 3.03B on 2026-11-23

**Robustness.** A 46-year-old listed incumbent with ~3,500 staff in 52 countries and a diversified mix of own-brand automation, OEM components (Cavro) and contract manufacturing (Paramit). Its closed platforms tie customers to Tecan software and consumables; community drivers (PyLabRobot) give users a partial exit for the EVO but are unsupported by Tecan.

**Physical-science relevance.** Tecan liquid handlers are built for life-science and diagnostics labs, but the same pipetting robots are used to prepare precursor solutions in chemistry and materials automation. For a self-driving lab, a Tecan EVO can be scripted from open Python via PyLabRobot rather than only through EVOware. Little direct relevance to LPBF alloy development, which is powder- and furnace-based rather than liquid-based.

### Thermo-Calc Software AB

founded 1997 · SE · origin: academic · openness 0→0 · private_bootstrapped · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 29 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Thermo-Calc is a closed academic spin-off that has lasted. The code moved from a KTH research group into a foundation-owned company and stayed closed, funded by licences and maintenance, and it became the reference CALPHAD tool (50,000+ citations, 2,000+ customer organisations). Staying closed did not stop open alternatives: pycalphad and OpenCalphad do the core equilibrium calculations and read the TDB format. Our assessment is that the vendor's moat is its assessed databases and integrated kinetics and AM modules, which the open projects found here do not offer.

**Takeaway.** Thermo-Calc is the closed incumbent that defines the CALPHAD workflow an LPBF alloy lab uses. A KTH research code became a foundation-owned company in 1997, and it sells licences, databases and now services, with a scriptable but proprietary Python API. Its longevity (development since 1977) and steady releases show that a closed academic spin-off can be durable without venture capital. For a lab that wants to work openly, the practical split is to use Thermo-Calc for the commercial databases and the AM module, and to publish assessments as TDB files and workflows in pycalphad, so others can reproduce the work without a licence.

**Risk events.** 1993: IP consolidated in the STT foundation after the seven developers donated their individual rights, the founding governance fix for multi-author academic code; 1997: commercialised as a company because commercial activity did not fit KTH's academic mission; 2025: launched Thermo-Calc Solutions, an engineering-services division, diversifying beyond licences; 2025 customer survey: 21% of respondents already used an LLM or AI assistant for Thermo-Calc work, and the company says it is 'exploring how to integrate ML and AI tools into our CALPHAD framework'; Open alternatives read its file format: pycalphad (MIT) and OpenCalphad (GPL inferred). OpenCalphad's first version was co-written by a Bo Sundman, the name of one of Thermo-Calc's three original developers

**Robustness.** Nearly 50 years of continuous development. A foundation (STT) is the majority owner rather than VC or PE. 2,000+ customer organisations in 60+ countries, 50,000+ journal citations, and twice-yearly releases (2026a, 2026b). Users cannot fork or audit the code or the commercial databases, so continuity depends on one firm. The open TDB format and the open CALPHAD codes are partial hedges.

**Physical-science relevance.** Thermo-Calc is the default CALPHAD tool for alloy design in metallurgy, the Digital Alloy Lab's field. Its Additive Manufacturing Module (2023) models powder-bed fusion with a laser or electron-beam heat source (Gaussian, core-ring or top-hat): melt-pool behaviour, solidification and evaporation-driven composition change across tracks and layers. It produces printability maps for keyholing, lack of fusion and balling, with alloy properties from an extended Scheil calculator, and it couples to DICTRA, TC-PRISMA and a CET model for microstructure. An Al database is among those the AM module supports. TC-Python exposes most AM-module functions, so it can be scripted inside high-throughput or self-driving alloy-design loops for LPBF aluminium alloys. Every node in such a loop needs a paid licence, though, and results rest on closed databases.

### Universal Robots A/S and Mobile Industrial Robots ApS (Teradyne Robotics)

founded 2005 · DK · origin: academic · openness 0→1 · acquired · revenue $308.3M (2025) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 21 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** UR grew to 100,000 installed cobots as a closed-hardware company whose openness is confined to interfaces (URScript, the BSD-3 ROS 2 driver) and a third-party add-on marketplace (UR+). Open drivers lowered integration costs for researchers and integrators without exposing the arm's design, which helped adoption, but they did not protect revenue: the Robotics segment shrank 18% from 2023 to 2025 and lost $99M in 2025, for reasons the filings attribute to demand and currency rather than openness.

**Takeaway.** Universal Robots shows the 'open interface, closed machine' model at scale: a university spin-out that sold a closed cobot, published open-source drivers so labs and integrators could program it from ROS, sold itself to Teradyne for $285M in 2015 and reached 100,000 units. A decade later the combined UR and MiR segment is shrinking and loss-making ($308M revenue, $99M pre-tax loss and ~400 job cuts in 2025), a reminder that a lab's open control code is portable while the closed arm it drives depends on one vendor's fortunes.

**Timeline.** 2015: acquisition $285.0M, Teradyne, Inc.

**Risk events.** Teradyne Robotics revenue fell from $375.2M (2023) to $364.8M (2024) and $308.3M (2025), with losses before taxes of $54.3M, $77.6M and $99.4M (FY2025 10-K segment note); 2025: Robotics restructuring cut about 400 employees, with $24.3M of Robotics severance ($15.3M paid in 2025, the rest due by Q3 2026); $4.9M asset impairment the same year; 2023-2024: smaller Teradyne-wide headcount reductions of 215 and 98 people, primarily Semiconductor Test and Robotics, including voluntary early retirement; Robotics backlog fell from $42.3M (end-2023) to $36.6M (end-2024); most Robotics revenue is in foreign currencies, so a strong dollar weighs on reported growth (FY2024 10-K); Robotics is a loss-making ~10% of a parent whose growth is semiconductor test ($2.52B of $3.19B revenue in 2025), so its future depends on Teradyne's commitment

**Robustness.** Large installed base (100,000 UR cobots by 2025, up from 50,000 in 2020) and a training and integrator network (UR Academy, 120+ centres; UR+ add-on ecosystem) make UR arms a de facto standard cobot. Ownership by Teradyne (market cap $36.5B on 2026-11-23) gives financial backing, but the segment has lost money for three years and is being cut. Controllers and firmware are closed, so users depend on UR for the arm itself; the BSD-3 ROS 2 driver means lab integrations and orchestration code do not depend on UR's own software.

**Physical-science relevance.** Cobot arms and AMRs are the generic 'hands and legs' of a self-driving lab: moving samples, vials, crucibles or build plates between closed instruments that were not designed to talk to each other. UR's BSD-3 ROS 2 driver is the open integration point that lets a lab script a UR arm from open orchestration code (ROS 2, Python) rather than PolyScope alone, and the 2024 AI Accelerator (NVIDIA Jetson) targets AI-driven perception. For the Digital Alloy Lab (LPBF aluminium alloys) a cobot is a plausible way to tend sample preparation, powder-handling or characterization stations around a closed LPBF machine, but no specific SDL or LPBF deployment was confirmed from fetched pages.

### Velo3D, Inc.

founded 2014 · US · origin: founder_startup · openness 0→0 · public · $108.7M (market_cap, 2026-11-23) · revenue $46.0M (2025) · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 12 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Velo3D stayed fully proprietary and sold support-free L-PBF as a differentiated, patent-protected process; that won SpaceX and aerospace customers but not a sustainable business at SPAC-era expectations. The collapse (a market cap of $1.43B to about $0.1B, delisting, debt-for-equity control by Arrayed) came from losses, customer concentration and cash needs; openness played no visible role either way.

**Takeaway.** Velo3D is the third 2021 metal-AM SPAC and the one that survived by being recapitalised rather than sold: after its value fell more than 90%, a creditor took 95% of the equity in 2024, and it relisted on Nasdaq in 2025 as a defense-focused producer with a going-concern warning. For an LPBF alloy lab it is the archetype of the closed, recipe-locked production printer, good at qualified parts and poor as an open research instrument.

**Timeline.** 2021: merger, JAWS Spitfire Acquisition Corp. (SPAC); 2024: acquisition, Arrayed Notes Acquisition Corp. (Arrayed Additive)

**Risk events.** Customer concentration: SpaceX was 41.1% of 2020 and 27.8% of 2021 revenue; top three customers 62.0% (2021), 47.0% (2024), 33.2% (2025); Market cap fell from $1.43B (2021-12-31) to $333.7M (2022), $102.1M (2023) and $108.7M (2026-11-23) (StockAnalysis); Dec 2023: $18M registered direct offering; 1-for-35 reverse split (2024-06-13) and 1-for-15 reverse split (2025-07-28); Sept 2024: NYSE delisting notice (8-K Item 3.01, 2024-09-12); the stock (ticker VLD) moved to OTCQX, later OTC Pink (VLDX); Dec 2024: debt-for-equity exchange gave Arrayed Notes Acquisition Corp. 95% of the stock; Arrayed CEO Arun Jeldi became CEO; Aug 2025: relisted on the Nasdaq Capital Market as VELO (2025-08-19) with a $20.1M offering at $3.00 per share

**Robustness.** Fragile: a going-concern warning, recurring losses, two reverse splits and a controlling shareholder (Arrayed held 95% after the exchange; Jeldi beneficially owned ~48.6% by March 2026). Customers depend on proprietary Flow/Assure software and vendor-qualified recipes, so there is no open fallback. Partial offsets: growing defense-led revenue (Q3 2026 $20.5M, about +50% y/y from $13.6M), $75.7M cash and a $40.1M backlog at 2026-09-30, and a five-year Sandia CRADA (Nov 2026).

**Physical-science relevance.** Directly relevant as a closed L-PBF platform: Sapphire printers make support-free metal parts in aluminium, titanium, nickel superalloys and copper alloys from 16 vendor-developed 'recipes' (2025). Parameters live in proprietary Flow software, so alloy-development work (new Al compositions, scan-strategy exploration, data for self-driving labs) is constrained compared with open-parameter research machines such as Aconity3D's. The 2026 Sandia CRADA and Arrayed's 'lightweight magnesium and aluminum alloy' manufacturing show the company moving toward defense production rather than research tooling.

## I. AI-for-science models and datasets (licence histories; added after the videos) (19)

### AlphaFold 2 and the AlphaFold Protein Structure Database

founded 2021 · GB · origin: corporate · openness 1→4 · public · confidence: high · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 5 yrs · 0 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** AF2 became the default tool in structural biology within months: 51k citations and 3.32m accesses for the paper, and a CC BY database of 200M+ structures. The only restriction at launch, non-commercial parameters, was lifted after about six months. Its openness also enabled the trainable reproduction (OpenFold) and the AF3-class clones that followed.

**Takeaway.** The reference case for open AI-for-science: a corporate lab released permissive code, weights (after a six-month non-commercial phase) and a public-institution-hosted database built from public PDB data. It produced field-wide adoption and a Nobel Prize. The same lab then restricted its successor (AlphaFold 3).

**Timeline.** 2022: CC BY-NC 4.0 parameters → CC BY 4.0 parameters (opening)

**Risk events.** Successor AlphaFold 3 was restricted at launch (see alphafold3.json); AFDB still uses AlphaFold 2 models as of 2026 (Wikipedia), so the open database lags the restricted model

**Robustness.** Code (Apache-2.0) and weights (CC BY 4.0) can be mirrored freely; the AFDB is served by EMBL-EBI under CC BY 4.0; an independent Apache-2.0 reproduction (OpenFold) exists.

**Physical-science relevance.** Structural biology and chemistry: half of the 2024 Nobel Prize in Chemistry went to Hassabis and Jumper 'for protein structure prediction' (Wikipedia).

### AlphaFold 3

founded 2024 · GB · origin: corporate · openness 0→1 · public · confidence: high · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 2 yrs · 1 closing / 2 opening moves

**What openness did.** Restricting AF3 did not stop it being cited (20k citations). It did hand the open ecosystem to others: within about six months at least four AF3-class models appeared under MIT or Apache terms, two of them from VC-backed startups. Google partly reversed course, with code in Nov 2024 and Apache-2.0 code in Jun 2026, but kept the weights gated.

**Takeaway.** AlphaFold 3 is the clearest closing event in AI for science. A corporate owner with a commercial route (Isomorphic Labs) withheld code and weights, and the community answered with permissive clones. Over two years Google re-opened the code in stages but never the weights. Labs relying on AF3 should prefer an MIT/Apache clone wherever commercial use or redistribution matters.

**Timeline.** 2024: AlphaFold 2 model: Apache-2.0 code, CC BY 4.0 weights → AlphaFold 3: server only, no code (closing); fork: Open AF3-class models: Boltz-1/2 (MIT), Chai-1 (Apache-2.0 from 2024-11-27), OpenFold3 (Apache-2.0 per GitHub; MIT per Wikipedia), Protenix (Apache 2.0, per Wikipedia); 2024: Server only → Code CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 plus non-commercial weights on request (opening); 2026: Code CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 → Code Apache-2.0 (weights still restricted) (opening)

**Risk events.** Server-only launch drew a wave of open clones (see relicensing_events[0]); Weights cannot be redistributed, so continued access depends on Google

**Robustness.** Single-vendor control of the weights, but functionally comparable open models exist under MIT/Apache licences (Boltz, Chai-1, OpenFold3).

**Physical-science relevance.** Protein-ligand and nucleic-acid complex prediction for chemistry and drug design.

### Boltz-1 and Boltz-2 (Boltz PBC)

founded 2024 · US · origin: academic · openness 4→2 · private_vc · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 2 yrs · 1 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** An MIT licence for an AF3-class model gave Boltz fast adoption (5M+ downloads and 1,000+ companies by its own count) and positioned it as the default open alternative to AF3. That user base preceded a $110M Series A and a turn to proprietary successor models, the open-core pattern familiar from software.

**Takeaway.** Boltz shows the lifecycle of an open clone: an academic MIT release fills the gap left by a restricted corporate model, wins users, becomes a company and then closes its newest models. Labs should pin MIT versions and keep the training data, which remain usable whatever the company does.

**Timeline.** 2024: AlphaFold 3 server only → Boltz-1 MIT (code, weights, training data pipeline) (opening); fork: Boltz-1; None: All models MIT → Open core: newest models (BoltzProt-1, BoltzMol-1) proprietary (closing)

**Risk events.** Shift to proprietary models after a VC round; Boltz-2 training code still 'coming soon'

**Robustness.** MIT-licensed Boltz-1/2 weights and Boltz-1 training data can be forked regardless of the company's future choices.

**Physical-science relevance.** Protein-ligand structure and affinity prediction for medicinal chemistry; used by 'top 20 pharma' per the company.

### Chai-1 (Chai Discovery)

founded 2024 · US · origin: corporate · openness 1→2 · private_vc · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 2 yrs · 0 closing / 1 opening moves

**What openness did.** Chai-1 started with a non-commercial licence and switched to Apache-2.0 within three months, as MIT-licensed rivals appeared. The company then kept its next-generation antibody model for partnerships, and per its homepage it raised a $400M Series C.

**Takeaway.** A startup used an open (eventually Apache-2.0) model as a calling card while keeping its frontier model closed, a typical AI-startup open-core pattern.

**Timeline.** 2024: Chai Discovery Community License (non-commercial) → Apache-2.0 (opening)

**Risk events.** Successor model (Chai-2) offered through partnerships rather than released

**Robustness.** Apache-2.0 Chai-1 code and weights can be forked; future models are at the company's discretion.

**Physical-science relevance.** Protein, ligand and nucleic-acid complex prediction for drug discovery.

founded 2023 · US · origin: academic · openness 4→4 · government · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

**Takeaway.** A DOE-lab academic model, fully open in code, weights and data. Publishing the training set multiplied its impact beyond the model itself.

**Robustness.** Permissive code, public weights and public training data; DOE-lab copyright.

**Physical-science relevance.** Battery and inorganic materials simulation with magnetic-moment (charge) information.

### ESM protein language models (Meta FAIR, then EvolutionaryScale, now Biohub)

founded 2019 · US · origin: corporate · openness 3→2 · nonprofit · confidence: medium · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 7 yrs · 1 closing / 2 opening moves

**What openness did.** Meta's MIT-licensed ESM-2 is among the most-used science models (3.0M HF downloads a month for one size). The restricted ESM3-open drew about 2.3k a month. When the team left Meta for a VC-backed company, the licence closed (non-commercial, API for the frontier model); after it joined a non-profit, the open models returned to MIT.

**Takeaway.** Openness tracked the owner's incentives: open at Meta, restricted at a VC-backed startup, open again under a philanthropic non-profit. Download counts show how much more the permissive models were used.

**Timeline.** 2024: MIT (Meta ESM-2) → Non-commercial ESM3 Community License; frontier 98B model API-only (closing); fork: EvolutionaryScale (team spin-out); 2024: Non-commercial only → ESM C 300M under the permissive Cambrian Open License (opening); 2026: Cambrian Open / Non-Commercial licences → MIT (opening); 2026: merger, Chan Zuckerberg Biohub

**Risk events.** 2024 closing at spin-out; frontier ESM3 98B still not released as weights; Ownership moved three times (Meta, EvolutionaryScale, Biohub)

**Robustness.** MIT weights for ESM-2/ESMFold and now ESM3-open/ESM C; Atlas CC BY 4.0; stewardship by a philanthropic non-profit since 2026.

**Physical-science relevance.** Protein engineering and design; ESM3's esmGFP is a new fluorescent protein 58% similar to the closest natural one (estimated as 'over 500 million years' of evolution).

### Evo 2 (Arc Institute)

founded 2025 · US · origin: nonprofit · openness 4→4 · nonprofit · confidence: high · tool: WebFetch (search budget exhausted)

*Computed:* age 1 yrs · 0 closing / 0 opening moves

**What openness did.** Releasing the training data along with the weights made Evo 2 reproducible, and the dataset is downloaded more than the flagship model (12k vs 8k a month). Corporate partners (NVIDIA) monetise hosting rather than the model.

**Takeaway.** The fullest release in the group: a non-profit published a 40B frontier-scale model with its training data under Apache-2.0. Non-profit, philanthropically funded labs are the most reliable source of fully open AI for science.

**Robustness.** Everything needed to retrain is public under Apache-2.0; NVIDIA hosting adds a commercial access path but no lock-in.

**Physical-science relevance.** Biology and biochemistry (genome design and variant-effect prediction); a full-stack open model at frontier scale (40B).
